The Secretar of State,
Vasnington.
Sir:
894.032/90
Confimmin my telegran No. 8 of Jánuary 22, 1930,
I have the honor to report that the Lower House of the
Diet was dissolved on Junuary 21, 1930, and that a new
election will take place on February 20th.
Dissolution early in the session had been antioipated
as probable for some time due to the numerical weakness
in the Lower House of the Government party, the Minseito,

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
and to tactical advantages wiich it was believed would
accrue to the Government in a general election at this
time. The last general election, held February 20,
1928, had resulted in a difrerence of but one between
the strength of the Lainseito and of the Seiyukai.
By
the time the recent session opened, however, the
Seiyukai had obtained by accretion, sometimes through
reputedly questionable means, a total of 273 seats, an
absolute majority, while the Minseito seats had dwindled
to 173.
Dissolution at this time would seem to be most
favorable to the Government. It will now enjoy the un-
doubted advantages of the party in power when a general
eleotion is held. The Governors and the police, for
example, are immediately under the control of the
LMinister of Home Affairs, who is in the present oase an
exceptionably able electioneer. The Minseito goes be-
fore the country with its sincere efforts to improve
the economic situation, which oulminated in the lifting
of the gold embargo on January 1l, 1930, still fresh in
the mind of the electorate and with its popularity as
yet undiminished by the execution of policies which may
prove to be less widely welcome than its economic reforms.
The Seiyukai, on the other hand, which has made every
effort to avert dissolution at this time, faoes the
country with the electorate still keenly sensible to
recently exposed Seiyukai soandals.
After the Premier and the Ministers for Foreign
Affairs and Finance had conoluded their addresses to the
Lower

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
91
894.032
Lower House (Despatch No. 1 of January 25, 1930). Mr.
Inukai, the President of the Seiyukai, interpellated
the Government. Had the Government entertained any
illusions regarding Seiyukai inferences that it would be
docile so that a dissolution might be avoided, the pure-
ly political aspect of Mr. Inukai's two prinoipal inter-
pellations may quite possibly have put an end to them.
Mr. Inukai attempted to wrest from the Government a
definite statement that it would not accept less than
a 10-10-7 ratio at the London Naval Conference, appar-
ently anticipating that such a statement might become
a welcome political weapon in case a compromise on this
issue should eventuate at the Conference. His next in-
terpellation concerned the recent resignation of Mr.
Kobashi, Minister of Education, for alleged acceptance
894.00/292
of a bribe (Despatch No. 1355 of December 4, 1929). In
the face of the far more extensive scandals within the
Seiyukai which are now common property, Mr. Inukai's
purpose in introducing this relatively minor venality
seems to have been principally to recall to the eleotor-
ate that scandals are not after all confined entirely
to his own party. After the Premier had evaded direot
reply to the first interpellation and had disposed of
the second by pointing out that at the time of the al-
leged acceptance of a bribe Mr. Kobashi had been neither
a member of the Minseito nor of the present Government,
the deoree of dissolution was read. Subsequently, the
Government issued an exposition of its reasons for dis-
solution, pointing out that the absolute majority of
the Seiyakei

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
一4
the Seiyukai in the Lower House would not only hinder
the Government in the execution of policy but did not
necessarily represent the will of the people as it was
gained after the last general election had been held.
Following the dissolution preparations by all
parties for the approaching general eleotion were at
once commenced. It would be, of course, unwise to at-
tempt any forecast of the outcome of the eleotion.
When the present advantages of the Minseito, as outlined
above, are considered, however, it is not unreasonable
to anticipate that its position in the Lower House may
be considerably improved. The several proletarian
parties are once more a point of interest through the
possibility of equality of strength in the two major
parties resulting in the balance of power being held
by them. Inability to arrive at agreement is, however,
already apparant among the proletarian parties. The
press reports that in their more promising districts
opposing proletarian candidates are being put forward
whioh will, as. it did in the previous eleotion, result
in a diffusion of votes and failure to gain a number
of seats proportionate to the votes cast.
The press is divided in its opinion with respeot
to dissolution at this time. It is almost unanimous in
agreeing that the Government should rather have called
an extraordinary session of the Diet shortly after
assuming offiee last July and then, dissolving it,
faced a general eleotion. Among the more reputable
papora

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
papers, however, the Tokyo NICHI NICHI and the Tokyo
KOKUMIN apparently feel that, failing this, the present
time was satisfactory for dissolution. The Tokyo JIJI
and the Tokyo CHUGAI SHOGYO, on the other hand, are in
disagreement with this view, expressing the belief that,
as the Government waited for the regular session of the
Diet, it should have delayed dissolution longer. The
CHUGAI SHOGYO thinks that an effort to pass the budget
and otier important measures should first have been
made while the JIJI express the belief that the Seiyukai
would have been quiescent during the session to avoid
disturbance of economic conditions in the very important
period that follows the lifting of the gold embargo.
Adverse criticism in the press is, however, free of
animus.

FILED
The Seoretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report developments in, Japanese
proletarian political parties, interest in whose activities
has recently inoreased due to the approach of a general
eleotion. The Embassy has reported from time to time
during the past the inaugurations, dissensions, and re-
alignments of these frequently changing groups and it is
now felt that a general survey may be of some value.
The ohief weakness of the proletarian politioal
movement 1s its disunity. On Maroh 5, 1926, the Labor-
Farmer

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Farmer Party was inaugurated. With the exoeption os
the Farmer-Labor Party which was inaugurated on Deceu-
ber 1, l925, and dissolved by the authorities the same
day because of radical tendencies, this was the first
A. proletarian political party in this country. Today there
are five proletarian parties, as well as a number of local
proletarian groups of a political oharacter too small to
be dignified by the term "party".
Dissension entered the proletarian parties with the
labor organizations which in the main today, as at the
beginning, comprise them. Before the period of proletarian
parties, efforts toward federation of labor unions had
been essayed for some years and had only very partially
succeeded. It was acarcely to be expected then that at-
tempts of these same disunited groups to join together
for political purposes could succeed immediately. The
differences that existed between the labor groups per-
sisted in the proletarian parties. Labor groups have
united to form a party, only to have one or more of the
groups soon withdraw to form a new party, to join a
party already in existence, or to await the appearance
of some other group or groups with whose viewa its own
sufficiently coinoide to warrant an attempt at political
union. Labor unions, as muoh, have as yet no legal
standing in Japan and in the eyes of the law are merely
voluntary associations.
The programe of the various parties do not differ
sufficiently to explain the division. The chief aim of
each party is admittmily to ameliorate the condition of
the proletariat. Their programs differ prinoipally in
emphamis

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.98)
一3 -
emphasis with respect to which sections of the proletarimt
should be aided and which measures of amelioration are
most urgent.
The dissension rather springs from former advocacy
of social theories which once engrossed the labor leaders
but which have now scarcely more than a sentimental or
academio value. Labor unions in Japan may be said to be
a development of the present century, and in fact it was
not until the war-boom and the post-war-boom that they
reached a position of importance here. Throughout their
development their leaders exhibited more interest in de-
ciding upon a theory by whioh their aims should be at-
tained than in the aims themselves. The social theories
of the nineteenth century of Europe and America reached
Japan only late in that century and were quite alien to
the thought of the people who were slowly emerging from
feudalism. Each theory found adherents among labor
leaders. The dissensions among the unions usually arose
from the refusal of one labor group to accept the theory
advooated by another group.
Today these theories have lost much of their im-
portance in the eyes of the labor groups. Over a period
of years, the leading argument creating discord was
whether labor's aims should be attained by parliamentary
means or by direct action. When proletarian political
parties advocated direct action the authorities dis-
solved them. Those that exist today have acknowledged
that they will proceed along parliamentary lines. Yet
this abandonment of their chief contention has not
brought union. The enmities created among labor leadere

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4-
by these disputes over theories still persist so that
dissension is now due more to personal differences and
jealousies, the inheritance of past quarrels, than to
genuine divergence of principle.
Further, in all Japanese political parties personali-
ties are more important than principles. Each proletarian
party, composed of ita various labor groups, is formed
about a leader or leaders. These groups within a party
support the man to whom they are most closely attached
personally and move as he moves, supporting him in his
quarrels and in his wanderings in and out of parties,
with little regard for principles.
Recent as the development of proletarian political
parties is, could they achieve unity their significance
would undoubtedly be considerably inoreased. For example,
in the general election of February, 1928, the first
eleotion of Diet members following the extension of suf-
frage from slightly more than 3,000,000 persons to more
than 12,000,000, the proletarians po1led almost 500,000
votes. They elected, however, but eight men, the suscess-
ful candidates averaging about 60,000 votes each. Success-
ful candidates of the regular parties, on the other hand,
averaged 12,000 votes each. In other words, had the
proletarian parties refrained from running several op-
posing candidates in one electoral distriot they might
have gained thirty seats in the Diet.
Although no one is apparently more acutely aware of
the disadvantages of such diffusion of votes than the
proletarian party lemders themselves, in the approaching
eleotion the same competition between rival preletarian
candidates will ooour. Already ninety-four candidates have

=== Page 37 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
been registered by the proletarian groups, almost one-
third the mumber of candidates put forward by the Sei-
yukai, the leading opposition party. Labor leaders ad-
mit that this large number of candidates will probably
bring them a return of very few more Diet members than
they gained two years ago. This situation is not confined
to the labor group. Party discipline has not reached the
stage in Japan where the party leaders or members choose
one candidate for each electoral district. It is not un-
usual to find several avowed members of a party seeking
election in the same distriot.
Disunity is not the only obstacle confronting the
proletarian parties; they suffer also from the apathy of
43
the masses toward all amelioration movements. This
apathy is indicated by the failure of laborers in general
894.
to respond to the appeals of organized labor, which is
the chief souroe of strength to the proletarian parties.
According to the Japan Labor Year Book (Nihon Rodo Nenkan),
at the end of 1928 laborers in Japan were divided as
follows:
Factory workers

Less than 500,000 laborers, or about five per cent
of the total, are, however, organized. There are eight
unions of farm laborers, with a total memberskip of
170,000, and 501 trade unions with a tetal mombership
of

=== Page 38 === (confidence 0.97)
-6 
of 309,000, of whioh 12,000 are women. Membermhip 1n
these unions showed a slight decrease at the end of
1928 as compared with the preceding year.
A
This vast number of laoorers who have failed to
respond to the inducements of labor organizations do not
have much interest in the proletarian politioal parties.
The largest proletarian party, the Social Democratio Party,
is said to have a membership of something more than
200,000, including some persons not strictly within the
laboring olasses, since this party attempts to represent
the salaried "white collar" class as well as laborers.
The next largest proletarian party, the Japan Popular
Party, is said to have a membership of something less
than 200,000 members. Other parties are all smaller.
The number of votes cast for proletarian candidates in
the last general election exceeded by about 10,000 the
number of organized laborers. Even though some organized
laborers probably did not utilize their votes, it is
reasonable to suppose that these candidates attracted
only a very limited number of people from outside the
ranks of organized labor.
There seem to be several causes of lack of interest
among the masses in labor organizations. Many are satis-
fied with present conditions while others repose a large
amount of trust in the authorities from whom, they feel,
improvement will come gradually and naturally. In mpite
of the rapid industrialization of Japan, the household
system of inauatry still maintains to a murprisingly
great extent. Under this system a relation between mester
and worker that is feudal in character exists to mnch a

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
degree that the worker is uaually contented with his
position and feels no need of entering an organization
whose activities might be directed against his master.
A
There is also apparent in the Japanese of the working
olasses, in addition to their conservatism, a slowness
of comprehension which leads the observer to believe that
the significance and possible benefits of organization
are not generally understood. An employee of the Social
Bureau of the Department of Home Affairs, deeply interested
in the labor movement, went so far as to state in this
regard that laok of interest, especially among farm
laborers, is thiefly the result of stupidity.
These causes of limited labor organization are at the
same time among the causes of lack of interest in proletari-
an parties. There are, however, additional reasons for
aloofness in this respect. The fact that a number of
these parties have been tainted with communism has quite
probably alienated the sympathy of many unthinking people
from all of them. Then too, the proletarian parties lack
competent leaders and funds. Among the leaders there are
some men of high standing, such as Dr. Isoh Abe, for many
years a professor at one of the leading universities, and
F.S.
Kice
Piity
Mr. Ko'Matsuoka, the strongest man in the Social Demo-
however
cratic Party. Both have already shown/that through them
unity cannot be attained. According to some disinterested
F.S.
students of the movement, among the leaders the most
promiming are some young intelligent men of the Japan
Popular Party, but it will be some years before mueh can
be expeeted from them.
In the past the parties well in funds have been the

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.97)
一 8-
most successful at elections, but the proletarian parties
are not among them. It had been hoped that by the
quadrupling of the electorate by the so-called "Universal
Manhood Suffrage Law" of 1925 eleotion venalities would
be greatly deoreased. This apparently has not been the
case. At least at the last general election vast sums
were alleged to have been spent by the leading regular
parties, the Seiyukai having spent, it is said, as much
as ten million yen. Regular parties receive large sums
from wealthy commercial houses. Some of the proletarian
parties insist that they would not accept money from
capitalists if it were offered, while the Social Democratio
Party, for example, which is said to receive some monetary
aid from capitalists, accepts it only without conditions.
As dues for membership in a proletarian party range from
fifty sen to one yen, they cannot be considered a source
of revenue of any consequence, nor can members of suoh a
party be expected to have private means from which any
significant sum might be contributed.
 The Social Democratic Party (Shakai Minshuto) is,

March 31, 1930.
6:
This interesting despatch points out
that the principal factors in the recent
election in Japan, which resulted in the
decisive victory of the Minseito, were (1)
the bad repute of the Seiyukai due to of-
ficial corruption, extravagance and various
indiscretions while in power and divided
counsels within the party versus (2) the
skilful campaign management, party unity
and the personal integrity of Mr. Hamaguchi
of the Minseito.
Over eighty per cent of the qualified
voters cast their ballots, which denoted an
increasing interest in practical politics.
The deoreased number of independents and
proletarian representatives elected points
to the gradual evolution of a two-party
system which, however, may be halted by
new realignments due to the fact that the
parties are still based more on personali-
ties than on principles; although it should
be noted that one of the chief issues in
the recent election was the "negative"
economic policy (prosperity through retrench
ment) of the Minseito versus the "positive"
policy of the Seiyukai.

298
Sir:
Supplementing my despatch No. 43 of February 24,
1930, I have the honor to submit a more detailed survey
of the general elections held on February 2oth.
The viotory of the Minseito was more deoisive than
had been antioipated even by Minseito leaders. Before
the eleotion they had forecast a probable 240 seats for

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
their party, conceding 2oo seats to the Seiyukai, the
leading opposition party.
. Actually the Minseito won
273 seats and the Seiyukai only 174, a difference of 99 and
an absolute majority in the Lower House for the Minseito
of 40.
 Successful independent and small party candidates
numbered only 19, of whioh 5 were proletarians.
The principal reason for the Minseito victory was
not so much the virtue of that party as the reputed
venality of the Seiyukai. The two chief counts against
the Seiyukai in the mind of the eleotorate were its ao-
cretion of non-Seiyukai Diet members, by reputedly ques-
tionable means, to gain a decisive majority when it was
in power and the alleged implication of some of its
leading men in unsavory scandals. At the general eleo-
tions two years ago the Seiyukai won only one seat more
than the linseito, but by the time the reoent brief
session of the Diet opened the Seiyukai seats had in-
oreased to 239, while the Minseito had dwindled to 172.
This alteration created widespread conviction that the
methods of persuasion employed were dishonest.
The
soandals in which Seiyukai leaders were said to be im-
plicated have been the subject of several Embassy des-
patohes during reoent months. They impaired the reputa-
tions of such Seiyukai men, to mention only a few, as
General Yamanashi, former Governor General of Chosen;
Mr. Ogawa, former Minister of Railways; and Mr. Amaoka,
 Director of the Board of Decorations.
Nor were these the only actions on the part of the
Seiyukai whioh aroused dissatisfaction. There are the
following popular conviotions as well: in the general
eleotion

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
elections two years ago, when the Seiyukai was in con-
trol, it resorted to excessive official influence and
interference; it indulged in extravagance at a time
when economy was essential to the economio and finanoial
recovery of the country; Mr. Kuhara was induoted into
the Seiyukai Cabinet only because he was a personal and
wealthy friend of the late Baron Tanaka, prime minister
at that time; Baron Tanaka's actions on more than one
occasion touched the dignity of the Imperial Court; the
Seiyukai failed to perform its chief pre-election pledges.
Had the people in general not been inolined to regard
these deeds as especially unworthy of a political party,
the almost universal and persistent adverse criticism
which appeared in the press would undoubtedly have aliena-
ted the sympathy of many former supporters.
The Linseito, on the other hand, enjoyed oertain
positive advantages. Perhaps the most significant was
the leadership of the campaign by Mr. Adachi, the Minister
for Home Affairs.
The party in power always has a con-
siderable advantage in'that it administers the election,
under the direction of the Minister for Home Affairs.
Fortunately for the Minseito Mr. Adachi proved to be a
man of exceptional ability in this regard.
Shortly
after the Minseito Cabinet was formed last July, the
usual ohanges of provinoial governors and local police
chiefs were made, strategio positions being filled with
Minseito men or men inolined toward the Minseito. Suoh

in the placing of officials where they would be acceptable,
were expeoted.
It was during the campaign of the few
weeks

=== Page 52 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
weeks preceding the eleotions that Mr. Adachi showed

under Seiyukai direction two years ago. In Tokyo, for
example, according to a press statement of an official,
two years ago 978 speeohes had been stopped;
this year,
only 155. Mr. Adachi realized apparently more wisely
than his predecessors that official interference is apt
to turn the feeling of the people against the authorities.
Interference is also said to have been so managed
that legitimute complaint was rendered difficult.
Teijiro Yanamoto, former Seiyukai Minister of Agrioulture

. In a recent oonversation the

Again, in the matter of arrests for alleged in-

Subse-

quently, however, arrests of Seiyukai men rapidly in-

This is said to have illustrated Mr.
Adachi's skill in two ways. The Minseito men, before the
election, were brought to realize that their being

than the arrests of Seiyukai men, the people were deeply
impressed by the apparent sincerity of the Government's
conduct of the campaign.
Mr. Adachi's greatest skill was perhaps shown in
the equality of distribution of votes among Minseito
candidates. In Japan the so-called intermediate electoral
system has been in force since the preceding campaign.
Before that time the system obtaining in the United
States was followed, that is to say, there was but one
seat from each district. Under the intermediate eleo-
toral system, eaoh electoral distriot has from three to
five seats, according to population. In the eleotions
of two years ago, as pointed out above, a stale-mate re-
sulted which was only broken by subsequent Seiyukai
activities of doubtful import.
Sinoe that eleotion the
Seiyukai has claimed that the intermediate system prevents
the issue of a decisive majority, saying that the votes
are so concentrated in each distriot on one or two cf the
stronger Seiyukai or Minseito candidates that the bulk of
the candidates of either party are oertain of defeat.
In this

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
In this recent election, however, the distribution of
votes among Minseito candidates was, in many instanees,
amazingly equal. In an electoral district of Akita Pre-
fecture, for example, the three Minseito men who were
elected received almost exactly the same number of votes.
Another instance that has come to my notice is equally
illuminating. In an electoral district in Kyoto Prefeo-
ture Mir. Saito, Parliamentary Vice Minister for Home
Affairs, is a very popular figure and was sure of eleo-
tion. In certain villages he made speeches requesting
the Minseito inhabitants to vote, not for him, but for
Mr. Den, a weaker minseito colleague. The result was
that, though Mr. Saito received some three thousand votes
less than usual, Mr. Den made a larger poll than would
normally have been expeoted and both were elected.
The
average number of votes of each successful Seiyukai
candidate was 22,435, while the average number of votes
of eaoh. successful Minseito candidate was only 20,404,
a difference of 2,O3l; the difference two years ago
was but 52. The faot that Minseito oandidates outnumbered
makes
Seuyukai candidates by 52, the difference  even more
significant. Other parties compared less favorably in
this respect than even the Seiyukai, with the result
that the Minseito with 52 per oent. of the total number
of votes cast gained 58.5 per cent. of the seats in the
Diet.
Perhaps, after Mr. Adaohi, the next most important
factor on the Minseito side was the personality and ohar-

The Minseito had also, apparently, more money at.
its command than the Seiyukai, a reversal of the usual
order, and displayed more intelligent use of its resouroes.
Baron Tanaka, president of the Seiyukai from 1925 until
his death last September, is believed to have brought to
the party exoeptionally large sums of money, either per-
sonally or through friends. Mr. Inukai, his successor,
on the other hand, had no such resources at his oommand.
The funds supplied by Baron Tanaka are said to have been
badly depleted by excessive expenditures in the eleotion
campaign two years ago, by his efrorts to obtain the so-
called "forged majority" in the Lower House, and by gen-
eral extravagance. Outside contributions are also be-
lieved to have deoreased. This would not be surprising
since it is reasonable to suppose that business and finan-
oial interests might hesitate to supply money to the
Seiyukai at the recent eleetion as freely as in former
years.
They may very likely have feared that a change
in Government so shortly after the lifting of the gold
ombarco,

Mr. Adachi is said to have saved his party considerable

The Linseito was also benefited by party unity, while

to succeed the late Baron Tanaka last autumn only beoause

The disas-

In

The result was that four Minseito oandidates were viotor-

The results of the election were in the main satist
factory to those who are interested in the politioal
development of Japan. They seemed to indioate inoreasing
political interest and intelligence among the people.
The recent election was the second general eleotion to
be held under the so-called "universal manhood suffrage
law of 1925", by which the electorate was quadrupled.
In the election of Diet members two years ago the per-
centage of absent voters was approximately 2l.3 per cent.,
a figure whioh was regarded as gratifyingly low. This
year the figure decreased to 19.0 per cent. Acoording
to some reports the peroentage was even less, a point
that can not yet be verified as official figures are still
lacking.
Even when some probable bribery and Mr.Adaohi's
skill in campaigning are taken into consideration, it is
still felt that this indicates growing interest among
those having the right to vote.
"Although the comparative-
ly equal distribution of votes among Minseito oandidates
was in large part due to Mr. Adaohi's skill, it would seem
to have been brought about in part by the sincere desire
of many people to have returned a Minseito majority.
They appear to have voted intelligently for weaker Min-
seito candidates in the belier that stronger candidates
did not need their help. This attitude, it must be ad-

The return of but five proletarian members, when

sympathetically inclined toward the proletarian movement

have been anticipated. Confronted, however, by an ab-
surdly large number of proletarian candidates, making
their success extremely doubtful, many people felt that
their votes would be more effective if cast for Minseito
candidates. The Minseito is, in comparison with the
Seiyukai, liberal, and is definitely interested in sooial
reform. In fact the Shakai Minshuto, the largest of the
proletarian parties, is so conservative that it is re-
garded, in so far as social policies are conoerned, as
but little removed from the Minseito.
The results of the eleotion also gave some cause for
optimism to those who look for the eventual establishment
of the prinoiple of two party politios. The Government
now enjoys an absolute majority; there is one major op-
position party, and the neutral and small party members
have

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)

have so nearly been eliminated that their activities
cannot delay parliamentary progress as in the past.
In
spite of these facts, however, the goal of two-party
politics is still distant.
The nature of the parties
is still unstable. The disruptive forces within the
Seiyukai have already been mentioned. It would be un-
wise to venture that in the future dissatisfied elements
may not arise within the Minseito and that there will be
no realignments similar to those which have ocourred
with such frequency in the past. Until there evolves a
real conflict over principles and until the parties
cease to be built around personalities and for material
gain, only the most sanguine would predict otherwise.
That the result of the present election is an advance
may, however, be admitted.
It may have been noticed that little reference has
been made to the part of party principles in this eleotion
They were, in fact, a minor consideration. Both parties,
of course, declared their principles. But vagueness and
similarity, wnich have been the subject of not infrequent
comment in Embassy despatches, as well as laok of vital-
ity when compared with the factors already outlined, rele-
gated principles to the background. One differenoe did
emerge, however, and was undoubtedly a not unimportant
influence. The slogan of the Minseito was "prosperity
through retrenohment", while that of the Seiyukai was
"prosperity tnrough positive polioy!
The Department
has already been informed of the steps taken by the
Government in preperation for the lifting of the gold
embargo, an important part of which was retrenohment.
The Minseito

emphasis placed on these two ways to economio reoovery
undoubtedly affected the electorate, since almost every
Japanese is touched by the present depression.
Special mention should, perhaps, be made of the
proletarian groups.
They showed up wery badly.
The
ninety-four proletarian candidates, manyof em opposing

502,313 votes, an increase of but approximately 12,000

Chief Principles
- of the -
National Popular Party (Zenkoku Taishu-to).
1 - Represents the interests of laborers, farmers,
the proletariat, and all oppressed peoples.
2 - Reform of the political, economic, social, and
cultural systems monopolized by the bourgeois
class and the emancipation of the proletariat.
3 - Attainment of the party's objects by legitimately
organized means.
4 - Universal suffrage for all of both sexes over 18.
5.- Absolute freedom or speech, publication, and
organization of meetings and sooieties.
6 - Abolition of the Peace Preservation Law, the Police
Peace Law, the Law for the Control of Aots of
Violence, the "Burglar"Law, and all other
oppressive laws.
7 - Abolition of political discrimination in colonies.
8 - Opposition to imperialistio wars.
9 - Elimination of seoret diplomacy.
10 - Freedom of migration of laborers and farmers to
any part of the world.
ll - Drastio reduction of armements.
12 - Reduetion of the oonseription period.
15 - Opening of portfolios of War and Navy to civilians.
14 - Application or high-rate progressive rates to tazes
on interest on capital, inheritance, incomes,
land, and business profits.
15 - Prommlgation of a laber bill providing fer rigirt of
organisation, strike, and celleotive contracts.
16 - Adoption of eight-hour day (six hours for miners).

The Secretary of State,

Sir:
I have the honor to inform the Department that
an element in the Privy Couneil, consisting of the
progressive and newer members, have been endeavoring
to effeet reforms in that body. Their efforts appear
to have been effeotually defeated; but the subjeet
was followed with such general interest that it de-
serves more than passing notice.
A mumber of Privy Counoillors were dissatisfied
with the manner in whioh the examination of the Treaty
of London was being handled by the mall group whieh
has

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
has had a predominating influence; and it will be remembered
that Visoount Ishii took oocasion, at the plenary sessien
whioh voted to recommend the ratification, to censure the
President in no equivocal terms. On Ootober 8th, after one
of the regular business meetings of the Privy Couneil, an
informal meeting took place to consider suggestions advanoed
by Mr. Okada, who has only recently been appointed to the
Privy Counoil and who was once conneoted with the Minseito,
for revising the method of procodure laid down for the
examination of important measures. The Privy Counoil's
Regulations as to Procedure provide that documents re-
ferred to the Privy Counoil shall be given a preliminary
examination by the President, or by his direotion, by the
Chief Seeretary. When this is completed, the President
appoints the members of the special committee to which the
dooument is submitted for final examinataon; and it is not
until the special committee adopts its report, when opies
of the report and of the document under examination are
distributed to the members of the Privy Counoil, that those
members who have not been on the special committee are
supposed to be informed of the text and substance of the
document. The suggestions advanced by Mr. Okada were,
therefore:
1. There should be a plenary session of the Privy
Council for the first reading of any impertant projeet
referred to it by the Emperor.
2. Members should be appointed either by election
or by rotation to special eommittees for the examima-
tien of impertant prejects.
3. Members to be allowed to attend sittings of
speeial

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
speeial committees.
There is legally, and in theory, no distinotion im
the privileges and prerogatives of individual members
of the Privy Counoil, but as a matter of faot Count
Miyoji Ito has held the Privy Counoil in his hand ever
since the death of Prince Yamagata. The power to direet
the funotions of the body are vested, naturally, in the
President, but the present inoumbent, Dr. Kuratomi,
does not have sufficient personal force to exercise this
power, and practically every projeot of politioal im-
portance is handled as Count Ito wishes. This is literally
true. Mr. Wakatsuki, when Prime Minister in 1927, fell
because the measures he had prepared to relieve the financial
system were distasteful to Count Ito, and again in l929,
when the Kellogg Treaty was before the Privy Couneil,
Count Ito suffered the late Baron Tanaka to remain in
office only beoause Baron Tanaka was willing, to the great
prejudiee of his dignity, to propitiate Count Ito .
Members who did not enjoy his favor were not appointed
to important committees. In the recent instance of the
examination of the Treaty of London, even Viseount Ishii,
who was a delegate to the Geneva Arms Conference in 1927
and who is the only person in the Privy Counoil with long
experience in international affairs, was not appointed
to the committee, for no other reason than that he was
not in Count Ito's good graoes.
That this uasatisfactory state of affairs has been
keenly appreeiated within the Privy Couneil has been
evident for some time; but there has been no epportunity
to attempt a reform until the present occasion, when Coumt
18o

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
 Ito, who started out to harass the Government without
any pretense of concealment, was forced to a surrender
by his inability to command the support of the progressive
element. This movement must therefore be regarded pri-
marily as an extension of the revolt against Count Ite
and as an attempt to seoure an equalization of power
among the Privy Counoillor.
As reported by the Embassy in its despatoh No. 1883
894007289
of September 24, l929, there has been for some time a struggle
between the Government and the Privy Couneil: the Privy
Council seeking to enlarge its power and the Government
endeavoring to curtail it. Just how much the progressive
element in the Privy Counoil has been animated by sympathy
with the Government, or again whether the movement in the
Privy Counoil has any connection with the struggle between
the Government and the Privy Counoil, it is diffioult to
say. Nevertheless, the consideration of the reforms pre-
cipitated a general disoussion in the press of the larger
question.
The Tokyo AsaHI expressed the following opinion
editorially in this regard:
"The traditional attitude of the Privy Counoil
towards the Gpvernment has been that of a judge
towards a person on trial*; and consequently
successive Governments have been extraordinarily
afraid of the Privy Council. It is said that the
* The Japanese pemal code assumes a person son-
vieted by a jnte d'instruetion to be guilty.

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
late Baron Tanaka counted its secretary, a mere publie
servant, one of the most awesome of man. Haturally, a
certain degree of blame attaches to those who permit
themselves to be so terrified by the Privy Counoil, but
nevertheless, it is not good that the Privy Couneil should
be allowed to make those responsible for the conduet of
government tremble before it".
This comment suffers somewhat from the florid style
whioh marks editorials, but it expresses accurately enough
the popular sympathy with the dootrine advocated most
consistently by the Minseito, that political power should rise
out of the legislature and should not be assumed by the Privy
Council. The Privy Council was created primarily for the
purpose of bringing the Constitution into being, and after
the Constitution was promulgated in 1889 it was preserved
chiefly to interpret the Constitution and to pass upon the
legality of projeots of law. The right which it has olaimed
of passing upon the polioy lying behind projeots of law, as
exemplified in the case of the fiseal ordinanoes in l987,
rests on no legal basis whatever but was usurped during the
presidemey of the late Prince Yamagata. One can readily
understand that prime ministers in those days were reluetant
to stand against a man who could summarily dismiss them from
office, but as the editorial above quoted points out, the
responsibility for permitting the Privy Couneil to usurp
power rests on those who were too timid to stand before it.
It would not be correet to think that the Japanese
people at large are completely satisfied with the sourse
pursued by Mr. Hamagushi vis-à-vis the Privy Couneil during
. the examination of the Londem Treaty. Befere the Treaty
was referred to the Privy Geuneil, several or the leading
papers

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
papers urged him to state his case frankly and, in the
event of trouble with the Privy Counoil, stand on the
issue that the Minseito's parliamentary doctrine
was being direotly challenged. Mr. Hamagehi rejeoted
this advice. He was at first evasive when questioned,
equivocal in his statements, and hesitant in his attitude;
and the impression is fairly generel that he at last denied
the right of the Privy Counoil to sorutinize the poliey
of the Government in conoluding the Treaty only when he
had been harassed beyond enduranoe. In short, the weight
of opinion seems to be that Mr. Hamaguchi was not so much
interested in protecting the traditional dootrine of his
party as he was anxious to have the Treaty ratified.
Whether or not his attitude was caloulated to
promote the best interests of his country is, of course,
not material to the question under disoussion. The point
whioh is emphasized is that he challenged the Privy Counoil
for reasons of expedieney and not of prinoiple. On the
other hand, there is abundant evidence to show that Count
Ito was working very olosely both with the disaffeeted
navel element and the government's opposition; so that the
dispute over the London Treaty was in no small degree a
dispute over a question of poliey, rather than a struggle
between the Government and the Privy Counoil for power. The
weight of opinion among the leading Japanese students is,
therefore, that the Government's recent vietory is not to be
regarded as a definitive establishment of the Minseite's
parliamentary dostrine, as it was fortuitously brought about
by the dissention in the Privy Couneil over an intermal
issue. It is believed that the Prity Ceuneil eannet be
doprived

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
deprived of its politieal influence so long as the Gevesm-
ment remains dominated by party interests; indeed, it is
considered doubtful whether this would be desirable in
the existirg ciroumstances.
Returning to the suggestions made for revising the
regulations of the Privy Counoil, an informal meeting
of Privy Councillors was held on the 15th instant, when
further developments were blocked by what might be termed
the neutral element. This comprises some of the most
intelligent and able members, such men as Dr. Sakurai,
the President of the Imperial Academy, Dr. Kamata, formerly
Minister of Education, and others of this type, who are
sympathetic with the proposed changes but feel that any
action at the present time, when feeling over the London
Treaty ineident is still running high, would be construed
as a motion of non-confidenee in the President and would
therefore bring about a partisan alighment. The only result
which has been obtained apparently is that the President
has agreed to the suggestion that members be permitted to
attend hearings of committees.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador
Eugene H. Dooman
First Secretary of Eabassy

CEPARTMENT OF STATE

The Honorable
FILED
The Seoretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
Referring to my despatoh No. 58 of November 17, 1930,
I have the honor to inform the Department that the Prime
Minister is making excellent progress towards recovery
and that he has been considered out of danger for some
days. He is still confined at the hospital, and his
physicians do not venture to fix any date by which he will
be able to resume his duties.
The political complications brought about by his
disability, whioh were suggested in my previous despatch,
have

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
have excited much interest and speculation in the pross;
but very little definitive information can be added, for
the reason that intra-party intrigues and negotiations
are carried on secretly and can be only surmised by what
comes to the surface from time to time. These superfioial
indications all point to considerable restlessness within
the Gorernment party and to the lines of division between
the Egi and Adaohi factions becoming more clearly defined.
I venture to offer this observation despite- I am tempted
to say, partly because of- public protests by both Mr.
Adachi and Dr. Egi that everything between them is serene.
Even though the Government commands a majority in
the Lower House by what would normally be a fairly safe
margin, there are several oiroumstances whioh may give it
cause for disquietude. A year and a-half will have alapsed
by the time the Diet meets since the Government came into
office, and this is considered to be an ample period for
the fruits of its fisoal and economio policies to have
ripened. It may well be that Japan's economio and financial
systems are in a more healthy state than they were the
early part of last year, as the yen has been brought up
to its par value, prices have been reduoed to a more
reasonable proportion to the average income, the large
industrial companies have been persuaded to deflate their
assets, the national debt has not been allowed to inorease,
at least, and so on. But these are not ponderable con-
siderations to the average voter in suoh periods of acute
depression

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
depression as that now being experienced in this country.
The rice crop this year was the largest ever harvested
and has so lowered the price of rice that it is proving
an unmixed evil to the farmers, who are the largest element
in the population. The condition of the agricultural com-
munities in the central part of Japan had in any event
already been very badly affected by a decline of more than
one-third in the price of raw silk, the largest item of
export. The state to which the export trade has been brought
by the economic depression in the United States and in other
countries which are Japan's best customers, by the decline
in the price of silver, whioh affects the purchasing power
of Asiatio markets, and by increased tariffs elsewhere, has
been reflected for some time in the condition of the urban
population, among whom a high rate of unemployment prevails.
It is perfeotly true that the Government cannot reasonably
be held to blame for these conditions, which were brought
about by circumstanoes over whioh it can exercise no control;
but there is nevertheless a very definite sentiment that a
pursuit of "positive policies", such as those advocated by
the Opposition, and a more lavish expenditure of money by
the State even at the cost of floating loans, would stimulate
business conditions. Measures of this sort are not justi-
fiable at the present time; and while they would prove
helpful over a brief period of time, they would infinitely
complicate the task which the present Government has under-
taken, of setting the finances of the country on their feet.
These ciroumstances are, of course, knowm to the intelligemt
classes, but they are naturally not appreciated by the in-
oreasing

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
creasing number of those occupied by the problem of making
a living.
The Government is seeking remedial measures, but
thus far only within the confines of its basic policies
of non-flotation of loans and of deoreasing taxes. It is
studying means of raising the price of rice and of raw
silk, but any scheme by which quantities sufficient to
enhanoe prices can be removed from the market would entail
either the expenditure of large sums of money or the
issuance of equivalent amounts of Government notes, and it
would thus necessitate a compromise with the economio and
finanoial principles pronounced by the Government to be
essential to the nation's salvation.
The foregoing brief outline may perhaps convey the
possibilities of attaok in the Diet by hostile elements.
Mr. Hamaguchi is a very sharp, although somewhat cautious
debater; he makes a practice of studying the background
of his Cabinet's policies, whatever might be the department
which initiated them; he can speak at any time with the
intelligence born of knowledge on any important point of
polioy; and he is, finally, the head of his party and can
authoritatively refleot its will. However, the most
optimistio predictions regarding his recovery do not
envisage the probability of his being able to receive
the full brunt of the opposition's attack when the Diet
meets after the New Year Recess, and they do not look
forward to seeing Mr. Hamaguchi do mueh more than to
direet the defense of the Government's position in respect
of

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
of its major policies. Some persons in official quarters
and in the Minseito fear that the Prime Minister will still
be confined by the middle of January; but the ooncensus of
opinion appears to be that he will not be strong enough
fully to resume his duties and that preparations should
be put in train immediately against what promises to be a
trying session.
As things now stand, Baron Shidehara would lead the
0F
Government in the Diet as primeminister pro tempore. I
need not enlarge upon Baron Shidehara's qualities of mind
and character, but he is not a politician. He has held
himself entirely aloof from the affairs of his party and
of its intrigues, he belongs to no faotion, and he has no
following. He has made it quite clear that he wishes to be
temporary
relieved of his/office before the Diet meets, and the Minseito
would like to see this wish carried out, not partioularly to
gratify Baron Shidehara, but because it believes that a
party politician leading the Government in the Diet would
be more nearly oapable of protecting party interests. Un-
less it is true that Baron Shidehara has political ambitions,
as some people affeot to believe, one may be fairly certain
that he will try to unburden himself of his additional port-
folio before the Diet begins its labors in earnest.
If Mr. Hamaguchi has suffioiently recovered by that
time to resume a substantial part of his duties, there will
naturally be no problem, but if he has not, the choice
of a successor to Baron Shidehara will require care and
delicate handling. As I have tried to explain in my earlier
despatoh

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
despatch, the preference of either Mr. Adachi, the
Minister of Home Affairs, or Dr. Egi, the Minister of
Railways, over the other is almost certain to create
trouble. Dr. Egi and his faction are still holding
to Baron Shidehara and would have him retained through
the session, which is perhaps indicative of a trend
in the Minseito towards Mr. Adachi. A good reason for
believing that these two factions have reached a dead-
look is the recent appearance of a movement in favor
Kshign
of General Ugaki, Minister of War, who has been on sick
OBF
leave for several months. Those behind the movement
consist largely of the old Seiyuhonto element- those who
left the Seiyukai when a situation similar to that which
exists in the Minseito arose in that party after the as-
sassination of r. Hara in l92l. It has considerable
influence, being represented in the Cabinet by Mr. Matsuda,
Ry031L
Minister of Overseas Affairs, and Mr. Tanaka, Minister of
RF
Education, and it can advance one excellent reason why
General Ugaki should be chosen, and that is, of course, that
a choice between the two party leaders would lead to
dissention. General Ugaki is not nominally a member of
the Minseito, but he has been associated with Minseito
Governments, he has no little political talent, and enjoys
considerable popularity within the Minseito.
There is some talk also of consoribing ir. Wakatsuki.
It will be remembered that after Count Kato's death, Mr.
Wakatsuki and Mr. Hamaguohi made an agreement by which
Mr. Wakatsuki should first succeed Count Kato and then
retire

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
retire from politios to make room for Mr. Hamaguchi.
Mr. Wakatsuki carried out both the letter and the spirit
of this agreement. He has transferred all his influenoe
in the Minseito to Mr. Hamaguohi, and it is doubtful
whether he would care to reenter polities, or whether the
rank and file of the party would welcome him baok, in view
of the inauspicious circumstances in which the Wakatsuki
Cabinet fell in 1927.
Respeotfully yours,
W. Cameron Forbes.

894.00/305
Sir:
Supplementing my despatoh No. 68, of December 1, 1930,
I have the honor to report that considerable unrest has
developed in political circles over the delay in deciding
the manner in which the Government will organize itself
during the approaching session of the Diet, in the event
that the Prime Minister is unable to resume his duties.
KIN
So far as I can ascertain, Baron Shidehara will not consent
OLF
to head the Government in the Diet, and the leaders of the
Minseito fear the consequences of selecting any one from
the party to succeed, even temporarily, the Prime Minister.
on

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
On December 6th, Baron Yamamoto, one of the three elders
of the Minseito, called on Prince Saionji to ascertain the
views which he held with regard to the replacement of 
Hamaguchi. The Elder Statesman is reported to have im-
pressed upon Baron Yamamoto the necessity of adhering so
far as possible to the popular will as expressed in the last
general elections, and that insofar as Mr. Hamaguohi was
invested with the confidence of the people, the Government
should not attempt to transfer it to some other person. The
party elders then endeavored to persuade Baron Shidehara to
continue as prime minister pro tempore , and while the. NICHI
NICHI reported on the 1sth instant that he had reluctantly
consented, several other papers carried a statement from
Baron Shidehara to the effeot that he believed it would
be unjust to the inseito to have it suffer from any error
committed by the Government while under the direction of
one who is not a meaber of the party. Another paper adds
that baron Shidehara promised the elders to accept the
decision of Mr. Hamaguchi, and that he will continue to
perform the duties of the Prime Minister if Mr. Hamaguchi
so wishes.
The members of the Cabinet who are affiliated with the
Minseito have been cooperating with the elders, and, anti-
cipating the likelihood that Baron Shidehara would be over-
persuaded, they decided on the 9th instant that Mr. Adaohi,
the Minister of Home Affairs, should undertake to see that
satisfactory liaison is maintained between the Cabinet and
the Minseito. It appears that a few hours before this
decision was reaohed, the Minseito had secured a promise
i. from Dr. Egi, the Minister of Railways, who is a strong
riva1

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
rival of Mr. Adachi as next in party seniority to Mr.
Hamaguchi, that the Cabinet officers would make no
decision Without first consulting the party directorate
The arrangement made by the Cabinet officers for Mr. Adaohi
came, therefore, as a complete surprise to the party and
seems to have created intense dissatisfaction in the Adach1
faction, which looks upon it as a soheme concocted by Dr. Egi
to place the responsibility for any difficulty in whioh the
Government may find itself upon Mr. Adaohi, without allowing
him to claim the credit if things should go well. The
faction has already begun to talk of seceding from the party.
It is very difficult to understand why there should be
all this pother when there is every likelihood that Lir.
Hamaguchi will be able to attend the Diet after the New
Year Recess. L.ir. Inouye, the Linister of Finance, told a
member of my staff yesterday evening that the Prime Minister
is making excellent progress, and that he is daily com-
municating with the various members of the Government by
letters written in his own hand, which show that he has
completely recovered his intelleotual faculties. The
present unrest goes far to strengthen the belief that
Japalese politios revolve around personalities and not
principles or dootrines. The Minseito has always been able
to exercise better discipline among its members than the
Seiyukai, but it is apparent that loyalty to some partioular
leader

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
leader is a controlling factor among its members.
Respectfully yours,
W. Cameron Forbes.

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to inform the Department that,
89400/308
confirming my telegram No. 50 of April 15, 1951, Reijiro
Wakatsuki, Prime Minister from 1926 to 1927 and lately
chief delegate from Japan at the London Naval Conference
of 1930, was notified on April 14th of his selection by
the Emperor as Prime Minister to suoceed Yuko Hamaguchi
whose resignation, together with those of his oabinet,
was submitted to the Emperor on the preceding day. The
resignation of Mr. Hamaguchi was universally expected,
following the announcement that he would require an
additional

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
additional operation. This had been predioted since the eariy
part of the year when it was learned that his injuries would
very likely disable hin for an indefinite period. Only the
iron will ana courage of the old "Lion", as he is called by
the public, enabled him to hold the reins of office through
the tryin days of the recent Diet session. In faot, the
strain of attendance at the Diet is said to have contributed
in no small degree to the present condition of his health.
But for the unfortunate mishap to the Premier, the
Hangucni cabinet had every prospect of a continued redidence
in office. By its two great achievements of lifting the gold
enbargo and of forcing through adherence to the London Naval
Treaty, it had gained the confidence and respeot of the
thinking public, and Mr. Hamaguchi himself has the quality
of personal popularity entirely aside from his real worth,
which has made him the best liked political leader of recent
years.
Baron Wakatsuki, who since his return from London
has been in retirement from political affairs, is said
to have accepted with reluctance the return to active
political life and the leadership of his old party. He
has, during a long political life, held the highest position
that & Japanese subject can hold and has been the recipient
of all the honor that a commoner can aspire to, and nothing
but the extreme exigencies of politios could have persuaded
him again to enter the political arena. Mr. Hamaguchi and
Baron Wakatsuki are in no sense political rivals. As members
of the Kenseikai which fathered the present Minseito Party,
they were recognized to be the most promising ministerial
material by Viscount Kato, then president of the party, and
were advised by him to agree on a plan of succession to the
premiership

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
premiership. In consequence, Mr. Wakatsuk1, the older of
the two, assumed the office in l926, on the death of Viseount
Kato, and at the next turn of the political wheel Mr. Hamaguohi
was chosen to head the Party and the Cabinet.
To understand why Baron Wakatsuki was forced out of the
retirement to which he had so gladly returned after his
return from London, it is necessary to review the situation in
the Minseito Party prior to the resignation of the Hamaguchi
Cabinet. The inseito, like all Japanese political parties,
is built around the personality of several outstanding leaders,
with little more than lip devotion to any cause or principle.
These leaders must conciliate the various elements in t heir
followings, between whom very often develop jealousies which,
except for the necessity of preserving a solid front against
the opposition, Would split the party. Since the injury to
Mr. Hamaguchi with the resulting probability of his retirenent,
supporters of the tvo most influential leaders vithin the Party,
Karcahrcro
#2
Mr. Adachi, inister of Home Affairs, and General Ugaki, Minister
of War, have striven to press their leaders forward to the
presidency of the Party. The Adachi faction contended that
he, being the most active and influential party manager, should,
under the principle of straight party government, be entitled
to the presidency. The contention of the Ugaki supporters was
that their leader had greater influence in nigh court circles
and was recognized to be the most distinguished candidate for
the presidency of the party. The feeling in the Party between
the supporters of the rival candidates was so strong that
a pcintment of either would probably have disrupted the Party.
At

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
-4
 At this juncture, the characteristic Japanese faoulty
for compromise again demonstrated itself by the selection of a
third man, a man with a distinguished record who had retired
from politics and who, having no active personal following
nor active enemies, was acceptable to all factions of the
Party. Dr. Egi, Minister of Communications and himself one of
the leaders of the Minseito, is credited with engineering the
new Jabinet. lso, Dr. Sengoku, present head of the South
anchuria Railway ho occupies a unique position in the councils
of the Minseito by virtue of his close connections With the
Litsubishi interests, is said to have had much influence in
aking the decision. he latter has no kind feeling for
Adachi, it is said, and ruled him out of the candidacy for
the preside cy of the Party.
The eliminated candidates retired with good grace. r.
Adachi, with an eye to further developments, has agreed to
hold his portfolio as Home Minister, while Goneral Ugaki,
although retiring from the Ministry on the ground of ill
health, the usual Japanese excuse, endorsed Wakatsuki's
appointment by saying that he was t he most expedient man
for the place under the ciroumstances. It is commonly believed
t at General Ugaki refused to continue in office not so much
for reasons of health as because of disinclination to continue
in politics unless he himself were at the helm of the government.
The new Cabinet cane into being with very little flourish.
Much attention was given in the press to discussion of the
probable policies of the new Government, but there was little
excitement and no demonstration. The change was widely re-
garded not as one of government, but of personnel. Even in
1ts

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
its makeup the Cabinet has very little change to show, parti
cularly in the more important positions. The replacement
of General Ugaki by General Minami will have little effeot on
Cabinet policies, inasmuch as in peace times the Minister of
War has little concern vith matters outside of military cognizance.
The other two posts newly filled, the Ministry of Overseas Affairs
now ocaupied by Mr. Hara, a veteran Minseito politician, and the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry filled by Mr. Sakurauchi, a
self-aade business man and auviser of the Minseito Party, are
comparatively new Cabinet posts and carry relatively little in-
fluence.
At the beginning of his administration Baron Wakatsuki, as
he is now entitled to be called, since his elevation to the peerage
a few days before his uppointment as Fremier, announced that he
intends to make no radical changes in policy from that of his
predecessor. at the close of the first Cabinet Council meeting
under his administration he issued & statement to the press which
in part reads as follows:
" I have always been a Minseito member, and have ever
supported the political principles and policy of the Party .
The various measures framed by Mr. Hamaguchi as Premier, have,
of course, my endorsement. So, there will be no change in
the guiding policy hitherto pursued....However, the policy
must not be too rigid. It must have a certain elasticity
as otherwise it will not meet the actual requirements of
the day. Therefore, while adhering to the guiding polioy
already fixed, I intend to adopt new measures suoh as are
required by the exigencies of the times. As for concrete
matters, we shall announce what we shall do at a proper op-
portunity in future. "
This statement certainly cannot be called either radical
or strikingly illuminating, but is probably indicative of the
actual policy of the new government. Baron Wakatsuki is known
for his facile ability to adapt himself to circumstances.
81noe

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Since the statement was issued, the new Cabinet has
announced that it will take over the outstanding polioie,
with retrenchment as the watchword, of the Hamaguchi Govern-
ment. Premier Wakatsuki has stated that he will adhere to the
much-debated no-loan policy of the preceding government, and in
general terms has stated that the Cabinet was considering ad-
ministrative, financial, nd taxation readjustments" to the
end of consolidatin and improving the financial foundations
of the country". With regard to foreign policies, no statement
has been issued, but it may be assumed that the new cabinet
with Baron Shidehara in his old place as Minister for Foreign
Affairs, Will carry on the policies of the Hamaguchi Ministry
with little change.
If not greeteu With enthusiasm by the public, the new
ministry at least has the confidence of the public. Neither
the stock market nor the bond market showed any appreciable
reaction to the Cabinet change. The press, in general, ex-
pressed mild satisfaction over the appointment of Baron
Wakatsuki. Co. enting on the new Cabinet the JIJI states that
the a pointment of Baron Wakatsuki is fresh proof of the firm
establishment of the principle of party government and that when
a cabinet r signs for reasons other than political, a new
cabinet should be formed by the same political party. The
JIJI's only complaint is that more of the ministers of state
were not replaced in the new Cabinet, hitting, apparently,
at Mr. Adaohi, the Home Minister, to whom, since the'arrest
of a JIJI reporte on a politioal charge, the JIJI has been
antagonistic.
The

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
The ASAHI welcomes a new Minseito government only as
a lesser evil than a Seiyukai government. It believes that
a Minseito government was inevitable inasmuch as that party
controlls a majority in the Diet.
The Osaka ASAHI says that political events have taken
the course anticipated. The change in the War Ministry was
generally regarded as inevitable, but the replacement of the
other two ministers rather falsified the general belief that
the entire personnel would be retained. The retiring ministers
were not very popular, but neither do their successors arouse
much public interest. But the partial change in personnel
gives the nev: Cabinet some air of freshness. The ASAHI
concludes by urging the new Cabinet to keep the proclamations
made by the Hamaguchi Cabinet when it was formed.
The KOKUiiIN hails the new Cabinet in rather enthusiastic
terms. It is relieved to learn that the principle of party
government has been preserved and considers that the passing of
power into the hands of the opposition as an indirect result
of an attempted assassination would have created a bad precedent.
Baron Wakatsuki is personally popular, particularly since
his return from London where his sagacity earned him great
respect abroad and immense prestige at home. The enthusiastio
reception accorded him on his return from the Conference was in
striking contrast to the treatment frequently given Japanese
delegates to international conferences on their return home.
It is said that he does not make an ideal party le ader because
he hes few ardent personal adherents. But the supposed
weakness has proved to be his strength at the present time,
when the interests of rival bosses in the same party olashed
and an acceptable compromise candidate of ministerial
oal1bre

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
calibre was needed to fill the gap. He has a genius for followim
the line of least resistance and thereby achieving his end. Ho
has an imperturbable equanimity which, in combination with
his demonstrated abilities in the realm of finance and ad-
ministration , have brought him the friendship of his political
colleagues and the confidence of the public. Seven times a
minister of state, he brings to his office the wisdom born
of a generation of olitical experience.
Respectfully yours,
. Cameron Forbes.

Dated Ootober 21, 1931
Reo'd 3:20 a. m.
Secretary of State,
Washington.
191, Ootober 21, 11 a.m.
CONFIDENTIAL.
The main interest of the Japanese Government the past
fev days has been domestic. A large element in Japan is
bitterly opposed to the Manchurian policy of the present
Government, and they have been demanding that the Govern-
ment take a stronger attitude. The situation beoame so
serious that the Government, fearing, it is said, a
coup d'etat in the part of the chauvinistic element,
has arrested or put under strict surveillance a number
of persons supposed to be ringleaders. Among these are
several army officers who are isolated in the Gendarmerie
Headquarters.
So far the Government has been able to keep this out
of the press, over which there is a severe consorship,
extending to

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
2- #191, from Tokio, Oot.m,11
extending to foreign correspondents. I have been told
that a communication to the press will be made in a few
days.
The Minister for Foreign Affairs told me that rumors
had greatly exaggerated the affair; that there was a
measure of dissatisfaction over the Manchurian polioy;
but that none of the critics had anything construotive
to offer and the fact that, he added, that in his judg-
ment there had been no danger and insufficient evidenoe
to implicate or justify the prosecution of any civilians.
The army he said had its own discipline, whioh he oould
not discuss.
NEVILLE

The Honorable

Sir:
I have the honor to report that eleotions of members
of prefectural assemblies in 37 prefectures in Japan, ex-
cluding only Tokyo Fu, the Hokkaido and 6 other of the
total 48 prefectures, were concluded on Ootober l6th.
The results of these elections show a decided viotory
for the Minseito, the Government party, whioh returned
802 representatives as against 635 for the Seiyukai, the
chief opposition party.
The Minseito secured an absolute majority in 25 pre-
feotures, the assemblies of whieh may now be considered
aireet

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
supporters of the government party. Previous to these
elections the Minseito held an absolute majority in
only 8 prefectures. Formerly the opposition party held
sway in 14 prefectures, a figure now reduced by half to
7, the remainder having gone over to the Minseito camp.
Figures published in the press following the eleo-
tion counts showed the following results:
Minseito (Government party)
808
Seiyukai (Chief opposition)
635
(Shakai (Social Democrats)-
3
Proletarians (Taishuto (Former Labor-Farmer's party)- 13
(Independent proletarians -
1
Others -
4
Neutral -
65
1,584
The success of the Minseito in these prefectural
elections may partly be asoribed to the skilful manoouvring
of the veteran political manager, Mr. Adachi, Home Minister,
who, as commander-in-ohief of the police foroes of the
Empire is in a position to bring strong pressure to bear
in favor of his party.
The success of the Government party can not be attribu-
ted to its popularity at this time. There have been, in
fact, the strongest critieimms voiced of the "weak-kneed"
polioies of the Wakatsuki government, centering on the
Foreign Minister, Baron Shidehara, and his policies vis-à-vis
China. Any leader, in time of orisis, who counsels a poliey
of restraint and conciliation, is likely to find himselr
disoredited

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
disoredited by the general publio. There is, however,
a large section of the public which regards the present
government as sufficiently honest and sound to deserve a
full opportunity of settling the present emergenoy. There
is also, no doubt, a disinclination to "swap horses" at
a time when all the energies of the nation are direoted
toward a solution of the problems at hand.
There are indications that the public appreciates the
difficult position of the present government, and is in-
olined to support it, against the criticisms of the oppo-
sition, in its conduct of the Manchurian affair. The
Osaka Mainichi stated in an editorial on Ootober 19th:
The existing Ministry is carrying out a most resolute
China policy in accordance with popular opinion - - - -
It is when the Ministry fails to satisfy popular opinion
that the Seiyukai may with confidence claim the advisa-
bility of the Government's retirement. What people want
now is not the opposition of the Seiyukai, but rather support
of the Government in view of the exigenoy of the situation.
No popular confidence may be placed in any party which
thinks of nothing but political power at suoh a time as
this, when the need is for national union in name and
faot.
On the other hand, the Seiyukai has given no promise
of any better plan of action. It has, in fact, carefully
refrained from its usual attempts to discredit the Minseito
government, believing, no doubt, that the present time is
hardly salubrious for taking over the reins of government.
It will doubtless content itself with grumbling and
critioimm of the government party until the latter has
reaehed

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.98)
一 4 -
reached some settlement of the outstanding issues, and
will then launoh an attack on the Government party and
endeavor to oust it from power. The Seiyukai is still
laboring under the strong handicap of the onus remaining
as a heritage from the unpopular Tanaka régime, when the
government was implicated in a series of sordid soandals and
when unwise expenditures placed the country in finanoial
difrioulties.
As regards the proletarian parties, from whom so much
has been hoped - and feared - the Sooial Demooratic party
sustained a heavy loss with only three members returned,
while the Taishuto (farmer-labor party) returned but a bare
thirteen members. The reasons for the failure of the pro-
letarians are diverse. The public has never favored the
term "proletarian", which smacks of red and Russia, anathema
to all good Japanese. The proletarian parties have an un-
savory record of internal disension and biokering over
policies, and inability to decide on and follow a satis-
factory program. Moreover, the proletarian representatives
in the assemblies and Diet have done little to distinguish
themselves except, on occasion, to throw their votes to
the side whioh promised the largest advantage.
A noteworthy development is the practical disappearance
in the prefectural assemblies of the so-called neutral
parties or groups, such as the Kokumin Doshi Kai, and the
Kakushin Club. These minority groups, as well as the pro-
letarians, tend to lose out as the Japanese publio under-
stands more and more of the two-party system of government,
and grows more awake to the undesirability of returning
minority

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.92)
- 5 -
minority representatives who prove to be little but
opportunists.
Respectfully yours,
Eluim t. muith
Edwin L. Neville
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

Dated November 6, 1931

Secretary of State,
Washington.
209, November 6, 3 p. m.
My 206, November 5, 4 p. m.
In the presentation of the memorandum to Baron Shideh-
ara I had a long and friendly interview in which the
problems were very frankly discussed and the Japanese
position fairly clearly set forth. He requested me to
reply that the memorandum would receive the most earnest
consideration from himself and his Government. He out-
lined the Japanese position which was:
That before the withdrawal of troops they wanted an
agreement between the Chinese and Japanese, binding on
both, affirming the following five general prinoiples:
One. No aggression on the part of either oountry
against the other.
Two. Obligating each country to respeot the integrity
of the territory of the other.
Three.

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
2- #209, from Tokio, Nov.6,3 p.m.
Three. Agreement on the part of t he Chinese Govem-
ment to prevent the enforoement of boycott by violence,
and freedom on the part of Japanese and Chinese citizens
to carry on their trade wherever they pleased and without
intimidation. (He recognized the right of individuals
to conduct a boycott by discontinuing purchases or trade
relations when and were they pleased).
Four. Protection of lives and property of Japanese
and Koreans resident in China. In this connection he said
immediate or early withdrawal of troops until these points
were agreed upon would result in general disorder and
acts of violence against the Japanese and Koreans in
Manchuria who would, he feared, be practically driven out.
Five. Recognition and reaffirmation of treaty rights.
He expressly excluded from these problems to be
settled before withdrawal any of the details and points,
numbering several hundred resulting from injuries, destruc-
tion of property, acts of violence or violations or evasions
of treaty obligations.
I pointed out to him the emphasis laid by my Government
upon the importance of not having these agreements reached
under

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
3- #209, from Tokio, Nov.6, 3 p
under military pressure and that while the troops were
occupation military movements and engagements were of mor
or less daily occurence, they could not deny that militiry
pressure was being exerted. He was however firm in the
position that if the fundamental principles were agreed
upon withdrawal would promptly follow.
It is the opinion of Mr. Neville, our Military and
Naval Attaches and some close observers, that there is
grave danger of the militant element obtaining control
of the Government and ousting the moderate element, and
that there is much public excitement and opposition to the
conciliatory policies of Baron Shidehara.
