The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's tele-
gram No. 191 of October 21, 1931, reporting that the
Japanese Government, fearing a coup d'état on the part
of the chauvinistic elements, put under arrest or striet
surveillance a number of supposed ringleaders, and that
a striot news oensorship, which has not yet been lifted,
E
had been imposed.
C
It has been very difricult to learm whe theple
was and who were concerned because of the dfnolin

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.98)
一2-
on the part of those Japanese who know the faots to give
information. A young Japanese official, however, who
read the records of the Metropolitan Police Office regard-
ing this affair, recently supplied a member of my staff
with an outline that I feel is, on the whole, reliable.
Apparently, on Ootober llth officers of the General
Staff discovered the existence of a plot to overthrow the
Government on October 17th and to establish a diotatorship.
The principal conspirators were army officers and their
object was to do away with all obstruction by the oivilian
branches of the Government to the aims of the Japanese
army in Manohuria. The Emperor was to be kept on the
throne under the diotatorship of Prince Chiohibu, though
who the real power behind Prince Chiohibu was to be - pro-
viding he would accept the position - I have been unable to
discover. Eighteen of these officers, of the rank of
major or captain, were immediately confined to their
barracks, while "a large number" of other officers were
transferred to other parts of Japan. There were probably
civilians connected with the plot, but the only name that
my informant gave definitely was Mr. Akamatsu, the General
Secretary of the Social Demooratio Party (Shakai Minshu-to),
a political proletarian party. This party is said to have
received a sum of money in return for a promise of support
following the coup d'état. T
That a proletarian party
should ally itself with a military diotatorship seems to
be an anomaly until it is remembered that the proletarian
parties in Japan have become so impotent during the past
few months that an opportunity of grasping power over night
would be a temptation whioh organizations with more
definite

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
definite principles might find diffioult to resist.
The conspirators intended to begin operations with
the assassination of the Prime Minister, the Minister for
Foreign Affairs, the Ministers of the Imperial Household
and Finance, and the Keeper of the Privy Seal, Count
Makino, all of whom are regarded by the army as unsym-
pathetio toward its activities in Manchuria. The plants
of two Tokyo newspapers, the ASAHI and the HOCHI, were
to be destroyed for the same reason. (In this conneotion
it might be mentioned that the Manchurian policy of these
papers have altered since the discovery of the plot.)
I have been informed that those officers who were
put under restraint have now been released, possibly be-
cause they could not be punished without the matter be-
coming publio, or possibly because the temper of the army
at the present time would render unwise suoh a procedure.
This latter point, however, I have disoussed in my
despatoh No. 383 of November 7, 1931.
Respeotfully yours,
Momerm Inber
W. Cameron Forbes
Embassy's File No. 800.-Japan

Sir:
I have the honor to comment on the failure of the
civilian branches of the Japanese Government to cheek
effectively the activities of the Japanese military
the inoident of September l8th, in spite of the fact that
genuine efforts have been made to do so.
This has been, I feel, fundamentally due to the
structure of the Government. The army has practieelly
autonomous power in military matters at a time of eris
a power in part an inheritance from the foueal

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 -
in part delegated to it following the Restoration. After
the Emperor was restored to power in 1868, the feudal
armies were reorganized into the Imperial Army and it was
only natural that it considered itself the sucoessor of
the loyal olans in being the protector of the Throne.
This attitude was made effective by the fact that the
Chief of the Army General Staff has direct access to the
Emperor and is responsible to him alone in matters of
strategy and taotics.
All other officials of the
Government, with the exception of the Chief of the Navy
General Staff, can approach the Emperor only through the
intermediation of the Minister concerned, in practice
with the approval of the Prime Minister. It is this
qualification of direct access to the Emperor which
makes difficult, if not impossible, adequate control of
the military by the Cabinet in a situation such as the
present one. Onoe the Chief of the General Staff has
obtained the Imperial sanction, the civilian authorities
may find themselves in a dilemma, not only because of the
sanotity investing the person of the Emperor and his de-
oisions but because the downfall of the Cabinet can be
precipitated by the resignation of the Minister of War
if it is followed by refusal of acceptance of the portfolio
on the part of those military officers eligible for the
position.
The Cabinet can control the situation by withholding
supplies to the Army. Such action would, of course,
precipitate a political orisis. It might result in the
fall of the Government. It usually 1s settled by oom-
promise through the intervention of personages of great
publ1o.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
public prestige. These are men of age and experience
and are generally referred to as Elder Statesmen or
"Genro".
Prince Saionji is called in the public prints
"the only surviving Genro". As a matter of fact, the
institution is a necessity, in view of the complicated
structure of the Japanese Government.
This division of power explains, in part at least,
the discrepancies between statements made by officials
of the Foreign Office and statements and acts of the
ta4
military.
For example, as the Embassy reported in its
793.442330
telegram No. 196 of Ootober 26th, the Vice Minister for
Foreign Affairs told Mr. Neville that no additional troops
would be sent to Manchuria while almost at the same moment
the General Staff was informing the Military Attaohé that
the despatch of an additional brigade to Manchuria was
under consideration. The present movement of Japanese
forces beyond the Nonni River, as reported in the press,
is another instance. The Foreign Offioe stated that
Japanese troops had been sent to the Nonni River for the
sole purpose of repairing the bridge that was destroyed
by Tsitsihar forces. Aocording to press accounts, how-
ever, Japanese forces have engaged Chinese troops in
battle at a point several miles north of the river.
The
independence of the military from civilian control makes
possible actions that are contrary to the stated polioy
of the Cabinet and of which the Cabinet may be in ignor-
ance at the time of their occurrence.
This multiplex form of Government results also in
delays of decision affecting foreign polioies. These
delays may have an unfortunate effeot.
An instance of
this

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
this may quite possibly be the failure of Mr. Yoshizawa
to state Japan's case adequately at the meeting of the
Council of the League of Nations on Ootober 24th. Mr.
Yoshizawa, I am told, received his instructions only
two hours before the meeting opened. It had been
necessary that those instructions receive the approval
not only of the Foreign Office but also of the Cabinet
and probably the Chief of the General Staff, following
which they were submitted to the Emperor for sanotion,
this latter step involving their scrutiny by the Emperor's
advisers - the Keeper of the Privy Seal and the Grand
Chamberlain. It may have been this inevitable delay
which was the real cause of Mr. Yoshizawa's inadequate
presentation of Japan's policy and the resultant impair-
ment of Japan's position in the eyes of the world. At
any rate, I feel that Japan's poor presentation to the
League Council of its point of view arose in no unim-
portant measure from this division of power.
There have been other factors contributing to the
difficult position of the Cabinet. The temper of the
army has been - and is - suoh that too great opposition
by the civilian government to the activities of the
military might result in its fall. The army is undoubt-
edly exoited and is convinced that its aotivities. should
not be hampered by the Cabinet. Although this point of
view may not now be so true of the higher offioers as it
was recently, it would seem still to be true of the
lower ranking officers. In fact, I have heard from
more than one source that the junior officers are the
ohiof

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
chief source of any apprehension that may exist. For
example, I am informed that there is an organization of
2,000 officers of the lower ranks which is extremely
chauvinistio and potentially a source of danger to the
Government. In case of their deciding upon any direot
action, their troops, being under their direct command,
would probably be more loyal to them than to very senior
officers.
There is an important part of the public that has
become imbued with this militant spirit. This is to be
expected of the reserves, since they have had military
service, of the nation-wide Young Men's Associations,
which have recently been the object of considerable army
propaganda, and of the various reactionary societies.
The reactionary societies have become increasingly aotive
since September 18th.
For example, 1,200 members of
the Great Japan Justioe Association, according to the
press, offered prayers at Meiji Shrine in Tokyo on
November lst that "Japan may emerge victorious from the
present struggle over Manchuria".
The organization of
students has been effected by army officers.
The recent-
ly formed Union of Patriotio Students held a demonstration
at the Yoyogi Parade Ground at Tokyo on November 6th.
10,000 students are reported by the press to have par-
ticipated.
Following the meeting, they marohed to the
entrance of the Palace and paid their respects, then
proceeded to the Foreign Office to advise the Minister
for Foreign Affairs to resign, and finally presented to
Count Makino a resolution oritioizing his failure to
assist the Emperor as he should. The head of this
organization

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
organization is Goro Shiba, a retired general. The
Patriotic Ladies Sooiety has decided to send two repre-
sentatives to Manchuria " to console" the Japanese
soldiers. The Japanese League of Juveniles held meet-
ings at the meiji Shrine on November 3rd and 4th. The
press states that 8,ooo boys were present and that 1t was
decided to send 25 of them to Manohuria "to console* the
soldiers. These various activities may not in them-
selves be important, but they illustrate the activities
of the military, which is behind them, and also the state
of mind of a seotion of the people.

A young Japanese official who has read the

the goup d'etat recently planned by young army
officers has given the following outline of the affair
which is believed to be, on the whole, reliable.
Apparently, on October ll officers of the General
Staff discovered a plot to overtirow the Government
on October 17 and establish a dictatorship. The
Emperor was to be kept on the throne under the
dictatorship of Prince Chichibu, but who the real
power was to be is wimown. ighteen of these
oificers, majors or cantains, were confined to their
barracks, and nany others were trangferred to other
parts of Japan. There were probably civilians
connected with the plot, but the only one known is
the General Secretary of the Social Democratic Party,
a political proletarian party. The impotence to
which the proletarian parties have been reduced
during the past few months seems the only explanat-
ion, besides the rece ipt of a sum of money in return
for a promise of support after the coup , of this
party allying itself with a military dictatorship.
The conspirators intended to begin operations with
the assassination of the Prime Minister, the Minister
for

I have the honor, with reference to my telegram No.
223 of November 18, 1931, to report in greater detail the
rumors of an impending Cabinet ohange.
I must confess that it is as yet impossible to evaluate
the various rumors that of late are heard with increasing
frequeney. To a Western observer there would seem to be no
adequate reason for a change of Government, as the present
Cabinet has an absolute majority in the Diet and the Diet

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2-
is not in session. Yet neither fact signifies that the
Cabinet may not resign. If it does, the reason or reasons
may be very obscure. The preceding Cabinet, for example,
fell while the Diet was not in session as a result of a series
of affairs touching the honor of the Court, including the
death of Chang Tso-lin near Lukden and the phrase of the
Kellogg Pact - "in the names of their respective peoples,
while the Cabinet before that, after successfully surviving
a Diet session as a minority party, fell because the Privy
Council disapproved of a proposal of the Government regard-
ing financial adjustment.
The standards of Western politios
cannot, then, be applied to the present instance and the
rumors now current must be given more attention than they
would receive in an Occidental country.
The principal charge brought against the present
Government is that of weakness - weakness with respect to
the activities of the Japanese military since September
and
18th weakness vis a-vis the League of Nations.
Yet with
the army possessed of super-Cabinet powers in a time of
594,00/322
crisis, such as the present, (Despatch No. 383 6f November
7, l93l), it is very doubtful if any other Cabinet could
have done much, if any, better, while the "weakness" of
the Government toward the League of Nations has had one
result which might be regarded as a diplomatio victory of
possibly no small importance. The Powers are apparently
confident that the oivilian government is blameless in the
Manohurian affair. This has perhaps resulted in the bring-
ing of less outside pressure to bear on Japan than might
have been the oase had a Cabinet as militant as that of
the late Baron Tanaka held office at this time.
The charge

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
The charge is also brought against the present Cabinet
that it has not improved the financial and economio situation
of the country as it should have done. Yet it has done more
in that direotion than any of its predecessors.
It removed
the gold ban and is maintaining the yen above par.
It has
reduced expenditures and has partially succeeded in the
difficult task of limitation of Japanese armaments.
It has
faced a situation world-wide in character and has led the coun-
try as adequately as any other government, lacking its ex-
ample, could probably have done. The no-loan policy of the
present Cabinet, though it has recently been interrupted,
was maintained for more than two years. Inoidentally, I
might mention that this is regarded by certein thoughtful
students of Japanese politics as the first genuine political
principle to be announced and maintained by a Japanese
political party, political parties in this country being
so far scarcely more than groups of followers of certain
strong personalities combining for party interests rather
than for the sake of prinoiples.
In defending the present Cabinet in the preceding two
not
paragraphs, I have done so, to enhance its reputation but to
point out that a change at present does not seem necessary
and to indicate that a subject discussed confidentially in
the latter part of this despatch may gain in significance
through the foregoing comment.
In trying to discover a reason for the possible fall
of the Cabinet in the near future, it is necessary to re-
call those faotors that decide the destinies of Japanese
cabinets. In brief they are: the Emperor's personal and
official advisers, the personal relationships within the
Government

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Governnent or tne Government party, and what may be termed
political realities.
One or all of t ese may come into
play at the time of a Cabinet crisis.
t present it would
seem that political observers feel that the next change of
Cabinet will be brought about by either the first or the
thira factors.
Whether or not the conjectures in this
despatch may or may not be subsequently confirmed by events,
the comments contained herein will at least aid the Depart-
ment to understand whatever conclusion may be the issue of the
present rumored movements.
It should be remembered that
Japanese politics has a genius for the unexpected.
Four
years ago, for example, when the Wakatsuki Cabinet was about
to fall, few if any political observers realized, until per-
haps at the last moment, that the late Baron Tanaka would be
called upon to form the succeeding Cabinet, despite the faot
that he was the president of the leading opposition party.
Should the rumored change of Cabinet result from what
may be referred to as "political realities", it will probably
be due to Mr. Adachi, the present Home Minister, entering
into an intrigue with Mr. Tokonami, now of the Seiyukai.
Either of the two parties, the Minseito and the Seiyukai,
are composed, as I have pointed out, of various factions
under the leadership of certain political "ohiefs".
The
olassic example of such a leader is Mr. Tokonami.
To take
up only the later perigrinations of this politioian: he
left the Seiyukai in l924 to assist in the formation of the
Seiyu-honto; the Seiyu-honto was amalgamated with the
Kenseikai in 1927 to form the Minseito; this he left in
1928 to establish the Shinto Club; this, in turn, he des-
troyed in 1929 to return to the Seiyukai, to whioh party he
now belongs.
Throughout his wanderings he has been followed
faithfully by oertain henohmen, for he is a man of an unusually
pleasing

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
pleasing personality, and with Japanese politioians a
personality is regarded generally as more important than
political principles.
Lr. Adachi is also an adept in ambiguities. To him
the Minseito owes in large measure its political victories
of 1929 and of this autumn (Despatch No. 370 of October 24,
1931).
r. Adachi returns to Tokyo to-morrow from the
Autumn lwanoeuvres and following his return, the press re-
ports, there will be activities on his and Mr. Tokonami's
part aiming toward an orientation of their two groups of fol-
lowers that will result in fornation by them of a so-called
coalition cabinet.
A brief description of the alignment behind these two
men may not be without interest.
Lr. Adachi's followers
are at present all within the Minseito. But Mr. Adachi
is said to be a disgruntled man because he failed to become
premier after the resignation of r. Hamaguchi in April of
this year. Lir. Tokonami has, of course, the bulk of his
followers in the Seiyukai, but some of his former henohmen
are in the Minseito, having remained in that party when
their leader left it in 1928.
These include Messrs. Hara,
Kobashi, and Tanaka, all recently Ministers of State in the
Minseito Cabinet.
There is reason to believe that these
former followers now in the Minseito are once more coming
under his influence. This was indioated last month by an
incident that aroused the interest of politically minded
Japanese. Mr. Kobashi, Minister of Education in the Minseito
Cabinet until late in l929, when he was foroed to resign
becauss of implication in a bribery scandal, was recently
found "not guilty".
A congratulation celebration was held
at Ueno Park in Tokyo last month. It was attended of
course

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
course by Minseito men. There was, however, an unexpeoted
guest; namely, Mr. Tokonami, now of the Seiyukai. His at-
tendance is regarded by many as significant support of the
persistent rumors that he and Mr. Adachi, with their followers,
are drawing together.
It is quite possible that this scheme will not be
realized, as such a union would be the result of tactical
expediency and would be a denial of the slow progress in
this country toward genuine parliamentary government. It
is very doubtful whether it would find favor with the Elder
Statesmen. Prince Saionji, termed "the only surviving
Genro", and Count Makino were called upon by the Emperor in
1927 to select the successor of the fallen prime minister;
they indicated Baron Tanaka as the new prime minister, ap-
parently because of their desire to establish parliamentary
government. It is doubtful if they would countenance a re-
turn to those days- only too recent- when secret pacts be-
tween political groups might be the foundation of cabinets.
If the Elder Statesmen are again called upon by the
Emperor to choose the man to form the next Cabinet and if
they do as they did in l927, their choice will probably be
Mr. Inukai, president of the Seiyukai, the Opposition Party.
Mr. Inukai is paired with Mr. Ozaki as one of the leaders
of the parliamentary movement in Japan, a man of incorruptible
integrity and a rare character in Japanese politioal life.
He was chosen president of the Seiyukai in lg9 chiefly be-
cause that party realized that only a man of unblemished
character could extricate the party from the disgraceful
situation in which the party found itself following the
series of scandals that occurred during the regime of the
Tanaka

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
Tanaka Cabinet. There would seen to be, however, serious
dralbacks to the selection of r. Inukai as premier.
The
Seiyukai's reputation is still very unsavory; the prestige it
lost when the scandals became known has not yet been regained;
the party has no definite principles, and it could scarcely be
expected to deal with the present crises more adequately than
the Cabinet now in office.
Furthermore, Mr. Inukai is
seventy-six years old, and it is problematical whether he
has the vigor necessary for the duties of prime minister.
Certain observers, in whon I place some confidence,
have told me that these difficulties may be partially cir-
cunvented by having .r. Inukai, as prime minister, form a
coalition cabinet of the best men of the two parties and
some independent men.
Thus the prime minister would come
from the opposition party, helping in this way to establish
parliamentary government to a certain degree, while the
ministers of state would be of a calibre more fit to cope
with the present situation than a purely Seiyukai seleotion.
Although the present prime minister, Baron Wakatsuki,
is generally regarded as "weak", he is the head of the
majority party and is a man of exceptional intelligence. It
is possible that he may be directed, should the present oabi-
net fall, to form a coalition cabinet of Linseito, Seiyukai,
and independent men.
This would not be a suspension of
parliamentary government nor a retrogression - since his is
the majority party, and such a cabinet would insure a continu-
ance of present policies in a perhaps more vigorous form.
Several other men have been mentioned as probable Prime
Ministers in case of a cabinet change.
Among them are
Baron Tatsuo Yamamoto and Baron Kiiohiro Hiranuma.
Baron
Yamamoto is a former Cabinet Minister and at one time was
head

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
head of the Hypothec Bank. He is at present an adviser
to the Minseito. Baron Eiranuma is Vice President of the
Privy Council.
I have very recently heard a report of great interest,
if true, as yet impossible of substantiation. The report
is that followin the formation of the next Cabinet (in
the very near future) the Emperor will issue an Imperial
Rescript in wich there will be phrases intended to lessen
the extra cabinet powers of the military.
The resoript
would, presumably, be arawn up by the Emperor's personal
advisers.
There have been runors that some influential
personal advisers of the Emperor have been opposed to the
wide military action in anchuria. It will be recalled
that Count  akino, Keeper of the Privy Seal, was one of
those high officials said to be marked for assassination in
the allesed plot of certain military ofricers discovered
894.00/321
last month (my despatch No. 382 of November 7, 1931).
It
is not at all impossible that if a change of Cabinet should
occur in the near future the real object of the change will
be to check the army.
▲ new cabinet, entrenched behind an
Imperial Rescript touching upon the subject of army control,
could adopt, I should imagine, a much more vigorous attitude
toward the military than has hitherto been possible. In
considering this matter, it would seem that an Imperial Re-
script of this nature,would be a very able first step on the
part of the opponents of the military to bring the civilian
government into a position where it can control effectively
all branches of the Government. The army might be offended
and angry, but it would scarcely dare aot because of the
sanctity

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.88)
- 9 -
sanctity of the Emperor. I shall, however, go more deeply
into this subject in a later despatoh.
Respectfully yours,
Mamcem
Forheo

The

toward their activities in Manchuria. The Embassy states
that it is informed that the officers who were put under
restraint have now been released, possibly because they
could not be punished without the matter becoming publio
or possibly because the temper of the army at the
present time would render unwise such a procedure.
The Embassy's No. 382, disoussing the sub ject of the
failure of the civilian branches of the Japanese Govern-
ment to check effectively the recent aotivities of the
Japanese military, states that this failure has been due
fundamentally to the structure of the Government in which
the army has practically autonomous power in military
matters at a time of orisis. The despatch deals with the
origin of this. feature of the Japanese Government
organization and its workings at the present time. "It
is this qualification of direct access to the Emperor
which makes difficult, if not impossible, adequate con-
trol of the military by the Cabinet in a situation such
as the present one"; "this division of power explains, in
part at least, the disorepancies between statements made
by officials of the Foreign Office and statements and
acts of the military"; and frequently oauses delay in
reaohing Government decisions.
The

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.92)
- 3 -
The despatch continues that there is an impertant
part of the public that has become imbued with the
militant spirit propagated by the military party.

ation
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION BETWEEN SECRETARY STIMSON
AND THE JAPANESE AMBASSADOR, MR. KATSUJI DEBUCHI.
F/H
SH
Manchuria.
I sent for the Japanese Ambassador. When he came
I told him that since I had asked him to come I had
894.00/330-1/
received a despatch from Ambassador Forbes telling me of
the resignation of the Japanese Cabinet, and we discussed
that for a few minutes. The Ambassador said he had also
received a cable but it merely gave the fact of the
resignation and no items. He commented on the fact that
it was very fortunate that this change had not come
before the final passage of the Resolution at Paris
2
yesterday, and that he thought it very likely that
Wakatsuki had withheld his resignation until the
Resolution had passed. He said he had no doubt that the
affairs in Manchuria were the reason for the resignation.
He said that Adachi, who controlled a good many votes
in the Diet of the Minseito Party had been agitating
for a long time for a change in the Cabinet but that
the Emperor had a great deal of confidence in Wakatsuki,
Shidehara, and Inoue, and Debuchi said he hoped that
MAR
those men would remain in the Cabinet.
2 1933
I then told him that it was being asserted against
me

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE SECRETARY
- 2 -
me that I had assured Debuchi that America would not
use the boycott, and I asked him whether he had gained
any impressions in our talks which would lead him to
believe that I had given such assurance. He at once
said no. In order to be doubly sure, I pressed him,
and he said that on one or two occasions he had gotten
the impression without anything being said that I
personally did not favor the boycott, but that I never
had said anything at all to give any suoh assurance.
He used the word "official" once or twice and I pressed
him on that. He said that there had been certainly
nothing which he could understand as "official" assurance.
Then he told me he had heard that from one of my press
conferences at Woodley the newspaper men came away with
a feeling I was not in favor of a boycott and he had
heard that the same thing was said at Geneva, but he
said he had also seen it denied several times that any
such assurance had been given. Finally, I had a press
clipping of an account of a speech by Henry Cabot Lodge
to that effect, and I dictated a letter of inquiry addressed
to Debuchi based upon that, asking him whether anything
had ever occurred in our interviewe to give him the
impression that he had received any such assurance from me.
▲8

=== Page 186 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
THE SECRETARY
一3 -
As I saw that he would feel hurt if I sent such a letter
to him formally and asked for a reply, I said I would
just read that to him and ask him whether on the statement
as read he could say yes or no. After I read it he said
his answer was certainly no, and he took a pencil and
wrote, "No. K.D." on the letter. I gave him a copy of
what I had written, and I told him that I felt very
badly even to press him like this but that he could
realize the matter was serious and that I had been made
very angry by the accusations. I told him I had no idea
of publishing such a letter but I might go after the man
who had made the attack and I wanted to be sure of my
ground with him (Debuchi) before I did so. I asked him
whether he had ever by any chance said anything that
might give anyone else the impression that he thought
I had given such assurance, and he said no. But he
told me of having a talk with a group of Japanese
business men in New York at which time the matter was
discussed. I asked him whether he said anything of
that sort to Toshi Go, the agent of the South Manchurian
Railway Company in New York. He said that Go was a very
insignifioant person and he hoped I was not paying any
attention to what he said.
Debuohi

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.98)

THE SECRETARY
-4-
Debuchi in this entire interview made a very
favorable impression upon me for his frankness and
honorable attempt to give me fully and frankly the
impressions which he had received in our conversations.
He evidently had received the impression that I personally
did not like to contemplate the use of a boyoott, which
was the truth, but that I had never said anything that
could be taken as an official or even a personal
assurance.

Resignation or the Wakatsuki Cabinet.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Reference Tokyo Embassy's telegram No. 258,
December 1l, 6 p.m. Rumors of an impending Cabinet
894.00
note
change have been current in Japan for some time (see 
894.00/335
2
Tokyo's 232, November 24, 2 p.m.). The reasons for these
reports seem to lie in:
(1) The attitude of Mr. Adachi, the Home Minister
and perhaps the most influential member of the Cabinet,
who was reported to have been dissatisfied because he was
not selected to succeed Mr. Hamaguchi as Premier when the
latter resigned.
(2) The diffioulty of balancing the Budget for the
coming year and the prospeot that Japan might have to
restore the embargo on the export of gold which was re-
moved when the present Cabinet came into power.
(3) The Manchurian situation.
Tokyo Embassy's mail despatch No. 396, dated
November 20, reports that the prinoipal charge brought
against the present Government is that of weakness with
FILED
2
respect to the aotivities of the Japanese military since
September 18 and weakness vis-à-vis the League of Nation
The

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
The Tokyo Embassy, in its 247 of December 1, 6 p.m.,
reported that the immediate Cabinet crisis seemed to have
passed; that Adachi, who had agitated for a coalition
Cabinet, had subsided for the present at least and was
not making any further moves.
Recent press reports from Japan indicate that the
Premier had given assurances that he would maintain a
firm attitude on the Manchurian question.
Tokyo Embassy's 258 of December 11 indicates that
the resignation of the Wakatsuki Cabinet was the result
of political intrigue and not of policies or actions of
the Government, nor, according to the Embassy's reports,
due to anything connected with the Manchurian situation;
it seems pr obable that either Baron Wakatsuki will be
called upon to form a new Cabinet or that this charge
will be given to Mr. Inukai, President of the Seiyukai,
the Opposition Party.
C
Mr. Inukai is an elderly statesman, 76 years old,
and is described as a "man of incorruptible integrity
and a rare character in Japanese political life". The
Party of which he is President, however, still has a
very unsavory reputation and it is likely that if
Mr. Inukai is charged with the formation of a new
Cabinet it will be made up largely of men outside his
own politioal Party.
on

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
On the other hand, if Baron Wakatsuki receives the
mandate to form a new Cabinet it is likely that some of
the new members will be chosen from the Seiyukai, which
has declared itself in favor of a stronger foreign
policy and of less rigid economies in the Government's
financial measures. Its position on the former may be
gathered from the following resolution (as reported in
the JAPAN CHRONICLE, November 11) on the Manchurian
problem, adopted at a general meeting of the Diet members
of the Party on November 10, 1931:
"Manchuria and Mongolia are the life-blood
of the Japanese Empire. The present Manchurian
affair is the outcome of Japan's exercise of the
right of self-defence, which has for its keynote
the protection of the Japanese residents and
Japan's vested rights and interests in Manohuria.
It is absolutely impossible for Japan to consent
to the withdrawal of her troops unless a sure
guarantee for their security is obtained. If
the League of Nations shows itself incapable of
seeing the situation and continues to offer
intervention or put coercion on Japan, instead
of amending its present mistaken course, Japan
may well withdraw from the League. Let the
whole nation unite for the maintenance of peace
and order in Manchuria and Mongolia and for the
execution of the national polioy which consists
in the upholding of the general stability of the
Far East."

The outgoing Wakatsuki binet, which was organized
in April to succeed the Humaguchi Jabinet, was regarded
from the first as a stop-gap governnent. Baron Wakatsuki
had been persuaded to emerge from retirement to assume the
presilency of the party and the mantle of the wounded Mr.
Hamaguchi

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
Hamaguchi in an attempt to bring into line the disorganized
forces of the Minseito party. This party had shown definite
signs of cleavuge hen the strong hund of the old Lion,
amaguchi, was reluxed, tending to split into groups around
several poverful personulities, namely r. Adachi, r. Egi
and the now Governor General of chosen, General Ugaki. ir.
Wakatsuki ur ced to keep these vuricus elclerts fron open
revolt for icurly eitht nonths, but his control over them
vas never ccrtair. Withcut & strone central leader und
troutled vith internal dis ension as to its policies, the
Yukatsuki Jubinet as conceded by many observers no chance
of survivin: the Diet even if it managed to live until
the session oponed.
The out oing subinet, during its short tenure of office,
Wus called upon to fuce n extraorlinary number of important
issues, chief of which Wus the manchurian problem. In these
few months, problens of overnment finunce and compilation
V
of the pudget of the next fiseal year have iven grave anxiety
to the Cabinet. The chief accomplishments of the Wakatsuki
Government have been a reduction of official sularies, a re-
duction in the pension system, the maintenance of free export
of gold. nd the appoint ment of several outstanding men to
important offices, such as General Uguki to the Governor
Generalship of Korea and Count Uohida to the presiuency of
the South Manchurian Railway. on the other hand, there was
strong criticism of the Government. It had been compelled
to abandon some of its promises. Its policy of strict re-
trenchment in government expenditure had been impossible of
accomplishment

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
accomplishment, and the Ministry's budget could not be
balanced without loans, in spite of its no-loan promises.
There is little doubt but that the repudiation of its
fundamental principles, announced at the time of the
inception of the Hamaguchi regime and again enunciated at
the formation of the Wakatsuki Cabinet, had a great deal
to do with the downfall or the Minseito government.
The imnediate cause of the downfall of the Wakatsuki
Cabinet, as stated in my telegram No. 258 of December 11,
was
apparently the result of a poli ical intrigue on the
part
of r. Adachi, Home Minister, and the collapse seems
to luve had o direct connection vith any action of the
Government,  r, as might easily have been expected, with
the anchurian situation. r. dachi's actions, for the past
few weeks, have aroused much conment and speculation. I
have on a number of occasions reported to the Department
that Adachi is perhaps the most powerful leader of the
Minseito, holding the allegiance of the best organized
group in the party. He has long been suspected of aspiring
to the premiership, and is said to have been disgruntled
because he failed to be chosen to head the Government after
the resignation of Ir. Hamaguchi in April. Although he is a
man of unrivaled genius for political organizing, he is
supposed to lack the higher qualities desirable in a statesman
heading the Government.
Early in November Mr. Adachi cast a bombshell into the
politidal situation by announcing to press representatives
that he would welcome a "national" or coalition Cabinet in

In the above account, I have touched on the immediate
causes

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.97)
-9-
causes of the fall of the Wakatsuki Cabinet, and have
mentioned the part which the failure of the Wakatsuki
Ministry to live up to its pledged policy in regard to loans
and financial retrenchment, layed in the fall of the cabinet.
There are other factors which contributed to the situation.
Chief of these, perhaps, is the influence of the powerful
financial and comercial organizations which dictate the
financial policies of Japan. The long continued depression
consequent upon the severe retrenchment policies of the Minseito
Government had given much dissatisfaction-to these interests.
Some had opposed the lifting of the gold embargo, two years ago,
and as the deflating effect of this measure bore doin on the
nation, causing a stagnation of trade, they were seeking for
a change that would promise relief, however temporary it might
be.
V
gain, there is much to inaicate that the military authorities
o the Empire huve of lute been increasin ly impatient with
civilian interference, purticularly in regard to the Manchurian
situation.(See Embassy's despatch No. 423, December 18,1951).
The Seiyukai, with its record and tradition of a "strong policy"
vis-a-vis China is said to have appealed to these authorities as
more amenable to the military views than the ilinseito. It is
safe to say that the army had no little influence, even though
possibly indirect, in the downfall of the Wakatsuki Cabinet and
in the establishment of the Seiyukai Government. In this con-
nection it may be worth noting that the new Minister of War is
rumored to be the leader of that group of young officers which
is said to dominate the army and which seeks to extend the powers
of the Military in government.
It is interesting again to note the absolute power of
decision which the Genro retains in a country where theoretically
the representative form of government has been established.
It was in his power to reappoint Wakatsuki; to have vreated
coalition

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
coalition ministry, or to choose a straight Seiyukai govern-
ment.
It would be interesting, but futile, to speculate on
the reusons for the Genro's decision. The Japanese press
praises the wisdom of Suionji in allowing the opposition to
succeed to power after the fall of a government, thereby
helping to develop the system of responsible government.
It is said thut the Genro before making his recommendation
to the Enperor, asked  nukai if he Would form a coalition
With the inseito. Inukai, it is reported, feeling his own
strength in this crisis, demurred and the Genro allowed him
to form a straint Seiyukai Ministry .
The new . inistry was greeted with no great show of
enthusiasm by the public. To a certain extent any new doctor
who would attempt to prescribe a ne. cure for the ailing
country was welcome, but the public hus not forgotten the
sordid scandals und Wste of the lust Seiyukai inistry
under the late General Lanaka. he hope is expressed in
the press that the new Cabinet under r. Inukai, who himself
hus the confidence of a large section of the public as regards
his p rsonal integrity and honesty, vill cleanse itself of
the soiled elements in the Party.
r. Inukai is un able politician. During a long political
career he has been elected to the Diet eighteen times and has
served as a inister of State three times previously to the
present.
He has before him at this time, however, a path
beset vith extraordinary difficulties. His most immediate
problem is that of the situation in the Diet, where there is
V
& margin of about eighty seats in favor of the Opposition.
There is much speculation as to whether there will be a dis-
solution in the forthcoming session of the Diet . Both the
Seiyukai

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.98)
-11-
Seiyukai and the Minseito are apparently averse to the
idea of dissolution, with its risks and expenses, but the
force of circumstances may precipitate the action. The cabinet
is, however, leaving nothing to chance.
Wholesale changes of
officials throughout the country, already announced, may be
taken to indicate that the new overment intends to lose
no time in establishing  strons govermmental machine on which
to rely in the event of an election. If the Government can
effect some compromise ith the inseito, it may do so, but if
the Minseito proves recalcitrant, the Goverment will be left
n. alternative but to dissolve the Diet and call for a general
election.
This the inseito would also avoid, for it is a
political axiom in Japan that the Government party always
wins in elections.
Aside from this problem, which is purely political, there
are others of far greater iportance Which the new Cabinet
must face.
of these the most outstanuing is the .anchurian
dispute, ith its complications in Paris and at home. In this
problem the Cabinet is fortunate to be in more complete accord
with the army than its predecessor, and will be able to act
2
without the friction and discord which marked the past. r.
Yoshizawa, reported as chosen to be the new oreign Minister,
is an expert on Chinese matters and was Minister to China
rior to his appointment to Paris. .s the son-in-law of the
Premier, it may be expected that he will cooperate closely
with I.r. Inukai.
In the field of domestic affairs, the most mportant
question for the new Government is the financial situation.
V
The first act of the new cabinet was to reimpose an embargo
on the export of gold, in accordance with the proclaimed
policy

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.97)
-12-
policy of the Seiyukai, reversing the policy followed by the
Minseito Government for the past two years(See Embassy's
despatch No. 406, December 2, 1931). This move, which was
made as an expedient to afford relief to hardpressed industry,
will have, it is feared, serious effects on the nation as
a whole. ihere is still the matter of the unbalanced budget
to contend with. r. akahashi, the new inister of Finance,
has announced that the new cabinet will adopt, in the main,
4
the budget which the previous government had under pre-
paration. The deficit is to be cared for by new loans.
The new Jabinet is fo tunate in huving made no definite
co mitients or promises to the public, und in thus being
unhampered in dealin with problems that confront it. There
are rumors that the cabinet will draw up a program of policies
in the near uture, but as yet none has been announced.

I have the honor to supplement my despatoh No. ses
of November 7, 1931, regarding the autonomous power of
FEB85182
the Japanese Army.
The autonomous power of the Japanese Army rests
prinoipally on the right of the Chief of the Generel
to enter the presence of the Emperer and to ebtain his
sanction of military policies and war plans witheut the
approval of or interferenee by eny ether organ of

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2一
Government. As this right - more briefly knowm as the
"right of direct access" (1-a-ku jo-so) - is also possessed
by the Chief of the Navy General Staff, comment in this
despatch respecting the power of the Army is equally ap-
plicable to the Navy. There are also other officers of
the Army and the Navy with this right: namely, Army
Division Commanders, Commanders of the Chosen, Taiwan,
and Kwantung Armies, the Inspeotor General of Military
Education, Commanders-in-Chief of Naval Stations and
Fleets, and Commanders of Minor Naval Stations.* As
any such officer, however, obtains Imperial sanction only
in connection with matters lying within his sphere of
duties, the right of direct access gains its principal
signifioance by the fact that the two Chiefs of Staff
oontrol strategy and taotics. The senior member of the
Supreme Military Council also has the right to enter
directly into the presence of the Emperor, but only in
an advisory capacity.
This right of direot access has had, I am convinced,
a very vital connection with the Manohurian affair. Had
this right not existed, the inoident at Mukden of September
l8th could soarcely have developed into the present serious
situation. In fact, it is quite possible that no inoident
would have ooourred.
Before entering, however, into a disoussion of the
relation of the right of direct access to the Manchurian
incident, a brief desoription of the division of power
that
* Page 256 of the KEMPO SKIGI (The Essential Meaning
of the Constitution) by Dr. Tatsukiohi Minobe, the
leading living commentator on the Japanese Consti-
tution. Published in 1927.

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.97)
4
- 3 -
that exists in the Japanese Government may help to olari-
fy the disoussion. In Japan all power is teohnically in
the hands of the Emperor.*
The Emperor is advised by
the various Governmental organs that have the right of
direot access after which, theoretically, he makes de-
cisions - actually, he sanctions whatever course of polioy
or action may be presented by the head of the organ "ad-
vising" him. The following diagram indicates the various
bodies which are directly under the Emperor. Each one
has the right of direct access. In some cases an organ
may have no relation to any other organ, or it may have
a conneotion that is more or less tenuous.

---Connecting Links.
* Dr. Shinyo Uesugi, one of the foremost commentators
on the Japanese Constitution, stated in his commentary,
published in 1924, that "The Emperor possesses in his
palm the power or ruling and controlling all arfairs of
State and must not be treated on the same level with re-
gard to the privileges of the sovereigns and rulers of
other countries.'

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
This chart illustrated the independenoe of the
Army and the Navy of control by other branohes of the
Government. The connecting link with the Cabinet is
the presence on the Supreme Military Counoil of the
Ministers of the Army and of the Navy, who report to
the Prime Minister the proceedings of the Counoil. s
the Supreme Military Council* does no more than advise
the Emperor with respect to such matters as may be pre-
sented to it and as it seems in practice seldom to meet,
this connection is not especially significant. (In
actual practice the Cabinet's one effective @heck on
the military is control of supplies, a cheok that, if
not exeroised with the greatest caution, might preoipi-
tate a serious crisis.) The presence of the two
Ministers on the Supreme War Counoil is, technioally, the
Prime Minister's only source of information of what the
military is contemplating. As neither Chief of Staff
needs, in practice, to submit to the Supreme War Counoil
his plans affecting strategy and taotics but may submit
them direotly to the Emperor for sanction, an important
policy or action can be initiated without the knowledge
of other branohes of the Government.
(In a subsequent despatch I shall discuss in detail
the civilian organs of the Government which have the
right of direct access and which are wholly or partially
independent of one another; namely, the Audit Bureau,
which is independent of the other organs; the Imperial
Housohold
* The members of the Supreme War Counoil are: the
Field Marmhal, the Fleet Admiral, the Minister of
the Navy, the Minister of the Army, the Chier of
the Army General Starf, and Generais and Flag
Ofricers especially appointed Supreme War Counoillors
by the Throne.

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Household Department, whose conneoting link with the
Cabinet is the Minister of the Imperial Household but
which has powers beyond the control of the Cabinet; the
Courts, whose only conneotion with the Cabinet is the
transmission of decisions to the Emperor through the
Minister of Justice, the latter being, however, according
to one of the framers of the Constitution, "no more than
a messenger boy" in this case; the Cabinet, which pre-
sents administrative measures to the Emperor; and the
Privy Council, which "advises" the Emperor with respeot
to certain measures of the Cabinet.)
This independence from other Governmental organs,
which either Chief of General Staff enjoys, made possible
the military action of September l8th that developed into
the virtual control of Manohuria by the Japanese. I have
been informed by a sound and very conservative authority
that the procedure in this case with respect to Imperial
sanction was in general as follows: the Chief of the
Army General Staff foresaw that eventually Japanese lives
and property in Manchuria would suffer from Chinese ag-
gression; that he therefore obtained Imperial sanction -
some time before September l8th - to take whatever
measures might be necessary for protection of Japanese
lives and property in case of Chinese aggression; that
the Commander of the Kwantung Army was informed that this
sanotion had been obtained; and that "when the Chinese
destroyed the South Manohuria Railway tracks near Mukden
on September l8th*, the Commander of the Kwantung Army
thus had Imperial permission to take "whatever action he
might deem necessary" for the proteotion of Japanese
11vos

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
lives and property. My informant concluded scornfully
- for he is apparently not an admirer of Baron Shidehara
- "the Minister for Foreign Affairs knew nothing about
it." From this it may be inferred that he meant also
that the civilian organs of the Government did not know
of the plan of the Japanese military against the Chinese.
When they did learn of it, the power to deoide what
measures against the Chinese were necessary lay with the
military.
In addition to the great latitude of action, derived
from the right of direot aocess, this right has also had,
I believe, a profound effect on the mentality of the
Japanese military, an effect that has been a very impor-
tant factor in the Manchurian affair. Had the right of
direst appeal not existed, it seems probable that the
attitude of the Japanese military toward oivilian branches
of the Government would have been leeearrogent-and less
arrogant and less impatient of non-military direction.
For an accurate description of this attitude, a brief
historical survey is necessary.
Following the Restoration of the Emperor in 1868 by
the loyal clans, a group of men began the modernization
of Japan. The feudal armed foroes were succeeded by a
modern army.
In the organization of this army that
group of men first modeled it after the Frenoh army and then,
following the defeat of France in l870, after the Prussian.
The independence of the military from civilian control
was an important factor in each of those military systems.
There were three outstanding reasons why this system
of an independent military should have appealed to those
Japanese who modernized the army. To gain the loyalty
of

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.98)
of the country in support of the restoration of the
Emperor and to discredit disaffected clans, the group in
power emphasized consistently that the ancient prerogatives
of the Emperor were being restored to him after having been
usurped by the Shogun since the time of Yoritomo (1l92 A.D.).
The placing of the army (and the navy) directly under the
Emperor was described as a return to the Emperor of one
of those traditional prerogatives. Secondly, those men
who restored the Emperor and who modernized Japan were
themselves warriors or members of the loyal warrior olans.
It was natural that they would look with disfavor on any
2
system that would subordinate the military to civilian con-
trol.*
Thirdly, the Napoleonic and Prussian systems were,
indeed, the nearest approach to Japanese experience, for
from antiquity to the Restoration the control of political
power in Japan had been retained only by armed force.
The Office of the General Staff was created in 1878.
Dr. Minobe, the leading living commentator on the Japanese
Constitution, wrote in 1927 in this regard as follows:
"The (Japanese) General Stafr Office . . . is a replica
of the German (Prussian) General Staff Office. In Germany
(Prussia) a olear distinotion or demarcation was established
between military power and political power, the General
Staff Office being vested with an absolute power to con-
trol the army quite independently of the Government.
(In Japan) there exist two different bodies or organs
advisory to the Emperor, one for the exercise of his
supreme power over general political and administrative
affairs
* In this connection, the recent statement of one of the
men who wrote Japan's Constitution is of interest.
He said that at a meeting of those men who were modern-
izing Japan's army, the question of control came up.
Both General Saigo and Prince Yamagata objeoted violent-
ly to civilian control, pointing out that, if the Army
and Navy were subordinate to the Cabinet, at some future
time a political party might reduce either braneh far

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
affairs (the Cabinet) and the other for the exereise ef
his. supreme power over the Army and the Navy (the General
Staff) . . . Those matters belonging to the latter
are not inoluded in the sphere of the funetions of the
Cabinet Ministers . . . Those who are in a position
to advise the Emperor on military or naval affairs . . .
belong directly to the Emperor and are not under the
control of any Minister of State."
In a recent conversation with a member of my staff,
one of the framers of Japan's Constitution, a staunch
supporter of ultra-conservative ideas, repudiated the
suggestion that the Prussian system had been imitated.
He maintained stoutly that modernization of the Japanese
army had been no more than a return to pre-Yoritomo
days. The truth, I believe, includes both arguments.
As a result of the theory that the right of direot
access with its corollary that from earliest times the
army has been under the direct control of the Emperor -
except when that power was illegally usurped,-the Army re-
gards itself as heir to a noble tradition, the historieal
protectors of the Emperor's person and the Emperor's
honor. This belief, together with other principles
of the Emperor cult, is instilled into officers and
mon, I am informed, during their period of military
training. It is not unnatural, therefore, that the
Army in Manohuria felt humiliated by quiescence before
Baron Shidehara's conoiliatory polioy in Manchuria;
believed the honor of the Emperor and of the Army im-
paired by the attitude of Chang Hseuh-liang and his amy
far below the proper strength.

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.98)
-  -
an attitude issuing from the conciliatory policy; and
that, finally, when Imperial sanction had been obtained
and a pretext found, destroyed Chang's power with vigor,
determination, and éclat, brooking no civilian efforts
at control.
Herein, it would seem, lies also the erplanation of
the reported insubordinate action of younger pffioers in
the face of higher authority, whether civilian or military.
The alleged plan of a military coup d'état and establish-
ment of a dictatorship* is an illustration of this mental-
ity in its most exaggerated form. But there have been
indicati ns also that junior officers in Manchuria have
gone further than their senior officers had commanded.
Such indiscipline has been chiefly due to, it seems to me,
the effect on the military mind of the right of direct
access to the Emperor and all that it implies.
In conclusion, I should like to disouss briefly the
legal source of the right of direct access in modern
times, for I oannot help but feel that efforts will be
made in the future by civilian organs of the Government
to bring the military under civilian control. . The dif-
ficulty of success in such an endeavor will be increased
by the fact that the right of direot access to the
presence of the Emperor seems to rest more firmly in
V
tradition and usage than in the written legal word, and
in the written word implioation may prove to be more ef-
fective than exact expression.
The Minister of Justice, for example, said in a
recent oonversation that the right of direct access is
granted by Artiole XI of the Constitution. s this
Artiole no more than states that "The Emperor has the
supreme
*Despatoh No. 382 of November 7, 1931.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.98)
- 10
supreme command of the Army and Navy", it is not very
helpful in the settlement of one of the most perplexing
problems of present-day Japan.*
Dr. Minobe, the commentator, does not give the
Constitution as the source of this right but Article
(Imperialordirance,or chokarei, no, 135, sigued Decumbea 25,1889-CC
VII of the regulations of l889 defining the organization
of the Cabinet, which was established in 1885. This
[Can be found in Horei Zencho, volume12, 22nd year
Article reads:
of Meiji, tage 320;available inthe ibrary of Congrees - CC]
"Article VII.Notice shall be given by the
Minister of the Army or by the Minister of the
Navy, as the case may be, to the Prime Minister
of those matters that pertain to military seorets
and command which are submitted to the Throne, ex-
cepting. those cases wherein such matters are speo-
ially submitted to the Cabinet by order of the
Emperor."
Doctor Minobe states that this article refers to what is
known as "direct access". The article appears, however,
to be inadequate as a definite, indisputable legal souroe
of this right.
The only other regulations in which the right of
direct access is mentioned (or implied) - as far as the
Embassy has been able to discover after exhaustive research
- are those regulations that govern the Office of the Army
General Staff and those that govern the Office of the
Navy General Staff. Artiole II of the former reads as
follows:
"The Chief of the (Army) General Staff is ap-
pointed by the Emperor from among generals and
lieutenant-generals of the Army and is placed
under the direct control of the Emperor. He
participates in military affairs of the Imperial
Headquarters (Iaku) and controls all plans relat-
ing to national defence and military taotios, be-
sides supervising the General Staff Office."
Artiole II
* Article XII of the Constitution is also usually
oited in this conneotion. It reads: "The
Emperor determines the organization and peace
standing of the Army and Navy."

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
Artiole II of the latter reads:
"The Chief of the Naval General Staff is
2
under the direct control of the Emperor and
participates in the important affairs of the
Imperial Headquarters (Iaku) and also controls
the official business of the General Staff.
The Chief of the Naval General Staff is appointed
by the Emperor personally."
These regulations were issued in l908, long after the
right of direct access had become firmly established.
Whether or not it was dealt with in previous regulations,
extended search by the Embassy has not revealed. It is
not, however, a vital question, as regulations of an
office that delegate to that office so important a right
could scarcely be regarded as the legal source of that
right.
In short, written legal sources of the right of
direot access to the Emperor by either Chief of General
Staff is sufficiently indefinite to make the question
one of great controversy among Japanese scholars.
very vagueness, it may be, is a source of strength to
those who maintain that the military should continue in-
dependent of control by civilian branches of the Govern-
ment. Advocates of the present division of powers
would seem to have as their chief sources of strength
both tradition and usage. I shall, however, discuss
the legal aspect in greater detail in a subsequent des-
patoh dealing with the right of direot access by other
organs of the Government.

in its preparation was Visoount Kentaro Kaneko, who as-

Referring to my despatch No. 423 of December 18,
1931, describing the autonomous power of the Army that
derives from the right of direct access to the Emperor
enjoyed by the Chief of the General Staff, I have the
honor to report the resignation of General Kanaya as
Chief of the General Staff and the appointment on December
23rd of Field-Marshal Prinoe Kagin as his successor, a
change

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
change that certain observers feel will strengthen the
position of the higher military authorities. In this
connection, I am transmitting, as of possible interest,
copies in translation of two articles that appeared
recently in Japanese periodicals, one dealing with the
influence of certain army officers and the other with the
relative strength of the Foreign Ofrice and the Military
since the outbreak of the Manchurian affair.
Considerable speculation has been aroused by the
appointment of Prince Kanin to this post, the most im-
portant in the Army as the Chief of Staff decides matters
relating to strategy and tactics and can obtain Imperial
sanction without the approval or interference of any
other organ of the Government. Although Prince Kanin
is reported to be a capable officer, as his career in-
1/.
dicates (see enolosed Confidential Biographic Data),
doubt is entertained whether he would have been seleoted
were he not a member of the Imperial Family. It is, of.-
course, impossible to substantiate such speculation, as
the reasons for the appointment would scaroely be made
public. A brief report, however, of the conjecture
created by this appointment may not be devoid of interest,
especially as some competent observers believe it to be fact.
I have already reported from time to time that senior
officers of the Army since the beginning of the Manchurian
affair have been said to find diffioulty in restraining
junior officers from action beyond the limit of orders
and to fail in certain instances in such restraint.
The
appointment of a Prinoe of the Blood as Chief of Staff
will tend to curb, it is said, these fiery younger officers.
So great is their reverence for the Emperor that the de-
oisions

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
cisions of a Chief of Staff, who is at the same time a
member of the Imperial Family, will be regarded, accord-
ing to report, as even more imperative than has hereto-
fore been the case. That a junior officer would go
beyond the orders of his senior may seem improbable to a
Westerner who knows Japan to be a remarkably well-
disciplined country. It is, however, but another
example of that curious indiscipline which is apparent
at times in the various branches of Japanese official
life. A recent example was the violent statement issued
to pressmen by a comparatively junior official of the
Foreign Office without consultation with the Foreign
Minister, following the receipt of the garbled Associated
Press report of the Secretary's remarks of November
793.94/3271
27th (Despatch No. 407 of December 4, 1931).
The appointment of Prince Kanin as Chief of Staff
may also, it is believed by some, further weaken civilian
interference in military affairs.
This refers to a
possible lessening of the influence of one of the
Emperor's personal advisers, Count Makino, Lord Keeper
of the Privy Seal. Count Makino is regarded by most
Japanese as opposed to the Manohurian program of the
Military. He was one of the five high civilian officials,
it will be recalled, who were marked for assassination in
the alleged military plot of Ootober against the Govern-
894.0013215
ment (despatoh No. 382 or November 7th).
If the military
consider him an impediment to their plans by reason of
pacifio advice tendered to the Emperor, it is possible
that such advice may in the future be discounted by the
Emperor now that his prinoipal military adviser is a
member of the Imperial Family.

In this conneotion an unsubstantiated report, ob-
tained from a personal friend of General Tatekawa, a
delegate to the Geneva Disarmament Conference, of the
reason for the resignation of General Kanaya, may have
some pertinence. Aocording to this report, which I be-
lieve came directly from the Military, since the out-
break of hostilities in Manchuria General Kanaya has
assured the Emperor, who is said to. be opposed to an
offensive program, that Japanese military action is
purely defensive. On November 27th General Kanaya
obtained Imperial sanction for the withdrawal of troops
from the direction of Chinchow. Subsequently, however,
when the military decided to advance again, General
Kanaya, because of past commitments respecting the de-
fensive character of action in Manchuria, could scaroely
approach the Emperor for a reversal of the sanction of a
few days before, especially as the new move seemed to be
of an offensive character. Kanaya thereupon resigned
so that a Chief of Staff unhampered by such commitments
could be appointed.
It may not be out of place to add
that it is unusual for a Chief of Staff to resign at a
time of crisis suoh as the present.
If Prince Kanin is to be something of a figure-head
as Chief of Staff - a matter as yet of supposition - one
of the two enolosed articles perhaps gains in significance.
Both artioles appeared in December issues of two reputable
Japanese periodicals and, though neither writer has any
especial reputation and accuracy cannot be vouched for,
2/.
they indioate illuminating points of view. The one which
appeared in Kaizo (Reconstruction) deals with two military

ful figures: Lieutenant-General Araki, who has become.

894100/337
No. 422 of December 17, 1931), and General Koiso, the

The article begins with an account of the
efforts of the Foreign Minister to check the military
after being surprised by the outbreak of hostilities in
Manchuria. It then describes General Araki, stating
that he enjoys the greatest popularity with younger

who have been abroad and have gained knowledge of Mussolini,
Hitler, and Hindenburg (perhaps a reference to the alleged
desire of certain younger officers for the establishment
of a military dictatorship in Japan); that he is chauvin-

power (a point of view held, I believe, by a large section
of the Japanese). According to the writer, although
Araki is extremely popular, General Koiso holds the actual
administrative power of the Army.
This is interesting
in conneotion with the view that Prinoe Kanin may be

for if the writer's remarks about Koiso are true the

The General Staff, this writer claims, has diotated to

on the authority of the Minister of War.
