SADAO ARAKI AND KUNIAKI KOISO
By Tatsuo Iwabuchi.
(The KAIZO of Deoember, 1931.)
At present the Army is active in political ciroles,
making itself the central figure. It can be said that
the Army is piloting the political world. It seems as
if the guiding policy of the administration has been
transferred from political parties to the Army.
As regards diplomacy, the Shidehara diplomacy or
the Foreign Office traditional diplomacy, whichever it
may be, has changed its color considerably within only
a month and a half.
When the Manchurian incident broke out Shidehara
was greatly surprised, nay, he seemed to be very indignant
on account of the seriousness of its nature. By this in-
cident the diplomatic foundations, which he had built up
after years of effort, fell. Therefore he thought he
must hold down the army. If the army assumed a strong
attitude and would not listen to his assertions, then
his only step was to resign. Perhaps he thought that
that time had already arrived.
With this determination
and polioy he attended the Cabinet Counoil.
The Cabinet,
in compliance with Shidehara's views, decided the follow-
ing items:
to take no offensive military aotion, to
cheok aggravation of the incident, and not to interfere
with the internal affairs of China. The War Minister,
Minami, approved Shidehara's policy. On account of
this decision, the despatoh of troops to Chientao and
Harbin was halted and at the same time the troops sent
to

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
to the Kirin district were gradually withdrawn to the
regions contiguous to the South Manohurian Railway.
All this was reported at the meeting of the Counoil of the
League of Nations between September 22nd and 30th. Up to
this point Shidehara's diplomacy proceeded.
However, the Army interpreted this matter in the
following light: the defeated troops and mounted
brigands were swaggering in Manohuria; therefore, in
order to prevent aggravation of the inoident, it was
thought necessary to take offensive measures.
If the
Japanese Military's activity should be negative and if
offensive measures should be taken by the Chinese, the
incident would be aggravated. If suoh should be the
case, the situation would become dangerous or disad-
vantageous to Japan not only from a strategic standpoint
but also from that of protection of our rights and inter-
ests. The concentration of our troops in the neighbor-
ing regions would become much more difficult and meaning-
less. In the meantime, the Chinohow inoident ocourred
and the meeting of the Counoil of the League of Nations
reopened about this time - Ootober 13th to 25th.
The final vote of thirteen to one brought the
Shidehara diplomacy to a standstill. Although it is
hard to foretell what will be the outcome of international
relations it would be difficult for the League of Nations
to apply an economic blocade in accordance with Artiole
IV. However, for the maintenance of the dignity of the
League of Nations, it seems that it will very likely ex-
press suoh an intention, as a result of the vote of
thirteen to one.
How will Shidehare adjust this phase of the diplo-
matio

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
matio situation?
The Army is in high spirits, like a
wild horse.
The Government is suppressing this high-
spirited horse and is trying to administer the affairs
of state.
. The result lies in the future.
Then, what
is the policy that is to be taken by the Army?
In this
connection it is interesting to know what kind of men
there are in the Army.
In glancing at the Army, the men that attract most
attention are Kuniaki Koiso, the Director of the Bureau
of Military Affairs of the War Department, and Sadao
Araki, the Chief Inspector of the Directorate of Military
Training.
Strictly speaking, the present backbone of
the Army is the young officers below the rank of colonel,
and it is Araki that is most popular among the young
officers. Araki is the center or nucleus of the
military staff, together with Koiso, in his political
activities in connection with the present question.
Certain people compare Araki with Shungaku Matsudaira
(the celebrated ex-daimyo of Echizen).
Whether it is a
good comparison or not the writer does not know.
The
position of Matsudaira was, however, indispensable to the
Shogunate government and was important in the amalgamation
of state affairs and militarism.
uiscontented with the
world aspect of the situation, there are many who are in-
dignant and who deplore what the League of Nations has
done and is doing.
. Such arguments are not necessarily
unjustifiable. The way is open for a new situation in
the course of long months and days amongst such various
thoughts and movements. Under the circumstances it is
not strange that such arguments should arise.
Suoh
arguments are apparent also in the Army.
For instance,
most

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
most of the promising young officers who go abroad have
observed the tendency of post-bellum ideas and actual
political movements. It is not unreasonable for them
as military men to think about what will become of Japan
when, upon their return to Japan, they hear about parli-
amentary administration, scandal, and cries for disarma-
ment - after having seen Mussolini, Hitler, and Hinden-
burg. However, Araki's idea is quite contrary to that
of the young officers. He says that our national con-
dition is different from that of Italy and Germany.
In
foreign countries the development of a situation begins
always from below but in Japan it is just the opposite;
for instance, the Restoration was from above.
The
water of a great river is muddy at its mouth but that
of the source is clear.
There lies the spirit of the
foundation of the Empire. The nation must not fail
correctly to understand the true quality of Japan.
He
particularly warns the young officers not to feel in-
dignant at our state of affairs simply by seeing the
examples of foreign countries.
There is something in him that reminds one of an
old samurai. He actually carries a real Japanese blade
instead of a parade sabre on his loin. There is an air
about him of the extreme soldier. Shortly before the
Russo-Japanese war, when the situation was oritical, the
youngsters at that time clamored to chastise China and
Russia. Araki was one of them and his heart burned
eagerly for the rise of Japan.
Araki had made the solution of a continental policy
his life work. For this purpose, he spent many years,
most of the time in Russia, from the end of the Russo-
Japanese

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Japanese war to the despatch of the expeditionary foroe
to Siberia.
When he worked hardest was at the time of
the despatch of troops to Siberia. He was at that time
in Harbin under Nobuyoshi Muto, the present Inspector
General of Military Education. Araki's ideal was to
make east of Baikal a boundary line of defenoe for Japan.
Unfortunately, the Government had no definite policy
owing to the constant ohange of the situation.
For
this reason, as his view could not be accepted by the
Government, he thought it was useless for him to remain
in Harbin and he returned to Japan after having submitted
a memorial to Giichi Tanaka, then Assistant Chief of the
General Staff Office (later Premier).
Therefore, Araki's
North Manchurian policy can be called an historical
policy.
Tanaka said to Araki that his view was too
violent and he could not transmit it to the Genro
(Yamagata).
Then Araki was very angry and turned fieroe-
ly upon him, saying that it was outrageous. He was
simply stating facts and Tanaka was shelving the memorial.
Araki in appearance looks like a maiden and his
attitude or bearing is quiet and like that of a polished
gentleman, but his heart is burning with the fire of a
patriot.
Araki's conception of the present situation is this:
Japan is now facing a crisis. There is no telling but
that Japan may fall to the position of a seoond-rate
nation like Spain if care is not taken. We are at a
point of critical moment either to maintain the national
honor or prestige or to become a second-rate power.
As the situation is getting so complicated, there is no
other solution than to go ahead with full energy to face
the

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6 -
the situation with one ideal. This state of chaos cannot
be overcome by wisdom or artifice; if one attempts to
accomplish the object by artifice he will fail. The
present age is very much like the period when war against
Korea was advocated. Men like the great Saigo are needed
to control dissatisfaction with the worldliness that ex-
ists in Japan and to halt the tendency toward indolence
and pleasure.
If Araki is an idealist, Koiso is a practical man.
Araki is of the Staff line. Koiso is a military adminis-
trator.
Within Koiso sharpness is hidden. He has pluck also.
Probably he is the statesman-politician of the Army.
When the Manchurian incident broke out, Koiso stopped
at the Foreign Office on his way to the Army Department
and, shaking hands with the Director of the Asiatio
Bureau, said that the incident would become a big affair
and could not be settled easily.
"For the sake of our
country let us go hand in hand."
He went baok and forth
between the Foreign Office and the War Department constant-
ly.
I do not know what the upper circles think of it,
but I do know that there is a perfect unity in the
opinions of the Army and of the Foreign Offioe among
those who are engaged in doing aotual work.
It seems that the Army is going to unify the
national polioy of Japan and make it known to the world.
As to this national policy, Koike explains it in the
following manner:
When the question of the inorease of the two
1191A
divisions was brought up, the following was contained
in

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
in the address to the Throne: *After the country is
opened, develop the national power with a progressive
spirit and expand the national fortune or fate and our
Empire's national policy and never change, even though
the surrounding conditions may change." The Army is
applying the above statement exactly as it is to the
Manchurian question, at the same time asking the recog-
nition of this by the Government and the nations.
This attitude taken by the Army overthrew the Saionji
Cabinet in the early days of the era of Taisho when the
question of the increase by two divisions was taken up.
The present attitude taken by the Army resembles very
much that of the previous case. The political situa-
tion of the present and of that time resemble each
other, the only difference between the two being that
an international question is involved in the present
Manchurian incident.
The security of our rights and interests in Manchuria
and the bill for the revision of the military system
whioh is to be enforced from the next fisoal year are
inseparable for the Army.
When the military system
revision bill was submitted to the Cabinet, Koike ex-
plained it at the Cabinet Council,in the following
manner:
"The London Treaty has caused a defeot not only in
the Navy but also in the entire national defence of Japan.
According to the Treaty, Japan maintains 70% parity
against Great Britain and America until 1935 but after
that becomes much inferior.
There is no telling when
and what will happen regarding international affairs.
Manohuria is vital to us.
When we look at the con-
dition

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
dition of Manchuria, China has been infringing on our
rights and interests there year by year. Thus, con-
templating the recovery of Manchuria, - if we keep our
eyes on what China is plotting - we find that she is
watching for an opportunity when our power of national
defence shall be inferior to that of Great Britain and
America. When she seizes such an opportunity she will
probably demand the abolition of the twenty-one demands
and the recovery of Port Arthur and Dairen. If the
twenty-one demands were abolished, the South Manohuria
Railway would have to be handed over to China in l938.
When these things were effected, it would be beyond the
power of Japan to do anything. This is the reason why
the completion of the plan for the revision of the mili-
tary system is fixed for the fiscal year beginning April,
1938. This is the reason why the national defence must
be completed just as soon as possible.
This is the united opinion of the Army as regards
the plan for the revision of our military system. Koiso
made a full and unreserved statemen* attack on policy, be-
ginning with the London Treaty and ending with the present
time, inoluding the attack on Shidehara's China diplomacy,
which arroused those who listened to it. Most of the im-
portant positions in the Army at present are ocoupied by
graduates of the Class of l879 of the Military Academy.
The prinoipal ones are Nimomiya, Vice Chief of the General
Staff; Sugiyama, the Vice Minister; Kashii, the Commander
of the Tientsin Garrison; Lieutenant General Shunroku
Hata; Lieutenant-General Shinji Hata; Lieutenant-Gemeral
Yanagawa; Lieutenant-General Ushijima, President of the
Military Aeademy. The prominence of the graduates of
tho

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.98)
- 9 -
the Class of l879 is due to the administration of personnel
by General Ugaki. Ninomiya and Sugiyama are considered
as direct adherents of Ugaki. However, there is Nobuyuki
Abé next to Ugaki. Koiso was made Director of the Bureau
of Military Affairs when Abé was Acting Minister.
The centre of power was gradually shifting from
Ugaki to Abé, from Abé to Minami, from Minami to Koiso - one
of the Class of l879 - before anyone realized it.
If Araki has become the central figure of the Army
spiritually, Koiso holds the reins of actual power and is
the backbone of the Army.
The intention of the Army to go forward in the
development of our national prestige and right by establish-
ing a national polioy, aiming at the great Saigo, is good.
However, what the Army considers good for Japan is not
necessarily good for Japan. On the whole, what is the
best thing that should be done for the good of the country?
No one can tell until after it becomes history.

FEB 1 9 1932
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
894.00/343
Sir:
I have the honor to supplement my telegram No. 19 of
January 21, 1932, reporting the dissolution, by Imperial
Ordinance, of the Diet in its sixtieth session, on the day
of its convening after the holiday recess. The Ordinance
simply read, " We order the dissolution of the Diet, in
accordance with the provisions of Article 7 of the Im-
perial Constitution".
After the dissolution, the Cabinet, in an extra-
ordinary meeting, decided to fix February 2o as the date
for

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
for the general election made necessary by the dis-
solution. This date was approved by an Imperial or-
dinance.
According to the Law of the Houses, it is necessary
for a newly elected Diet to meet within five months of
the election, but it is customary for the session to
convene about ten days after an election. Thus, the
Lower House to be elected next month will probably
assemble in an extraordinary session early in March.
The dissolution of the Diet had been generally
anticipated and caused no surprise. The Government
could hardly be expected to carry out its administra-
tive policies while it was opposed in the Diet by an
opposition majority. The party affiliations of members
of the Lower House at the time of dissolution were as
follows:
Minseito
247
Seiyukai
171
Undetermined
29
( including Adachi faction)
Independents
3
Vacancies
16
The situation is similar to that existing in 193o, when
a Minseito government lead by Hamaguohi found itself op-
posed by a Seiyukai majority in the Diet. Also, the step
taken by the present Government to solve the situation
was similar to that taken by Mr. Hemaguchi, except in
one particular. The Hamaguohi Government allowed the
Seiyukai, then the Opposition, to formulate an interpella-
tion on the Government poliey and to debate the government
policies in the Diet before asking for dissolution. The
Inukai

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Inukai Government, however, dissolved the Diet immediately
after the last offioial speech had been delivered in the
Lower House.* For this action it has brought down the
almost unanimous censure of the press, which accuses
the Government of muzzling the Opposition and of using
big stick methods for attaining its end, to the detriment
of the cause of constitutional progress. True, the
Cabinet presented its policies to the House of Peers on
the morning of the dissolution and allowed interpellations
from the Peers, but the Opposition Party, which is not
numerously represented in the Upper Chamber, was not thereby
given an adequate opportunity for criticism.
The press also expresses regret that, since the dis-
solution was a foregone conclusion, a general election
should not have been called for as soon as the present
Cabinet took office, instead of waiting until the con-
vening of the Diet. The ASAHI, one of the most powerful
newspapers of Japan, has been particularly insistent upon
the calling of a general election before the convening of
the Diet. The reasons for not so doing are obvious. The
Government Barty was hesitant to call for an election
immediately after appointment for fear of being criticized
for using " strong arm" tactics and forcing an appeal to
the electorate. Moreover, no new government cares to begin
its administration with a step that will cost the country
many millions in money. It may be added that until the
Sakurada Mon incident of January 8 when an attempt was
made
*
For the speeches of the government leaders vide
Embassy's despatah No.474, January 29, 1932.

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
一 4 -
made on the life of the Emperor, the Government party
had some chance of surviving the Diet session without
an appeal to the electorate. The opposition, knowing
that the maintenance of its strategic position in the
Lower House depended on its good behavior and cooperation
with the Government, would not be inclined to risk an
attack on a newly appointed Cabinet unless furnished
with an unusually strong bludgeon. This it has in the
Sakurada Mon affair, and the Cabinet, knowing this to be
the case, probably decided to act before the attack com-
menced.
Lastly, the Cabinet has probably delayed calling for a
general election until its own political machinery had been
built up throughout the country. Since the appointment
of the Inukai Cabinet, widespread changes have been made
in the personnel of governors and police officials of the
prefectural governments, who are in a position to bring
great influence to bear in elections, and at present the
Seiyukai can face the elections with a certain amount of
confidence.
Study of the editorial columns of the Japanese press
indicates that the public realizes that the coming election
presents a question of decision of unusual importance to the
Nation at large. It is obvious that neither of the two major
parties is popular. The impending election is significant
in that to an unusual extent it is to be a contest between
policies and platforms, rather than personalities as is
usually the case in Japan. There has seldom been an
election in whioh the issues have been so well defined
and the difference between the stands of the opposing
parties so marked.
In

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
In regard to finances, the Seiyukai plans to raise
funds and to balance the budget by issuing bonds and sus-
pending the national sinking fund, without increasing
taxation. The Minseito proposes to solve the problems of
national finance by increased taxes and by administrative
adjustments. Against the inflation policy of the Soiyukai,
the Minseito stands for currency deflation and the stabili-
zation of exchange rates. There is a radical difference
between the two parties on the question of the gold standard.
The Minseito Party has consistently stood for free export
of gold and maintenance of the gold standard, while the
Seiyukai has been an advocate of inflation and an embargo
on gold.
The Seiyukai proposes to carry out an ambitious five-year
industrial plan, to cost Yen 360,0oo,c00, and to organize a
monopoly of rice. It proposes plans for extensive waterway
and highway construction, as well as improvements in com-
munications and railways. In contrast to these positive
sohemes, the Minseito declares for a stricter regulation
and rationalization of industries, looking toward an in-
creased efficiency of output.
Lastly, there is the matter of the attitude of the two
793.94
parties toward the military activities in Manchuria. Neither
party has, in its public statements, made mention of its
attitude toward the Army, but it is understood that the
Seiyukai stands for close cooperation with the military au-
thorities, while the his tory of the Minseito shows that it
has opposed expansion of the powers of the military govern-
ment and had endeavored, with little success, to ourb the
activities of the Army in Manchuria. It may be mentioned
that neither party has come forward with any definite plan
for

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
for Japan to follow in Manohuria after the present
military action is completed. Both parties expatiate on
the importance of that region to the existence of Japan,
but neither have any definite proposals for consolidating her
position in Manchuria.
The Seiyukai, being the Government party which, under
ordinary circumstances always wins in elections, is conceded
the better chance of success in the coming elections. In
the first place, the Government Barty has the advantage of
administering the election laws and of prosecuting, or
overlooking, breaches of the regulations. For this purpose
it can make changes in governmental and police personnel,
insuring that its own interests are supported. Secondly,
the Government party is usually more amply provided with
campaign funds than is the Opposition. In the present
case, this point has unusual significance. The reimposi-
tion of the gold embargo by the Inukai Government enabled
several big banking firms to make enormous profits on
exchange, and the natural expectation is that these firms
will now share their profits with the Government Barty.
Under ordinary conditions a government newly appointed
to office would have a clear advantage in a general eleotion
for the simple reason of newness and absence of an unfavor-
able past record. These factors operate to some extent
in the present election, but since its assumption of office
the Seiyukai has had a tremendous blow dealt to its fortunes
by the Sakurada Mon affair. This affair, touching as it
does the person of the Emperor, is an uncertain quantity
in political caloulations, but has certainly hurt the
Seiyukai cause. The Minseito will undoubtedly use it to
some extent in attaoking the Government, but it is a dangerous
woapon

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
weapon, as it may easily prove to be a boomerang for
anyone using the Emperor's name for political purposes.
Furthermore, the Seiyukai has never completely recovered
from the evil reputation it earned during the Tanaka
Ministry when the Party was involved in graft and scandal.
The Seiyukai does have the advantage of good organi-
zation and leadership. Mr. Inukai, with his long years
of political experience, is unsurpassed as a leader, and
he has shown courage and resourcefulness in the few weeks
he has headed the Government. On the other hand the
Minseito will be handica ped in the election by the lack
of firm leadership. Baron Wakatsuki has not proved to be
a strong leader, and the Perty is still suffering from
the shock of the Adachi schism, as well as from the loss
of that wily election manager( See Embassy's despatch
894.00337
No. 422, of December 17, 1931).
I shall not fail to report to the Department the
outcome of the general election to be held on February
20th.
Respectfully yours,
Jnhes
W. Cameron Forbes.

Sir:
I have the honor to submit the following comfont
on the domestic situation in this country.
Thecom-
ments are submitted with some hesitanoy, due to the fact
that they are not suseeptible of direct proof; the only
foundation they have is that many Japanese feel that some
change of a serious nature may occur in the political
field. In some cases it seems to be scarcely more than a
vague uneasiness, in other instances there is an attempt at
definition.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
definition.
There are several sources from which it would seem
change might come. I have had occasion in several recent
despatches to discuss the powers of the Japanese military
and the tenor of the military mind, particularly with re-
gard to the more junior officers, - from colonels downward.
The Manohurian affair, as I have previously reported*, is
an emanation of the former; of the latter - the abortive
plot of last October on the part of younger officers to
establish, allegedly, a diotatorship.**
In this connection I should like to report am: in-
cident that is said to have occurred the following month
in the office of the Chief of the Army General Staff.
Some weeks ago the Embassy received a hint of an untoward
occurrence of a nature somewhat similar to the alleged
October plot. The usual informative sources of the
Embassy, however, seemed ignorant of the matter. It
was only a few days ago that I obtained some details in
this regard, which I believe to be fairly worthy of
credence. At least, those Japanese - with whom I have
since discussed the affair - while professing ignorance,
unanimously agree that the incident sounds most probable.
In November, I am told, a junior officer entered the
office of General Kanaya, who was then Chief of Staff,
and drew a revolver. Before he could do any mischief
he was seized and disarmed. When questioned as to the
reason for his attempted act, he replied that the Manohurian
polioy of the General Staff was too weak. Instead of
this
894.00/338
* Despatch No. 423,or December 18, 1931.
** Despatoh No. 38z of November 7, 1951.
4.00/321

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
this officer being held for possible punishment, he was
thereupon told: "You have the right idea* - I quote my
informant - "patriotism is a splendid thing, but you mis-
understand us. Our policy is not weak. You must give
us time."
The young officer was then released. I have
little doubt that he was set free because of the fear of
the senior officers that his punishment would cause trouble
among his contemporaries. For the same reason, apparently,
the plotters of October were not punished. On the contrary,
I have been reliably informed, a number of them have since
received promotions.
This alleged inoident may have been given too much
emphasis in this despatch, but it is illustrative of the
point of view said to prevail among the younger officers.
The Embassy has had occasion to report recently the
related information that General Araki, now Minister of
War, is the senior officer regarded as having the strongest
control over the junior officers*, that he is said to be
their "idol", and that for this reason he was made Minister
of War, as for similar reasons Prince Kanin, a member of
the Imperial Family, was last month appointed Chief of
Staff.*
Since the time these matters were submitted to
the Department as "the opinion of competent observers",
they have come to be regarded as facts by thinking Japanese.
The consensus of intelligent Japanese opinion at
present seems to be that General Araki's popularity with
the junior officers has been impaired by his failure to
resign

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.98)
resign after the attempt against the person of the Emperor
early this month.*
Not unnaturally, the youngor offieers,
inculcated with reverence for the Emperor, would expeet
their "idol" to be the first to resign. Some Japanese
believe, however, that it was General Araki who prevented
the Cabinet from following the desire of the majority of the
Ministers of State to resign, the reason given for his at-
titude being that the army leaders prefer the subservient
Seiyukai Party in power to a return of the less amenable
Minseito, which would bring as Foreign Minister the much
disliked Baron Shidehara. I have heard several Japanese
refer to the present Cabinet as"the Araki Cabinet, which
would seem to signify that the Seiyukai Cabinet, through
him, is under Army control. (In this connection it is
interesting to note, as possibly relative, that an addition-
al budget of Yen 20,910,000 for the expenses of the Man-
churian affair from September l8th to the end of the
fiscal year (Maroh 31, 1932) was approved by the Cabinet
without opposition and was yesterday approved by the Privy
Counoil.)
Whatever may be the facts, it appears that one
control of the super-patriotic officers has been weakened.
The foregoing matter is cited because the Army is a
very important element in the Government and would be a
big factor in any political change. It would seem not
inoonceivable that the Army might take over the Government
in form as well as in substance.
This idea is regarded
as most improbable by many Japanese; as possible by others.
At least, the potentiality is one factor in the general
uneasiness to which reference was made at the beginning
of this despatoh.
n
* Despateh No. 460of January 16, 1952.
894,001H61/59

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
An important proportion of the Army apparently regards
capitalists with suspicion and dislike and since capital-
ists (the Mitsui and the Mitsubishi interests, chiefly)
are believed to be behind the political parties, the
Seiyukai and the Minseito receive a share of this feeling.
It would not be surprising if, in its arrogance, a part
at least of the Army should feel that civilians are less
capable than the military in government. Certainly the
increasingly critical economic situation that exists here
might easily be regarded as proof of the failure of parlia-
mentary government and political parties. The Army must
further be aware that an appreciable number of intelligent
civilian leaders view with alarm the grandiose expedition
on which the Army has been embarked since September 18th.
They feel that it will lead to eventual economio and
financial ruin. These civilians may be expeoted, when
an opportunity is presented, to attempt curtailment of
the powers of the Army. Of this even the Army must have
some realization. There seems to be an increasing opinion
among thinking Japanese that the Army may fail in Manohuria.
If this should happen the Army might blame the failure on the
civilian government and oust it so that the powers of the
military may not be cut down and so that - in its opinion
- the Emperor's Government may be effioiently administered.
This would be virtually a military dictatorship.
There are factors that would run counter to such an
ambition were it entertained. For example, the Army 1s
said to be disunited. The senior officers and the junior
officers are not mutually sympathetic. It has been stated
that military affairs in Manohuria are in the hands of four
comparatively junior officers (mentioned as being Lieutenant-
Colono1

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
Colonel Ishiwara, Colonel Itagai, Colonel Doihara, and
Major-General Miyake, Chief of Staff of the Kwantung Amy)
who disregard the desires of General Honjo, the Commander
in-Chief of the Kwantung Army. United action might be
difficult in respect to a coup d'état, lessening the
probability of success. I shall attempt to obtain furthor
information along this line, but it is, of course, difficult,
for the military, despite an astonishingly naive frankness
in some aspects of their plannings, is in others very
secretive.
In my despatch No. 473 of January 29, 1932, I have
dwelt at some length on the serious economic and financial
situation of the agriculturists of Japan, who form approxi-
mately half of the population of this country.
The ex-
treme docility of this class beneath increasing burdens
- which is indicated by their lack of organization - leads
one to believe that possible disorders in the rural dis-
tricts would be of a sporadic character and more or less
confined to tenant farmers, with scarcely any other objec-
tive than injury to those whom they feel to be immediately
responsible, namely, landlords, and without any definite
desire for political change. However, should trouble
break out elsewhere, it would not be impossible that the
tenant farmers might assist.
A military ooup d'état
might be welcomed by them, particularly if they come to a
realization a few months henoe that the inflation polioy
of the present Government will not bring them relief.
Mention should again be made, as the Embassy has in-
dioated in the past, that prediction with respeot to action
by Japanese is next to futile, except as an assurance that
- 1f

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.97)
- 7 -
- if they do anything - it will be the unpredioted.
There
is this to be said, however, on the other side: the Japanese
may be amazingly long-suffering and give no indication of
dissatisfaction - only to exhibit it suddenly and aotively.
In the Navy, for example, there have been instances of ap-
parently acquieseent sailors suddenly throwing overboard
an officer against whom they have conoeived a grievance.
In the American navy such resentment would probably be
noticeable in many ways before an outbreak of violence.
Despatches are now in preparation, for submission in
the near future, dealing with sooial unrest among the
laboring classes and allegedly increasing radicalism. I
shall not go into detail here, but one indication of appre-
hension in regard to labor's dissatisfaction is the police
guard maintained at the houses of those families of the
Mitsui interests that profited by the "dollar coup" of
last month.* In regard to the "red" movement, I have
been reliably informed that the Court is genuinely per-
turbed. It is possible that the fear of the Mitsuis may
have no more solid foundation than a guilty conscience,
for their profit was at the expense of the countryts in-
terests, or, at least, of no assistance to the economio
situation. The Court, too, may have no real reason for
its apprehension.
Yet, whether or not the foregoing may ever become
pertinent in the light of subsequent events, it is at
least a partial explanation of the atmosphere of possible
ohange of which many seem to be consoious.
Respectfully yours,
KTC

URGENT.
35, February 24, 2 p. m.
URGENT AND STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL FOR THE SECRETARY.
This morning during a pause in the plenary meeting
Sato sought out Gibson and Davis who were sitting in
an ante-rocm and requested permission to sit down and
talk with them.
In substance he said that he and Matsudaira were
deeply concerned about developments at Shanghai and
admitted that they had reached such proportions as
not only to jeopardize their position in Manchuria
but to necessitate either a greater military effort
at Shanghai or cessation of hostilities. They were
convinced the latter course was essential and were
considering making strong recommendations to their
Government that Japan take initiative in proposing steps
to terminate hostilities and to establish peace with
the concurrence and assistance particularly of the
United States and England.
He seemed

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
2-#35, from Geneva,Feb.24,2p.m.
He seemed to think that it would be more logical
to procced under the Nine Power Treaty which has the
advantage of excluding Russia but perhaps the
disadvantage of bringing in the few small powers
parties to this treaty that could contribute little
to a settlement.
Another thought which seemed to strike Sato as
importan: was that the Nine Power Treaty was the
most lo ical arproach in that it gave to Japan the
means of takine the initiative thus saving its face
and provided the possibility of a hopeful result
before the meeting of the Assembly Iarch 3.
In conclusion Sato said he and Matsudaira were
seriously worried by the way the Japanese military
authorities had got out of hand and felt that unless
Japan's future was to be jeopardized some means must
be found for stopping hostilities, reestablishing
cooperation with the Western Powers and getting the
conduct of affairs back into the hands of the civilian
authorities and that they would make strong recommenda-
tions along these lines to their Government.

Before the eleotion it was generally antioipated that
the Government party (Seiyukai) would win. However,
even the most optimistic of the Seiyukai leaders had
not dared to predict a victory as complete as the election
actually brought to their party. The latest election

The majority thus obtained by the Seiyukai is the
largest of any in the parliamentary history of Japan.
The casual observer of the election might conclude, from
the enormous majority gained by one party, that issues of
extraordinary importance had been presented to the decision
of the voters, and that the election returns represented
a definite expression of public will. Actually, this was
not the case, insofar as I am able to determine. One may
look in vain for any outstanding factor which would turn
the support of the public toward either of the two major
parties.
The student of the recent elections would notice that
little reference was made to party principles in this
election. They were, in faot, a minor consideration. Both
parties, of course, declared their principles. But vagueness
and

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
and similarity characterize the program of the two
major parties. There is, it is true, a sertain dis-
tinotion in the popular mind between the two parties.
The Minseito has stood for a passive, or conserrative
policy, undertaking to solve the nation's diffieulties
by strict economy and a program of retrenohment. To
this end it has enforced and supported the free export of
gold, and had advocated increased taxation and avoidance
of loans to balance the national budget.
On the other hand, the Seiyukai declared that a
policy of inflation was necessary to save the nation. It
stood for the embargo on gold and declared that instead
of resorting to taxation, it would meet obligations by
borrowing. In general the Seiyukai stands, in the publio
mind, for a positive or active polioy. It has, it is
true, set forth a program of ten fundamental policies,
which include a five-year industrial program, proteotion
of domestio industry by increased oustoms duties, educational
reforms, rationalization of industry, reduction of taxes
and increases in salaries of oivil servants, et cetera.
However, very little attention has been paid to this pro-
gram by the publio or the press, it being fully realized
that these measures represent aspirations rather than
policies, for which there is little expectation of
realization.
These policies featured but little in the elections.
There was much heard of slogans of prosperity and depression,
and a great deal about personalities, but neither party
had anything definite to offer in the way of conorete
programs

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)

programs. The victory of the Seiyukai, overwhelming as it
was, may be attributed to a variety of factors, nome of
extraordinary significance, whieh I shall endeavor to outlins
here below .
1. The Minseito has found itself growing steadily
weaker during the past year, due to an extraordinary series
of unfortunate events, beginning with the assault on the
Party's stalwart leader and President, Mr. Hamaguehi. Mr.
Hamaguchi's resignation and subsequent death was the odcasion
for a centrifugal movement among the lesser leaders of the
Party. General Ugaki ( who although not a a rty man was
considered a potential president of the Party) resigned from
the Cabinet. Baron Wakatsuki, who was recalled from retire-
ment to head the Party in the hope of uniting the contending
factions, has proved to be a disappointing leader, lacking
in qualities of leadership and political acumen. He was
unable to prevent friction among his lieutenants, which
friction led to the sohism of Adachi and the loss of that
master election engineer to the Party.
With the downfall of the Minseito Cabinet in December,
the Party had on its roll names of only three persons of out-
standing personality: Dr. Egi, former Minister of Railways,
Baron Shidehara, and Mr. Inouye, former Minister of Finance.
Dr. Egi has been seriously ill with oancer; Baron Shidehara
has been ill and unable to take part in the campaign; and
Mr. Inouye met his death at the hand of a political assassin
a few days before the eleotion. The death of Mr. Inouye, who
had been appointed campaign manager for the Minseito, seemed
almost to be the last straw on the overburdened Party, not
only because of the direet loss to the Party, but alse because
the

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)

In consequence of these events, the Minseito faced

loss of publio confidence. On the other hand, the Seiyukai has,
since its decisive defeat in the general eleations of l930,
built up a strong internal organization, eliminating much
of the undesirable element which brought on the scandals
and mismanagement of the Tanaka Ministry; have appointed
as leader of the Party Mr. Inukai, probably the most astute
politician in Japan and a man of relatively high integrity.
Among the party leaders are men who,if not in the first
rank of statesmen, are of acknowledged ability; notably
Mr. Takahashi, the present Minister of Finanoe, and Dr.
Suzuki, present Minister of Justioe. The public could
not but be impressed with the superiority of the leaders
of the Seiyukai to those of the Minseito.
2. The Seiyukai, being the Government Party, had a
decided advantage in t he elections. The Embassy has fre-
quently referred to the axiom in Japan that the Government
Party always wins in elections. This is explained by the
faet that elections are held throughout the country under
the supervision of the police, who are responsible to the
prefemtural governors, and they in turn to the Minister of
Home Affairs. The Embassy reported some time ago that
there had been an extensive change among the refectural
governors and police ehiefs following the change of
goverments in December. The poliee are able, as ad-
ministrators of the very striet elections laws, to in-
fluence voting by proseouting or overlooking breaches
of the regulations. There have beem, however, fewer com-

has suffered considerable diminution of his reputation.
However favorably his conciliatory polioies may be re-

oredited in Japan at present. The Army is in the saddle,

A point, which may be of speoulative interest, is
the effect of the hostilities in China on the result of

As I had the honor to report in my telegram, the

Contributing to this apathetic attitude on the part
of the public is a feeling, widely commented on, that
the representative system of government has proved to
be a failure. This feeling has grown out of disgust at
the venality of the politicians, disillusionment at the
freedom with which the parties break promises and pledges,
impatience with the lack of final authority of the Govern-
ment under the provisions of the constitution, and dis-
appointment at the parties for their failure to present
intelligent programs of administration or to give the
nation good government. In this connection I quote from
an editorial article in the OSAKA MAINICHI, a powerful
and conservative newspaper: " The prestige of parliamentarism
is fast declining consequent upon the low political morality
of the leading parties. There are people who are apprehensive
lest Fascism may come into existence in Japan. We do not wish
to believe that the conditions in our country are so bad
as to require this drastic operation. Nevertheless, we cannot
be blind to the fact that unless the dignity of parliamentarism
is recovmred without delay, the crisis may come". Indications
are not lacking that such movements have be en planned.
Prior to the day of the elections, each voter was pro-
vided with a certificate of eligibility, which was exohanged
for a ballot at the voting places on election day. The voter
was required to inscribe on the ballot the name of his
candidate, and to deposit the ballot in a box. Aocording
to offioial reports, a total of 9,706,431 votes wes cast.
A considerable number of these votes were invalid because
cast for parsons not candidates for elections. It is said
that George.Washington neceived a number of votes in Tokyo.
The total number of registered voters was 15,257,841, but

That the present paroentage of voters should remain
so relatively high, after the novelty of voting had worn
off in the two previous elections, is a source of some
gratification to students of parliamentarism in Japan.
The high vote cast may in part be accounted for by the
provision for the election of several members of the Diet
from eaoh of the l22 electoral districts- the number varying
with the population of the districts. Thus each district
nas its own competitive battle between a number of candi-
dates, which tends to create a more direct interest in the
campaign on the part of the voters than might exist in a
nationwide competition.
Of the total of 9,706,431 votes cast, the Seiyuka1
received 5,639,311 votes and the Minseito 3,397,238. Since
they obtained 304 and 147 seats respectively, it will be
found that every Seiyukai member represents 18,728 voters
and that every Minseito member represents 23,llo voters.
A fact which is ommonly pointed to as evidence of the
growing intelligence of the Japanese eleotorate, is the
gradual elimination of the independent and small party members.
In the past the small party members have contributed little to
parliamentary progress, and bave been mainly useful by lending

The results show quite clearly the trend toward the
two-party system in Japan, a trend which seems to have been
emphasized by the establishment of manhood suffrage. There
is an historical background for the two-party system in
Japan. Since early in the Meiji era there have existed
two groups of politicians, even though the clan bureaucraey
interfered with the normal working of a two-party system.
The Jiyuto was the predecessor of the Seiyukaf, and the
Kaishinto of the Minseito, whose respective histories
extend back fifty years.
Of the smaller groups, the Adachi faction polled
145,667, the neutrals 176,739, and the proletarians
256,629. Thus each Adachi member pepresents 29,133 voters,
each proletarian member represents 5l,325 voters, and each
independent member represents 58,913 voters.
These figures indicate the necessity of organization
for success in t he eleotions. The Seiyukai, with its
excellent politioal machine, was able to distribute its
candidates in smh manner as to obtain the largest return
for the lowest proportional number of votes. At the other
extreme are the independents and proletarians, who lacking
effective organization wasted a goodly proportion of the
votes cast for them. The relatively efficiems dispesition
of

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.99)
-13-
of votes for the Adachi faction is accounted for
by the limited number of candidates under that head
and the prestige which the name of the former Minseite
chieftain still carries.
The showing of the proletarian candidates in t he
election was particularly disappointing. Only five
were succdssful from among the 29 candidates. The
number elected is the same as in the previous election,
but the total number of votes cast for proletarians
was 291,140; a 1oss of 227,000 from the 1930 total.
In the first general eleotion under manhood suffrage
the proletarian placed seven members in the Diet and
their prospects for the future appeared bright. Ap-
parently they have not justified the early expectations,
and have continued to lose influence and favor among
the public. Many thinking Japanese regard the pro-
letarian failure with apprehension. Given repre-
sentation in t he Diet it was hoped that the proletarians
would direot their activities into constructive measures
and prevent the growth of dangerous radicalism.
The chief reasoh for their defeat was apparently
disunity. Cooperation and organization among the various
factions seems impossible of accomplishment. In addition
the failure of the proletarian representatives in the Diet
to bring about passage of any legislation benfiting the
working glasses has caused the people of those classes
to turn from them in disoouragement. The only proletarian
member of any prominence elected is Dr. Isoo Abe, formerly
of Waseda University. Bunji Suzuki and Ikuo Oyama, pro-
minent proletarian leaders, were both defeated.
The

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
The Minseito lost many prominent members in the
election. Among these unsuccessful candidates are
Mr. Hara, former Minister of Railways; Mr. Nakamura,
former President of the House of Representatives; and
Mr. Kobashi, former Minister of Education.
There is nothing in the Japanese election law to
prevent ministers of state from running for a seat in
the Diet. In fact the practioe is almost universal,
excapt among the Ministers of War and Navy, who are
not members of political parties. In this eleotion all
members of the Cabinet who ran for office were successful.
The Premier, ineidentally, is the oldest member of the
Lower House.
Of the three independents elected, one was a Korean,
Mr. Boku Shin Kin. His candidaoy in Tokyo aroused much
interest, insomuoh as he announoed that his object in standing
was to demonstrate the loyalty of Koreans to the Empe ror, and
to vindicate the reputation of Koreans injured in general
by the Sakurada Mon Affair, in which a Korean made an
attempt on the life of the Emperor. He is the first Korean
to be eleoted to the Diet. Another independent returned to
the Diet is Yukio Ozaki, the veteran politioian now abroad.
Respeotfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Edwin L. Neville,
mbassy's File No. 800.Japan.
Counselor of Embassy.

101, March 17, 5 p.m.
There is ecidedly less political tension, from the
international point of viev, since the annauncement of
the prospective withdraval of contingents from Shanghai.
In Manchuria there are continued reporte of banditry and
disturbance. These reports are not given as muoh promi-
nence in the press as was formerly the case and there seems
to be a much oalmer general atmosphere in regard to the
China gituation.
The domestic political situation seems far from
settled. The Home Minister has resigned and the Prime
Minister has taken the duties of that office in addition
to his owm. Rumors continue that there will be a Cabinet
change after the close of the Diet. It is too early to
predict anything definite as to personnel. Prinoe Saionji
has been spending muoh time in Tokyo receiving visits from
different

=== Page 373 === (confidence 0.99)
MET
2-#101 from Tokio, Maroh 17
different political leaders. This has given rise to
great deal of speculation but no definite announoomen
has been made.
The Diet will meet in extraordinary session tomorrow
It has been called for the purpose of passing the speoil
budgct necessary to take care of the Shanghai and Manohuri
military expecitions. The Privy Council insisted that fur-
ther financial arrangements for military expendituro be
made by the Diet and refused to accept the Government!s
contention that they were in the nature of emergenoy bills
which the Government had power to aot upon and refer to
the Diet later. Indications are that the amount asked for
by the Government will be in the noighborhood of 83,000,000
yen. It is anticipated that the Diet will remain in session
for only a few days, that there will be no opposition to
the Government proposals, that adjournmont vill be taken
on the 22nd om 23sd of March.

The Secretary of State,
VILLD CONFIDENTIAL FILE
Washington.
Dear Mr. Secretary,
There is a great deal of underourrent of things
going on, that I don't seem to be able to cable and yet
feel I should report, that will make rather interesting
but, I fear, rather unpleasant reading.
The day before yesterday at eleven thirty in the
morning as Baron Dan stepped from his automobile in front
of the Mitsui Building, a young man idling in the vieinity
894.44-
Banon Da
whipped out a pistol and shot him; and he died within an
hour; the bullet, entering the right side, passed through
his body and lodged in a rib somewhere near the heart. He
pitohed forward practically unconscious and did not regain
consciousness.

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8-
consoiousness. As I have written you before, he seemed
like a very sick man; he was seventy-four years of age
and was complaining that he was constantly losing weight
and suffering from poor digestion. His assailant was a
young man who came from the same vicinity as the young man
who shot Inouye and he used the same kind of automatio
pistol. Rumor has it that these pistols were obtained
from some army or navy officer; it is of course olaimed
that they were stolen. They now talk of a death gang
said to be composed of five young men who have entered
into a pact to die, each killing one great figure.
It
is noteworthy that these are the great conservative leaders;
rumor has it that the other three marked to go are: Baron
Shidehara, Count Makino and Baron and former Prime Minister
Wakatsuki. Baron Dan had received several threatening
letters and had a personal guard with him who disarmed and
overpowered on the spot the young man who did the shooting.
An interesting side-light on the public attitude toward
Baron Dan appeared in the paper to-day. It said that the
people in the little town from which the murderer hailed
have compelled his family to move out of town; and the
wife of his brother is applying for a divoroe. The
Emperor conferred posthumous honors upon the Baron.
papers are full of attacks upon the police for lack of
activity, as existence of this death band had been known.
They are charged with negligenee in failing to run down
the members of the band. I don't doubt but that they are
doubling their aotivities now.
The wildest rumors race about town about Baron
Shidehara. Baron Bassompierre tells me that the servants
of his household and those of the Swiss Legation are cer-
tan

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
tain that the Baron is dead and that his death is being
concealed from the public. Bishop Reifsnider told me
yesterday that according to a rumor that has reaohed him
Baron Shidehara was attacked by a high-up naval offioer
who had immediately prooeeded to commit hara-kiri in the
former's presence. Doctor Teusler tells me this morning
that he understands the Baron has had a hemorrhage of the
brain but that he is recovering.
The most commonly heard
version is, however, that he has had a heart attack.
In
any case he is in hiding and nobody seems to know where
he is.
Fleisher adds that he is one hundred per cent.
certain that Shidehara is alive and states that he is in
the hospital.
. The paper, on the 7th, announced that he
is at home, which probably is a ruse to put people off the
track, but that he is still unable' to receive visitors.
While I was dictating this letter Mr. Fleisher was
announced and he informed me that while at his office a
little while ago a group of six policemen arrived and in-
formed him that a mob of Ronin (patriotio fanatios) were
on their way to mob the Advertiser. He had immediately
gone to the Foreign Ofrice where by ohance was Baron Harada,
894.911
Private Secretary to Prince Saionji, who heard the story
and ws greatly disturbed. Mr. Fleisher saw Shiratori,
who is the evil genius of the place that they don't seem
able to get rid of, who launohed forth into a diatribe against
all things American, declaring that Japan was the only
nation that had any sanity, and that the United States
had gone mad with anti-Japanese feeling.
The Advertiser
had printed some piotures from China, one of which was a
picture of arms captured from the Japanese by the Chinese;
this has given offence to the Japanese people. There is a
little

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
little paper called the Nippon which is read by the
violent element and the insurgents, and tends to inflame
them; and it came out with a violent attack upon the
Advertiser the morning of the 8th.
Fleisher, after
seeing Shiratori, came right around to see me, but I
told him he was making a mistake: he should have gone
to Yoshizawa because he is a man with enough vision to
know the international disadvantage to Japan in any aot
of violence against the Advertiser. It is, of course,
also true that in the present excitable state of mind of
the populace, fanned by the newspapers emphasizing our
protests and holding them forth as expressions of hostil-
ity to Japan, that there is actual personal danger to the
homes and families of both the Fleishers, father and son;
they are asking for police protection for their homes,
which should be given. I am asking MoIlroy to represent
to the army the disadvantages to them and to the cause of
Japan of any attack on this very moderate paper, which has
been steering a very difficult course between giving the
news on the one hand and not offending Japan or violating
the prohibitions contained in the government bans on the
other.
I think it well to send along all these side-lights
upon the extremes to which the war frenzy is leading the
Japanese people, as evidence in support of the position I
took in sending my recent telegrams urging great care in
putting out further material tending to inflame publie
opinion here.
Late on the night of the lzth the feeling was that
possibly the police warning to the Advertiser was a hoax
whioh

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
which they were trying to run down, and possibly intended
as a warning to the paper so as to give them the idea of
what might happen to them. I told Mr. Fleisher that if
he had any reason to think he was in danger that he and
Mrs. Fleisher could come and make a little visit at the
Embassy. He sent word back that he did not antioipate
any personal danger.
Dootor Nitobe made a speech a little while ago in
which he said that one of the great menaces to Japan was
military domination. He has received a number of
894.20
threatening letters and has now taken up his abode in
Saint Luke's Hospital where the federal government has
detailed a couple of policemen to guard him. A day or
two ago he was compelled by five military officers to
leave the hospital and appear before some military associ-
ation, which demanded an apology from him for his remarks.
He made some explanationThe fact of this performance was
published in the Advertiser the following day but without
oomment.
If Fleisher had commented sharply on it he
might have been subjected to violence and possibly his
paper wreoked. The morning of the 7th I called on Doctor
Nitobe and had a talk with him. He said frankly that his
life was in danger, that he had no doubt they were laying
for him, and that he had been requested by what he expressed
as "very high authority" to lay low and not expose himself
in publio and to wait for better times. Also that he had
been assured that a change would take place either at the
end 'of this year or the beginning of next, at which time
his influence would be needed. What this means has to
be left to conjeoture. Whether it comes from the Throne,
or near the Throne, the Imperial Household, Prinoe Saionji,
or

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
or merely from the leaders of the Minseito Party is also
a matter of conjecture. There are good reasons for not
mentioning names because it is apt to put whoever makes
them in actual personal danger as things are going to-day.
Prince Saionji has just moved to Tokyo; he is unap-
proached and unapproachable by foreigners. Baron Bassompierre,
who has been here eleven years has met him just once: he
prefers to sit like a god on high Olympus and direct things
in that way. He meets nobody except his trusted advisers.
The police are now (March l0) much more active.
Yesterday, it is said, fifteen thousand of them were busy
rounding up the criminal element and tracing every clue
leading to the recent murders of great men.
They know now that the revolvers with which the orimes
were committed were purchased by a naval officer, named
Lieutenant Fujii, in Dairen; he was killed in an aerial
battle in Shanghai, being one of the first men killed.
Query: Was it not his own way of eliminating himself?
He purohased eight revolvers.
Another member of the death band has come in and con-
fessed and his talking is implicating others. The police
have numerous suspeots.
Baron Bassompierre has developed the habit of dropping
in some time during the day at the Embassy and chatting
quite freely. He gave me an interesting bit of gossip
yesterday to the effeot that Prince Saionji is moving
actively in the matter of a national government and is en-
countering a good deal of opposition on the part of the
Seiyukai and possibly the military clique. He has been
conferring with Inukai, the Premier, and with Araki, the
Minister

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.98)
一 7 -
Minister of War, yesterday, and to-day has a session with
Yoshizawa, the Minister for Foreign Affairs. Aocording
to Baron Bassompierre, this change will be towards curbing
the military and the establishment of a more conservative
polioy, if it goes through.
.This would naturally be op-
posed by the army. And I imagine Prince Saionji is too
astute to take any chance of trying to put anything across
unless he is perfectly able to carry it through.
seems to me much more likely that the information I re-
ceived from Doctor Nitobe is the more correct: that the
change will come toward the end of the year or the beginn-
ing of next, rather than immediately. This would give
time for the soldiers to get back from active operations
in the field, reaction from the war frenzy to set in, and
the resentment caused by the repeated representations from
the ambassadors of foreign powers to have quieted down a little
bit. I have indicated in my telegrams a number of times
the position that Baron Dan took in regard to this. He said
to me repeatedly that: "If your Government can only keep
quiet for a little while, the conservative elements here
will have a chance. We haven't any while these measures
are being taken, and which tend to inflame the publio mind."
When I saw him last - two days before his death - he seemed
very happy over the faot that it had apparently been given
out from Washington that no more protests would be made.
He took my hand in both of his and shook it enthusiastioally
and repeatedly. He said: "I think things are going to
olear up now."
He was very aotive in going about attend-
ing luncheons, and dinners, and met the members of the
League

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
League of Nations Commission at various functions.
Yesterday a very vigorous full-page advertisement came
out with big headlines the whole way across the page to the
effeot that:
"The United States Should Cooperate with Japam.
Japan Treads the Path of Righteousness." And under it: ▲
Change of Viewpoint by Americans is Ardent Hope of Japanese
People." The whole statement is in big type and in four-
teen places emphasis is placed on phrases by putting them
in heavier black type. It begins: "Japan will audaciously
walk the path of what she thinks right, irrespective of what
outside pressure should be brought upon her.* It goes on
to criticize the foreign ministers and prime ministers for
expressing views highly unworthy of this great empire.
It
is published as a statement by the Shiunso. The Japan
Times is supposed to be subsidized by the army just now;
and it is noteworthy that on two or three recent occasions
they have omitted my name in mentioning funotions that I
attended. For example, the audienoe that the Emperor gave
to Theodore Roosevelt and the luncheon that was given to the
guests, whose names were all given, but mine, - whioh was,
obviously, an intentional slight. Also, they have not men-
tioned my calling at the house and attending the funeral of
Baron Dan. Although the Advertiser, which appears in the
morning, gave prominenoe to these faots, the Japan Times,
which oomes out in the evening ignored them, evidently
purposely. I merely mention these facts as straws indicating
which way the wind is blowing in that vioinity.
Yesterday (Maroh leth), Mr. Fleisher, Sr., oalled again
in the afternoon. He is in a very disturbed frame of mind.
His paper is losing money; he has not had the support from
Japanese sourees that he had hoped for; and the bans prevemt
h1s

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.96)
- 9 -
his publishing the news. He is not in sympathy with the
polioy of the Government and cannot support it: if he op-
posed it, his paper would forthwith be wreoked. He looks
forward to extremely black times, so much so that if I took
him seriously I should make a grave error in leaving at the
present time and until just before Grew's arrival. He
thinks things are going from bad to worse. He is now con-
vinced, in spite of all denials, that Baron Shidehara was
the victim of a murderous attack but that they did not
succeed in killing him.
▲ young man endeavored to get near Inukai, the Premier,
and took poison when prevented.
Fleisher also says there has been an attempt made on
Wakatsuki, the former Premier.
On Maroh loth I had a meeting of the whole staff and
outlined the course of events of the last few days since the
last meeting and indicated the nature of the telegrams I had
sent, and the attitude of the ministers and diplomatic corps
to the effeot that no more outside pressure should be brought
to bear on Japan. It was the unanimous sense of those
present that this was the correct policy to pursue. One of
those present, a careful and competent observer, remarked
that the continuafion of the policy of sending vigorously
phrased protests would lead straight to war.
Respectfully yours,
WCF/AA
W. Cameron Forbes

CONFIDENTIAL FOR THE SECRETARY.
One. General cCoy informed me yesterday that League
Conmission leaves this morning for Nanking where they will
remain four days seeing Government leaders. They will then
proceed to Peipin perhaps via Hankow where they will spend
a few days prior to visiting Manchuria. After visiting
every part of Lanchuria reachable by present means of trans-
portation the Commission then proposes to visit Japan where
they will see leaders once more, pausing in Korea to collect
information. They will then return to China to sound leaders

September. (END GRAY)

=== Page 404 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclosure No. /
The Address of Mr. Yoshizawa, Minister for
row
.caszy
Tjo.
Foreign Affairs, at the 6lst Session
of the Imperial Diet.
March 22nd, 1932.
At the last session of the Diet I had oocasion to
discuss the more important of the foreign questions
confronting this country. To-day I have the pleasure
of reporting, and of stating my views, on the various
phases that have since developed.
As regards the Anti-Japanese movement in China Proper,
the Japanese Government seized every opportunity to urge
upon the Chinese authorities the thorough-going suppression
of the same. However, China failed to respond, and the
movement continued with increasing intensity and persistence.
In Shanghai. where the agitation was conducted with especial
vigor, the Minkuo Jihpao, a vernacular journal of that city,
published on Je mary 9th an article highly abusive of our
Imperial House, and on the 18th of the same month Japanese
his
priest/ and thoir companions were murdered or wounded by
a Chinese mob. The two incidents were sufficient to inflame
the Japanese residents, who had long endured the ceaseless
Anti-Japanese movements and whose patience had been well-nigh
exhausted under the trying conditions of more recent months,
The situation thus came to assume a grave aspect.
The

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The Japanese Consul-General at Shanghai, acting under
the instructions of the Government, presented to the Mayor
of Shanghai strong protests regarding these two incidents,
and at the same time made various demands for the settlement
of the affairs. The Mayor complied without much demur with
our demands relating to the first incident, and after some
delay and much conversation, in the end accepted our terms
regarding the second. On receipt of the Mayor's reply, at
3 o'clock on the 28th, the Japanese authorities, anticipating
that the situation would improve. adopted an attitude of
watchfully awaiting the fulfilment of their promise on the
part of the Chinese. In the meantime, the 19th Route Army.
then concetrated in the vicinity of Shanghai, having assumed
a disquieting attitude and making a display of warlike
preparations, the Shanghai Municipal Council haa decided
to take due precautions against the possibility of
disturbances being created within the Settlement by
undisciplined Chinese soldiers, or by mobs and radical
elements. Accordingly, the Council, meeting on the 28th.
declared a state of siege, to commence that day at 4
o'clock in the afternoon. As a result, the garrisons
of the Powers were assigned to duty at their respective
sectors in accordance with a pre-arranged plan of common
defense. It was while the Japanese marines were proceeding
to take up their post in the Chapei district, their allotted
sector, that the Chinese fired upon our forces, and compelled
them

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
them to take measures of defense. Reports are sometimes
circulated making it appear that it was the Japanese marines
who commenced the fighting. in spite of the Chinese acceptance
of our terms of settlement. But the facts of the case are
precisely as I have just stated.
Moreover, we exerted every effort to prevent eny
aggravation of the situation, and upon two occasions we
entered into agreements with the Chinese authorities for a
truce. But each time the agreement vas rendered nugatory
by the action of the Chinese army. Our demand, presented
on the 19th of February, that the Chinese army should withdraw
, to a certain distance, was rejected by the Chinese.  Thereupon,
on the 20th, our troops which had been dispatched as a rein-
forcement, acting in concert with the navy, undertook to
compel the withdra al of the Chinese forces outside the
prescribed area, and completed the task on March 3rd.
As soon as the Shanghai Incident occurred. the Chinese
Government proposed to the Council of the League of Nations
to make application of Article 15 of the League Covenant
to the Sino-Japanese conflict, and later, upon the request
of China, the case was transferred to the Assembiy of the
League. The Japanese Government took the view that the
Shanghai Incident was a merely local affair which was not
of a nature likely to lead to a rupture, while as regards the
Manchurian Incident, not only had there been no renewal
of

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
of hostilities, but the matter was already pending in the
hands of the Council under Article 1l of the Covenant and,
moreover, the commission of inquiry had indeed left for the
purpose of conducting investigations on the spot into the
actual conditions prevailing in China; there was therefore
no legitimate ground for applying Article 15 to either of
these cases. It was vith an unequivocal reservation based
on that view that our government participated in the delibera-
tions of the Council and the Assembly. However, the Assembly
passed a resolution on March llth, which was in more than
one respect unacceptable to our government. On account of
this unacceptability, our representatives under the instruc-
tions of the Governnent, expounded the Japanese position,
made a reservation
doclared  protest embodying our contention with regard to
the application of Article 15 of the Covenant, and abstained
from voting.
In Manchuria, by reason of the special position it
occupies in relation to China Proper, and the inveterate
antagonism of the Manchurian people towards the militarist
rule to which they had been subjected, it appears that
subsequently to the downfall of the erstwhile Manchurian
authorities lest autumn, the efforts of the local leaders
for the maintenance of peace and order in their respective
districts graduelly assumed the form of a movement for
autonomy. A decl ration of independence was made a short
time

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.98)
一5-
time ago by former provincial governors and other Manchurian
leaders. Our government received a communication under date
of March 12th from the Minister for Foreign Affairs of the
new government, announcing the establishment of a new
independent State of Manchuria. As regards this communication,
we have gone no further than unofficially to acknowledge its
receipt. I am certain, however, that it will be in accordance
With the best interests of the new state if its government
will, as the announcement says will be the case, really
respect existing treaties and the rights and interests of
foreigners, and faithfully adhere to the principle of the
Open Door.
As regards the various questions pending between Jepan
and the Soviet Union in connection with the fisheries in
northern waters, the negotiations assiduously conducted at
Moscow since ths ne of last year have so far failed to bear
fruit.
From the broad stand-point of friendly relations
between the two countries it is most desirable that we should
arrive at & fair and suitable settlement. In fact, the
difference in the vievs of the two governments concerning
the basis of settlement is gradually narrowing. It is the
intention of this Government to continue their efforts towards
carrying our points and so to safeguard the legitimate rights
and interests secured to us by treaty
I have now given an outline of the lεtest developments
in

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
in the foreign affairs of Jap:n. While the interests of
this country in Manchuria are certainly of paramount impor-
tance from & political view-point, those in China Proper are
in the main economic rather than political. Consequently, if
the Nanking Government and the Nationalist Party abandon
. their Anti-Japanese policy and direct their energies towards
achieving internal unity and peace, and economic prosperity
and progrcss, there is no room for doubt as to the possibility
of restoring complete friendship and concord between the two
countries. I may add on this occasion a word about the
atmosphere of Europe and America, which has not always been
sympathetie. to Japan since the outbreak of the Manchurian
Incident, and especially since the Shanghai Affair. That
atmosphere was created simply through misrepresentations of
facts emanatins from Chinese sources or through misunder-
standings of various kinds. I am confident that in time
the sentiment of the Western Powers will turn gradually in
favor of Japan.

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
53
C-OF
