Sir:
Referring to the Embassy's despatoh No. 5l2 of Febru-
ary 27, l932, I have the honor to report that attention
has been further directed towards reactionism in Japan as
a result of disclosures whioh followed the assassination on
March 5th of Baron Dan, Managing Director of Mitsui, and of
frank interpellations during the extraordinary sessfon
1
the Diet that ended Mareh 24th.
According to reports in the press the assassifof
Baron

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Baron, Dan, as well as the assassin of Dr. Inouye, Minister
of Finance, who was murdered on February 9th, were members
894.44
of a "blood brotherhood league" which had twenty mombers
and which intended to murder eight other prominent Japanese.
1/.
(Details are given in Enclosure No. 1.) Fourteen members
of this group have been indicted for murder, including
the alleged leader, a priest named Nissho Inouye.
The
press states that "it is reported that the late Lieutenant-
Commander Fujii of the Imperial Navy (killed early in the
recent action at Shanghai) supplied eight revolvers to the
blood brotherhood and that a certain military officer in
active service supplied fifteen revolvers."
That the organization of assassins was on a larger
scale than the press reports indicate I have heard from
a reliable Japanese source in direct contaot with the
Metropolitan Police Board. According to this informant
the police discovered seven groups* of approrimately
twenty members each, all under the direction of the
priest, each group maturing plans for similar direct
action, all in possession of revolvers that were sup-
plied by naval and army officers - the revolvers having
been brought from China by airplane. I was further
told that the membership included intellectuals, univer-
sity students, and even lower officials of the Govern-
ment, and that all the members have been or are in
oustody for investigation.
The most
* The first group was organized on
April 10, 1931.

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3 -
The most interesting point - but one on which
definite information is as yet unobtainable - is the
possible connection of the priest and these seven groups
(which appear to be a perversion of the communist cell
system) with persons of importance. The informant men-
tioned above stated that a number of culprits took refuge
in the house of Mitsuru Toyama, entry of the police being
prevented for some days by super-patriots armed with
swords. Mitsuru Toyama founded and has been the leading
spirit of the Kokuryukai (Black Dragon Society) the
membership of which includes General Araki, Minister of
War, Admiral Kanji Kato, former Chief of the Naval General
Staff, and (retired) Admiral Kamiizumi, advocate of a
stronger navy.
.The press reported that some of the of-
fenders were discovered hiding in a house rented but not
occupied by a Mr. Gondo, who in the past has taught
military men a philosophy which is a mixture of the prin-
ciples of Bushido, Shintoism, and Chinese classics.
•In
the past among these pupils were General Araki and Lieuten-
ant-Commander Fujii, mentioned in connection with the sup-
ply of revolvers. The priest, Nissho Inouye, frequented
Gondo's house until shortly before his arrest, at which
time he was in hiding in the residence of a junior naval
officer.
All this has aroused widespread suspicion that men
of standing were implicated in the assassination conspiracy.
This is indicated by a statement reported to have been made
by the Public Procurator, apparently for the purpose of
allaying such doubts. He is quoted as having said that
"the

=== Page 414 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
"the judicial authorities have disclosed the fact that
Nissho Inouye (the priest) is the only leader and instiga-
tor of the members' of the Blood Brotherhood League" and
that "no other persons of great influence outside the
League are implicated in the movement of this body".
I
am unable to throw more light on this point at present.
If there are others of importance culpable the reason for
refraining from taking measures to punish them are very
probably similar to those that prevented police action
after the discovery of the coup d'état plot of last
October, namely, the danger of organized resistance.
The effect of the police disclosures created wide-
spread uneasiness, which was reflected in certain speeches
made in the recent extraordinary session of the Diet, al-
though certain allowance must be made for utterances by
members of the Opposition. In the Lower House a repre-
sentative said on March 24th:
"of late the capital is
almost in chaos; social unrest is very conspicuous, al-
most unprecedented. . . Sensational rumors are every-
where.
It is surprising to find that bullet-proof
vests are selling well and that one was presented to
Premier Inukai. . . In Japan to-day there are two
dangerous undercurrents, namely, communism and Fascism.
I might say that Fascism has for its object the destruotion
8
of parliamentary government and capitalism. In faot the
8
Fascists are looking for an opportunity to alter the
national organization of Japan." In reply the Prime
Minister said, in part: "It is true that the movements
of Fascists are progressing remarkably beneath the surface."
In the House of Peers a member stated on the same day:
"The

=== Page 415 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5 -
"The police have arrested accomplices of the two assassins.
Among them are several university students. It seems that
military men have had connection with various groups of a
dangerous character. . . Through the medium of magazines
and other publications these dangerous bodies have been
propagating Fascism.
. . The Blood Brotherhood League
is merely one of such dangerous bodies."
The uneasiness that was prevalent during March, however,
has noticeably decreased during the past week or two.
There
are fewer rumors of a disturbing nature in circulation.
It is recognized that the reactionary groups continue to
be a potential danger, particularly if a section of the
military were to express its dissatisfaction with political
affairs by direct action. At the moment any suoh action
is not regarded as imminent. This aspect of the subject,
however, will be discussed in a despatch now in preparation
that deals with the growth, organization, and aims of the
reactionary societies.
Respectfully yours,

Current Thought in Japan (Kyushu).
The Consul at Nagasaki reports that in
spite of the high patriotic fever prevalent
in Kyushu, whioh is stimulated by triumphal
celebrations of returning troops, there is
an element in the population that is not so
pleased with developments. Manchurian com-
potition is feared for one thing, particu-
larly in coal, which is the main industry
of the island. This disoontented element,
however, is afraid of public opinion, which
might regard it as unpatriotic and even
Communist, and so remains silent. One
newspaper artiole whioh is quoted, however,
indioates that this element is beginning to
break its silenoe. The newspaper, FUKUOKA
NICHI NICHI, has a good reputation and is
read all over Japan.
The substance of the article is that
while the "Come Baok to Asia" (Asiatic
Monroe Doctrine or mainland expansion
policy) slogan, which is gaining in
popularity, has its good points, world
opinion and relations with other countrios
are also important. Diplomacy is not a
game of solitaire, and while it is right
that Japan should be master in Asia, it
must be remembered that every master has a
family and that he should treat his family
with kindness and consideration.

TOKYO

May 10, 1932.
This excellent report on the
reactionary movement in Japan (Tokyo
Embassy's No. 588, April 22, 1932)
is well worth the study of the Depart-
ment on account of the good idea it
conveys of the great weight and preva-
lence of extreme nationalism in Japan.
It is stated that reactionism in Japan
started as early as 1888, and that it
has received impetus with every effort
of western nations to curb Japanese
expansion and with every advance of
western culture on Japanese life. The
greatest development of reactionary
societies has occurred since 19l8, but
it is only since the Manchurian adven-
ture that they have become of nation-
wide significance. Mr. Salisbury has
been able to compile a list of 124
societies, of which 82 were founded
since 1918. It is stated that one
reactionary leader reported that there
are 300 reactionary societies of a
political character, with a total
membership of 300,000, but that others
estimatethe total membership as 600,000.
The control of Manchuria and Mongolia
has long been the ambition of a part of
these sooieties.
The

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
The importance of these societies
has been enhanced since last September
by the assistanoe which they have been
able to render the military through
propaganda and, silencing  opposi-
tion. Their significance lies not in
any aotion which they might initiate
but in their possible support b other
groups in action relating to internal
affairs. The inclusion of many
prominent leaders of sound political
views somewhat lessens the likelihood
of radical activity, however.
Until the Manchurian affair the
fundamental object of reactionary
societies in Japan has been the preserva-
tion of the peculiar national polity and
characteristics of Japan, but since
September 18 the principle that Japanese
control of Manchuria and Mongolia is
necessary for the preservation of Japan
as a great power has welded these
societies together and been added to
their aims. Ryohei Uchida, perhaps the
most prominent reactionary leader,
recently made a statement that indicates
the type of mind of reactionaries in
Japan. He said that Manchuria has never
belonged to China and that the Foreign
Office, industrialists and newspapers
and magazines are not really Japanese
and do not understand Japan.
At

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
At present two views exist regard-
ing extreme political movements that
would receive the support of reaction-
ary societies. One is the formation of
a super-party cabinet that would back
the military and the other is the taking
over of the Government by the military.
A proletarian leader expressed the
opinion that the military would take
control of the Government when the
Japanese capitalists refused to lend
more money for the Manchurian enterprise.
Either movement would probably be
assisted by other organizations of
reactionary tendencies such as the
Imperial Reservists Association, 3,O00,000
membership, Young Men's Associations,
2,577,000 members, Young Men's Training
Institutes, 1,O00,000 members, Young
Women's Associations, 1,616,O00 members
and the three National Defense Associa-
tions.
Reactionary societies disclaim the
word "Fascism" because opinion on the
abolition of parliamentary government is
divided. Rather their new spirit is a
product peculiar to Bushido and does not
owe anything to foreign influence. Labor
is aligning itself with reaction.

894.00 1382 (C.F)
Supplementing my despatch No. 561 of April 7, 1932,
regarding reactionism in Japan, I have the honor to comment
on the development and present position of reactionary
organizations, including reactionary societies, reactionary
proletarian groups, and organizations with reaotionary
tendencies.
Interested Japanese agree that the reactionary movement
of today began with the publication of a magazine called
NIPPON JIN (Nippon Man) in April, 1888, and of a daily paper
called NIPPoN the following year, the first number of the
latter being published on the day the Constitution was pro-
mulgated.

=== Page 428 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
mulgated. Two contributors to those publications are
today the leading figures among reactionary societies;
namely, Mitsuru Toyama and Ryohei Uchida. Although the
modern movement is recognized as having begun at that
time, the spirit prompting these reactionaries had long
existed: the leaders today can justly be regarded as suc-
cessors to the ronin of feudal times, both claiming loyalty.
as motivation. The immediate reason for the birth of the
modern movement was reaction against an enthusiasm for
Europeanization that was sweeping Japan at that time, an
enthusiasm illustrated by the advocacy by a Minister of
Education (Viscount Arinori Mori) of adoption of English
as a secondary language.
Since the inception of the movement each phase of
development has been the result of occurrences closely
related to activities of Western nations. During the
eighteen-nineties reactionism was incited by the forced
return of the Liaotung Peninsula. to China and its subse-
quent transfer to Russia, by the partition of China, and
by the massacre at Blagoeschensk by European Russians of
Tartar Mongolians, all of these occurrences indicating to
certain Japanese the danger of the "white peril".
In the
latter part of the nineties (the arbitrary date is 1898),
the propaganda of socialism began in Japan. From that
time, one of the principal objects of reactionism was to
combat the spread of this and other radical movements,
the reactionary societies at times employing physical
violence against radioals. The greatest development of
reactionary societies, however, has occurred since l9l8. 
The

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
The World War made clear the imperfection of European civili-
zation; the results of the Washington Conference were re-
garded by some Japanese as unsatisfactory; the London
Naval Treaty was viewed as disadvantageous to Japan.
They have also been influenced, it seems probable, by the
Fascist movements in Italy and Germany.
Satisfactory statistics are not available, but the
1/.
Embassy has been able to compile a list of l24 so-called
reactionary societies. (One reactionary leader recently
stated, however, that there are some 3o0 reactionary societies
of a political character with a total membership of 30o,000.
Others estimate the total membership at perhaps 600,000.)
Of these l24 societies the year of organization of 96 is
known. 82 of these were organized since l918, a fact which
indicates the rapid development since the war. Only 3 of
these 96 were organized before 1900, while 11 were organized
between 1900 and 1917.
Those that existed before 1888
were probably without political color at that time.
influence of the London Naval Conferenoe on their growth is
in part indicated by the fact that 13 of these 96 societies
were organized since l930. At present there are four in the
process of organization.
It is only since the beginning of the Manchurian Affair,
V
however, that reactionary societies have received nation-
wide attention and have come to be regarded by many as a
possibly important factor of the domestic situation. The
usurpation of power by the military, particularly by the
group of younger and extremely nationalistic officers,
naturally enhanced the position of reactionary societies,
sinoe

=== Page 430 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
since their interests and aims are to a certain dogree
similar. The control of Manchuria and Mongolia by Japan
has long been the ambition of at least a part of the re-
v
actionary societies. Ryohei Uchida, mentioned above,
recently informed a member of my staff that he had memori-
alized the Government to this effect in 1913.
Another
leader said a few days ago that the enforced return of
the Liaotung Peninsula by Japan and its subsequent trans-
fer to Russia has been the dominating factor in the develop-
ment of reactionary societies. The importance of these
societies has been enhanced since last September by the
assistance which they have been able to render the military
through propaganda, the silencing of opposition, and organi-
zation of support.
Their potential danger has been realized
throughout the country by the recent disclosures that fol-
lowed the assassination of Baron Dan, reported in my des-
138
patoh No. 56l of April 7th. It is probable that these
societies will maintain their present position as long as
the military remains in power.
It is easy, however, to exaggerate the importance of
these societies as a unit.
Some of them would seem to be
little more than oultural.
The inclusion in their member-
ship of such men as the present Minister for Home Affairs,
the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry, the Senior
Director of the Mitsui Bank, and the Mayor of Tokyo in-
dicates that by no means all the members are fanatical
patriots. It would seem that their significance lies not in
any concerted action they might initiate but in possible
support they would give to others - say, the military -
in

=== Page 431 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
in a course of action relating to internal affairs.
Until the Manchurian Affair, the only theory that
seems to have been common to all of the societies is
"the preservation of the peculiar national polity and
characteristics" of Japan.* Since September 18th it
is probable that they are more or less welded together
by patriotism and by the conviction that Japanese con-
trol of Manchuria and Mongolia is necessary for the
preservation and development of Japan as a great power.
It is doubtful if other generalizations are applicable
to these societies when discussed as a unit.
Within
these societies there are fanatics, potential assassins,
extreme chauvinists, blackmailers, and rowdies, but also
there are intelligent men who can have little in common
with such people except those factors mentioned above.
The two chief leaders are, however, fanatical and could
influence a large section of these societies to support
a movement such as suggested in the preceding paragraph.
The type of mind of these leaders was indicated in the
conversation a few days ago with Ryohei Uchida, previous-
ly referred to. He stated that Manchuria has never be-
longed to China and that the Japanese Foreign Office,
Japanese newspapers and magazines, and Japanese industrial-
ists are not really Japanese and do not understand Japan.
2/.
Enclosure No. 2 further illustrates the character of these
This enclosure is a trans-
societies and their members.
lation of a description of the seven reactionary societies
which
* The word "reactionary" (hando)is not used in refer-
ence to these societies but kokusui, which liter-
ally means "essence of the country".

=== Page 432 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
which the officials of the Metropolitan Police Bureau
regard as the most important. It was published this
year in a semi-confidential volume intended for the in-
struction of higher detectives.
The volume was lent
to the Embassy in confidence.
At present two views exist regarding possible
developments of an extreme character that would probably
receive the support of a large section of the reaction-
ary societies.
One is the formation of a super-party
cabinet that would give complete support to the military
and the other is the taking over formally by the military
of control of the Government.
These eventualities are
still in the realm of speculation. In regard to the
latter, a proletarian political party leader recently
stated in conversation that in his opinion the military.
would take control when the Japanese capitalists finally
refuse to lend any more money to further the Manchurian
enterprise.
Either movement would be subserved by the reaction-
ary societies, and also probably by other organizations
of reactionary tendencies.
The most important of such
organizations is the Imperial Reservists Association,
which was established in l9l0 and now has a membership of
about 3,000,000, the object being the "promotion of martial
spirit", according to the JIJI YEAR BOOK OF 1931.
Young
Men's Associations also have reactionary characteristics.
There are 16,289 such associations with a total member-
ship of 2,577,000.
Organizations that could be expected
to give moral support are Young Men's Training Institutes,
numbering 15,701 with a membership of nearly 1,000,000,
the

=== Page 433 === (confidence 0.98)
一7-
the members being mostly working youths in their teens
who attend the institutes for ethical and military instruo-
tion, and Young Women's Associations, whioh number 14,256
with a membership of 1,616,000. Mention should also be
made in this connection of three so-called national defence
associations: the Navy Association (Kaigun Kyokai), estab-
lished in 1926 and having a membership of 9,300; the Sea
Defence Association (Kaibo Gikai), established in l922 and
having a membership of 9,400; and the National Defence Asso-
ciation (Kokubo Gikai), established in l9l7, and membership
unknown.*
It is understood that the word "fascism" has been used
lately in press despatches from Japan. The reactionary
societies claim that this word does not apply to them. Ryohei
Uchida when questioned as to whether or not the reactionary
societies favor the abolition of parliamentary government
replied that opinion on this point is divided. Another re-
actionary replied that perhaps half the societies are in
favor of this and half against. Apparently, members of
these societies regard the word as applicable in Japan only
to the movement that has developed recently in proletarian
667
political parties (page 5 of despatch No. 512 of February
27, 1932). I have gathered, also, that the reactionary
societies are not greatly interested in theories and are
uncertain what terms of theory describe their aims.
On April 15th the Shakai Minshuto (Social Democratic
Party), one of the two national proletarian political
parties, split on the question of state socialism, the
Central Committee having voted 61 to 52 against the
adoption of state socialism as a party prinoiple. Follow-
1ng
*It is to be expected that members of one organization
mentioned in this dospateh may possibly be members of
other organizations mentioned as well.

=== Page 434 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
ing this, Katsumaro Akamatsu, leading proletarian advocate
of state socialism, seceded from the party with a consid-
erable number of followers. He is said to have as sup-
porters three groups; namely, the Japan-Farmers' Union
(Nippon Nomin Kumiai), with 38,000 members; the Teiyu
Like-minded Association (Teiyu Doshi-kai), a political
club with 4,O00 members, and the Young Men's Socialistic
League (Shakai Seinen Domei), a political club with 5,0oo
members.
The press reports that he is now attempting to
form a state socialism party and hopes to obtain support
from a part of the other national proletarian party, the
National Labor-Farmer Masses Party (Zenkoku Rono Taishu-to),
but it is doubtful if any important part of that party sup-
ports this political theory.
. Apparently, however, he will
be joined by another proletarian group led by a Mr. Yasaburo
Shimonaka.
. Shimonaka states that the views of his group
coincide with those of Akamatsu's group and that their
leading principle is "Tenno Shugi", which might be trans-
lated as Emperor-ism.
He also says that his group, as
well as Mr. Akamatsu's group, can be termed Fascist if the
word is qualified by the adjective "Japanese".
He claims
to be in control of the Japan Federation of Labor Unions
(Nippon Rodo Soremmei),with a membership of 40,000; the
Patriotic Labor Party (Aikoku Kinro-to), with a membership
of 10,O00; and 21 local bodies calling themselves New
Political Party Supporting Associations, with membership
unknown.
He states that, after joining forces with
Akamatsu, the new party will have a membership of 100,000.
That the reactionary societies do not use the word
"Fascimm" with respect to themselves does not necessarily
mean

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.97)
- 9 -
mean that it is not applicable; it may mean no more than
that they do not wish to acknowledge that their nationalism,
which they insist is a product peculiar to Bushido, owes any-
thing to foreign influence.
They may be said to be Fasoist
to this extent, however: they would probably support, in
concert with other reactionary groups, a movement for
political change undertaken in the name of the Emperor.
Respectfully yours,
Elain  hseille

LEADING REACTIONARY GROUS.
Tokyo.
DAI NIPPON KOKUSUI-KAI
(Great Japan Nationalist Society)
This association was organized in October, l9l9, on
the initiative of Isaburo Nishimura and with the support
of Takejiro Tokonami(then Minister of Home Affairs),
Kokichi Aoyama, Nobusaburo Shino and others known for
their chivalrous spirit. The association, which was
named b the late Jogo Sugimura( a veteran scholar of
Chinese classics and famous educationalist), is a large
and poverful body of :ouths advocating the principle of
lo alty to the Imperial House and nationalism, having
for its object the sacrificing of self in the execution
of their duties to the State  nd Sovereign, the motto
of the members being the respect of chivalrous spirit
and patriotism. Naturally, the members of this associa-
tion are men of quite diffe ent frame of mind from those
imbued vith such dangerous r dical thoughts as socialism,
conn unism, syndicalism, et cetera, who are their anta-
gonists, nd very frequently the members come to a violent
clash vith the socialists or meibers of labor unions or
other radical elements. The most remarkable case was the
conflict between the Kokusui-kai melibers and me bers of
the Suihei-cha( outcasts' organization) which occurred
in Nara Prefecture in March, 1c23 and in which both sides
emploryed rms, the riotous disturbance having been
quelled by the interference of troops. This event, which
cre ted guite a sensution ut the time, is still fresh in
the mind of the public.
The Hokusui-kai is a comper tively poverful and
strong organization with a large folloing, vhich is,
however, divided into two groups, i.e., Kanto or Eastern
and Kansai or Western groups, which sometimes vie with
each other.
DAI NIPPON SEIGI-DAN
(Great Japan Justice Society)
This was organized in February, 1925, in Osaka by the
late Sahei Kobayashi, a citizen of Osaka, who was noted
for his chivalrous spirit and revered by the citizens of
the K ansai district on that account. At precent the.
association is presided over by Eizo Sakai, another
public spirited man who has succeeded to Kobayashi
(founder) and who takes interest in the task of capital
and labor harmonization. The societ has succeeded in
amicabiy settling several cases of labor disputes in the
district in and about Osaka. Under his guidance and
leadership the association has become very powerful
recently and meanwhile its roll of membership has con-
siderably increased, it having many members in various
places outside Osaka and Kwensai district. It has now
two headquarters, one in Osaka and the other in Tokyo,
the latter having been created in the fall of 1929. The
body is compared to the Black Shirts of Italy.

=== Page 453 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
DAI NIPPON SEISAN-TO
( Great Japan Productive Industry Party)
This is the youngest bod of all reactionary groups
advocating nationalistic princi le and is closely af-
filiated to the Kokuryu -kai, previously referred to.
It
was orgunized in June, 193l, in Osaka, and the first
general meeting  s held at the Sankai-do Hall, Tokyo,
on November 20, 1931. Among its supporting bodies, the
mo e influential are the Kokuryu-kai, Meitoku-kai, Kaiten,
Jihosha, Tenkokai(of Tokyo), Shiden Jidosha Shinyukai,
Jichi Remmei, Kitahama, Jichi-kai, Insatsu Shokko-kumai
Keishin-kai, Ninwakai(of Osaka) and six other similar
associations in K oto, Kobe and K ushu. Besides these
associations, it h: s man influential individual sup-
porters including Magosaburo Domaye of Osaka, Soichi Tsuru,
and Shigeyoshi Kiso of Kyushu, and others. The officers
are ae follows:
Adviser- Mitsuru Toyama.
Pr sident- Ryohei Uchida.
Inanagero, Zen-ichi Suzuki, Hironobu Sato,
Jo Akaba, Inagosaburo Domaye, Kennan Oyamada,
hi ten others.
The principles of the part: are as follows:
The p rt aims at estublishing a good administra-
tion on the basis of perfect harmony between the ruler and
the ruled in strict accordance with the rovisions of the
Constitution;
The party expects to abolish or effect the reform
of such institutions and legisl tion as are unadapted to
the national polity or the trend of na tional development
of the state, and to simplify the system of political organs
a d the method of their oper tion;
The party expects to make efforts for effecting the
principle of self-supply and thereby solidif ing the basis
of the state economy.

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
KENCOKU-KAI
(Empire Foundation Society)
Originally founded by Dr. Shinkichi Uesugi(died 1929),
former professor of the Imperial University of Tokyo
and an authorit on the constitution widely known as a
staunch auvocate of nationalistic ideas, in February,l926.
The Kenkoku-kai hs gradually risen to importance and
now take. the lead of all other bodies of similar color
as the vanguard of anti-commaunistic, anti-capitalistic and
anti-socialistic movements. It is the strongest advocate
and upholder of n tionalism nd loyalty to the Imperial
house, and s such is the most formidable enemy to the
enthusiasts of the coln uni tic idea. The members of this
association belong to the so-called extreme reactionary
group and are known for their constant activities and
enthusiasm in condu ting anti-socialistic and anti-
communistic campaigns. After the death of its founder, Dr.
Uesugi, the acsociution was and is presided over by Bin
Akao, ho is assisted by Satsuzo Ihera, the la ter being
the actual leader, wielding greater influence among and
colncin more respect of the  embers than the president.
KOMURYU-NAI
( Black Drugon Society)
Organized in February, 1901, by Ryuhei Uchida, noted
publicist and staunch advocate of nationalism, and his
compatriots, this association has for its object the
u holding of the cause of loyalt t the Empcror, the
harmoniz tion of the Oriental and Occidental civilizations,
and the elevation of the st tus of this country to a
position to lead all Asiatic races on the road to their
development and expansion. The activities of its leading
embers manifested in its anti-Bolshevism campaign directed
against the Osaka Asahi Shimbun in 1918 made the asso-
ciation and its leader sudienly famous and left strong im-
pressions of its existence in the minds of the public.
Indignant at the attitude and standpoint of the Osaka
journal which o posed the dispatch of an expeditionary
force to Siberia in 1el8 b the Government(the Terauchi
Ministry) and expounded its views in somewhat vehement
phraseology occasionally bordering on lèse majesté, the
leading members of the cssociation , assisted by the
nembers of the Ronin Association, ano thcr body of men
advocating Nipponism or n tionalism, attacked the Osaka
Asahi Shimbun office, appeuling to direct action, and forced
several members of its editorial staff to resign and leave
the estavlishment, these including Ikuo Oyama(later head
of the Rono-to or Farmers an Labor Party), Nyozekan Hase-
gawa and other able writers tinged with radical thoughts.
R ukei Murayama, president and proprietor of the Osaka
Asahi Publishing Company, was also involved and was found
one night bound to a stone lantern at Nakanoshima Park.
In June, l93l, Ryohei Uchida and his compatriots organized
in Osaka an association named "Dai Nippon Seisan-to", a
quasi-

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
quasi- political party advocating nationalism, of which
the present association, i.e., Kokuryu-kai, is now the
principal supporting body.
NIPPON SHAKAI-SHUGI KENK U-JO
( Ja an Socialism Research Asociation).
Though the smallest of all existing bodies advocating
nationalism, in res  ct to its scale and size of its
membership roll, this associ tion is worth while men-
tioning here as a body aavocating the principle of state
socialism nd the most significant of all associations
upholding the cause of nationalism. The attitude and
stanapoint of the members who are represented by such
prominent of socialistic thinkers and scholars as Tatsuo
Tsukui, Natsuo Eto, Junjiro Ishikawa and Katsumaro Akamatsu,
are theoretical, unlike those of other nationalistic bodies,
and as such the mo ement of this group is arresting keen
attention of those ho are interested in the development of
national-socialism in thi: country. The " who's who"
of the leading maabers of the staff are briefly mentioned
hereunder:
Tatsuo Tsukui was formerly chief secretary of the
Kenkoku-kai under Dr. Shinkichi Usugi and is the
possessor of a socialistic theory of his own;
Natsuo Eto is the grandson of Shimpei Eto of the
Restoration fame, whose neme is very famous in connection
With the famous Saga Rebellion in the e rly stage of the
Mei ji Era of whic: he vas the leader. At present the roung
Eto is on the : t ff of the Toa Keizai-Chosa-Kyoku(East
Asia Economic Research Bureau) presided over by Dr. Shumei
Okawa;
Junjiro Ishikawa is a noted Marxist and one of the
leading members of the Shakai Minshu-to(Social Democratic
Party);
Katsumaro Akamaro is Chief Secretary of the Shakai
Minshu-to, a theorist, and one of the foremost le ders
of social movement in Japan, being son-in-law of Dr.
Sakuzo Yoshino.
The principle and iueal of the association may be sunmarized
s follows:
" We believe that the traditional monarchy is the
form of Gover ment best fitted for the Jap nese mtion
and under this belief we expect to carr: out the adminis-
tr tive rule of this country according to the dictation
of that principle; We believe the Japanese state is held
responsible for the assurance of the living of Japonese
people and from mhat belief we demand the State to effect
all necessar: reforms for that purpose; We deem the existing
anachronistic economic s stem of capitalism, based on the
principle of private ownership of all productive organs
presses on the life of the nation and therefore we expect to
upset such an economic s stem at the e rliest possible
moment; We believe the common ownership of productive organs
and

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
and concentrated economic planning by the State
are the only effcctive measures for relieving the
impoveriched conditions of the great najority of
the Japanese nation and thercfore we expect the reali-
zation of that obj-ct b all means; We claim that all of
the people of Ja n are entitled to equal rights and should
shoulder equal obligations nū that nobody should be
llo.ed to pursue his on interests against the interests
and welfare of others and we des ise those who act in
contradiction of that principle; We believe all nationals
are entitled to enjoy equal share and rights in the supply
and resources of the necessities of life and we claim that
 na tion posres:in rel tively extensive 1 nd end excessive
re ources, compared vith the size of its population, ought
to open it. doors to other impoveri hed nations; From the
aforementioned principle and point of view ve insist that
t'ose nctions ho own more than enough land and resources
must offer their sup rfluous land and resour ces to the use
of the Japanese nation".
Katsumaro Akamatsu is an advocate of the theory according
to Which the international movement of proletarians as is
carried on by the present da. socialists is still premature
and holds the opinion that before an thing else efforts
should be mede for  dapting the state organism of this
country to thet of national socialism. His attitude is
viered s a ca e of conversion from the so-called inter-
nationalism to nationalism, Wich principle is inconsistent
with that of the Shakai Minshu-to to Which he belongs. His
entry into the present a sociation is, therefore, regarded
as seriously affecting the movement of the right socialists
in this country, and even an eventual split in the ranks
of that party is forecasted, although the control over its
members and their mo eents is smoothly operating due to the
efforts of its leader Isoh Abe, Akamatsu, and other leading
members.
ZEN-NIPPON AIKOKUSHA KYODOTOSO KYOGIKAI
( Federation of All-Japan Patriotic Associationg)
This was established in March,1931, as the central and
guiding organ of all patriotic associations throughout
the country, including Dai Nippon Seimen Domei(Great Japan
Young Men's Federation of Japan), Nippon Seimen-to (Japan
Toung Men's Party of Japan), Kyushin Aikoku-to(Radical
Patriotic Party), Nippon Rodo-kai (Japen Labor Association)
and others. The principle it advocates is pure Nipponism
or m tionalism and its object lies in the up-rooting of
capitalism and realization of State socialism. From the
standpoint given the Federation clamors for the downfall of
all existing political parties and aims at the stamping
out of communistic and socialistic elements as well as
the expulsion of all proletarian parties. For the realiza-
tion of its avowed object and principle the Federation
relies upon the efforts and co-operation of officers and
men of the Army and Navy, it being the opinion of of the
members of the Federation that the Army and the Navy are
not intended for the service of the so-called bourgeois
classes but directly belong to the sovereign and are in-
tended for the service of that most exalted personage.
As

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
As such, the Federation h s not a few sympathisers among
the youthful officers of the two military services, it
is understood, and it promises to become one of the
leading factors in the nationalistic movement in future.
The platform of the Federation contains the following:
" We refuse to recognize the present parliamentary
politics and expect to realize the revival of the former
system of goveriment under direct control of the Emperor.
" We expect to establish the system of supreme control
of national industry under the supervision of the Emperor
and expect to root out capitalism.
" We expect to root out the evil of class rivalry in
the nation and to elevate the status of this country and
adv nce ite n tional prestige.
The chief secret ry of the Federation is Tatsuo Tsukui, who
acts as its chief executive in the meantime.
Furthermore, there are meny more associati ons of lesser
im ortance, hich are lso included in those bodies advocating
notionalism and regarded as reactionar. elements, end among
the. th note..orth: are the Shuyodan(Moral Culture Society)
and the Kokuhonsha(National Source Society), the former being
presided over by Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma. However, these
associations are not properly to be classed under the group of
the so-called nationalistic or reactionary bodies, their object
bein to promote the development of culture and to help the
growth of sound thought in the nation. The. should be classed
as bodies dev ted to ethical ovement.

My telegram No. 128, May 16, 4 p. m.
No new overnment has yet been formed. The Emperor
has sent for Saionji to advise on this point and he is
expected to come to Tokyo tomorrow. There is much
opposition to a party government and certain army
officers are reported as insisting that the army will
have nothing to do with any party administration.
There is talk of a coalition or national government but
recent events seen to have inhibited eagerness for office..
Takahashi, Minister of Finance, is holding on as provion

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL DIVISION OF
ERD/ord
MAY 19 1932
Edwin L. Neville, Esquire,
Chargé a'Affaires ad interim,
American Embassy,
Tokyo.
894.00/397
Sir:
I have the honor to report that a peculiar
story has reached me, which may be of interest to
the Embassy.
There have been articles in the newspapers
recently regarding the suicides of several Japanese
Army officers, usually in China. Ultra-patriotism
is invariably the motive given for these suicides.
The story which I have heard is that the officers
who committed suicide are some of those who took
part in the attempted coup d'etat in Tokyo in
Ootober last. The story is to the effect that the
authorities did not wish to court martial the
officers, because of the publicity which might
result, and consequently sent them to China to be
killed or to commit suicide if they did not happen
to be killed. Also that an aviation officer who
was killed in China was the man who supplied the
pistols to the blood brotherhood whioh assassinated
Inouye and Dan, and that he was probably put out of
the way to prevent any further investigation of his
connection with the assassinations.
FILED
I shall be very glad to learn if the Embassy
91932
has any confirmation of these rumors.
Respeotfully yours,
E. R. DICKOVER
E. R. Diokover,
Amerioan Consul.

DATED MAY 17, 1932
Légation del Japon
Buenos Aires, le 17 Mai,1932.-
Monsieur l'Ambassadeur,
Votre Excellence a eu la délicate attention de
venir m'exprimer la sympathie à l'égard de ma patrie,
doulouneusement frappée par la perte tragique de son
Président du Conseil, M. Tsuyoshi Inukai.
J'ai l'honneur de Vous assurer de ma profonde
gratitude pour vos sentiments de condoléances et m'em
presse de Vous faire part que je n'ai pas manqué d'en
transmettre à mon Gouvernement.
Veuillez agréer, Monsieur l'Ambassadeur, aveo
mes sincères remerciements, les assurances de ma tres
haute considération.
Chibatm Miyakoshi
Chargé d'Affaires du Japon.
Son Excellence,
Monsieur Robert Woods Bliss,
Ambassadeur des Etats Unia d'Amérique,

=== Page 528 === (confidence 0.99)
(Enclosure N° 2 in Despatch N• 1638)
TRANSLATION OF NOTE FROM THE
JAPANESE CHARGE D'AFFAIRES
DATED MAY 17, 1932.
Japanese Legation
Buenos Aires, May 17, 1932.
Mr. Ambassador,
Your Excellency had the courtesy to come and express
to me his sympathy for my country which is grievously
afflicted by the tragic loss of its Prime Minister, Mr.
Tsuyoshi Inukai.
I have the honor to assure Your Excellency of my
deep gratitude for your sentiments of condolence and I
hasten to inform you that I did not fail to convey them
to my Government.
I beg Your Excellency to accept the assurance of
my highest consideration with my sincere thanks.
Chibata Miyakoshi
Chargé a'Affaires du Japon

I have the honor to report that at 5:30 P. M., on
Sunday, May 15th, Premier Tsuyoshi Inukai was shot and
fatally wounded by a group of eleven cadets and six young
naval officers.* At the same time attacks in the nature of
demonstrations were made on the homes of Count Makino and
Admiral Suzuki, the Metropolitan Police Headquarters, the
Headquarters of the Seiyukai Party, the Bank of Japan, and
the Mitsubishi Bank. It was also learned that simultaneous
attempts
894.00/388
* Embassy's telegram Nó. 126 of May 15, 1932, 10 P. M.

=== Page 532 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
attempts were made to cut off the electric power stations
supplying Tokyo.
Three or four individuals were wounded
by pistol shots, and one policeman at the home of the
Prime Minister was killed.
The attacks chiefly consisted
of the throwing of small hand grenades which did little,
if any, damage.
Inmediately following these simultaneous
attacks most of the participating officers voluntarily sur-
rendered themselves at the Gendarmerie headquarters.
Wild rumors were immediately circulated and all
politicians and important personages connected with the
Government gathered at various places to discuss the situ-
ation.
Among those who reported to the Emperor during
the evening were Lieutenant-General Araki, the Grand
Chamberlain Admiral Suzuki, and Count Makino.
An im-
promptu meeting of the Cabinet at the Official Residence
of the Premier decided to appoint Finance Linister Takahashi
to the position of Premier pro tem.
Baron Hiranuma, the
Vice President of the Privy Council, called together a
number of that body; and Lieutenant-General Araki was re-
ported to have sat up most of the night with a group of
the leading officers of the Army.
The next morning, Tuesday, May 17th, at 11:30 o'clock,
the temporarily appointed Premier, Takahashi, called at the
Imperial Palace to tender the resignations of all the
members of the Cabinet.*
Discussions took place during the day at the Seiyukai
headquarters, where an attempt was made to sink individual
differences and unite on one candidate for the office of
President of the Party.
A meeting of the principal
Seiyukai leaders also took place at the residence of the
Prime
894.00/389
* Embassy's telegram Nó. 128 of May 16, 1932, 4 P. M.

=== Page 533 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Prime Minister and a decision was reached whereby Dr.
Suzuki, the present Minister for Home Affairs, would be
recommended for the office of president of the Seiyukai,
to be installed on Friday.
The principal groups which had to be considered in
solving the complex problem of organizing a new government
were as follows: (1) the Emperor and his advisers; (2)
Prince Saionji; (3) the Privy Council; (4) the Seiyukai
Party; (5) the Army; (6) the Minseito Party.
Lack of
unity in the Seiyukai Party and among the Army further
complicated the situation in the preliminary discussions.
It was learned that Prince Saionji had already decided
to come to Tokyo so that it was unnecessary for an Imperial
messenger to be sent for him.
One of the most active members of the Seiyukai Party
during preliminary discussions was Mr. Kaku Mori, Chief
Secretary of the Inukai Cabinet.
He not only took a lead-

Suzuki and Tokonami but attempted to bring about a reconcili-
ation between the Seiyukai and the Military leaders.
Press reports indicated that the principal issue was
whether a super-party nationalistic cabinet or a purely
party cabinet should be formed.
The situation during
Monday (the l6th) was tremendously confused with all of
the various factions mentioned so active that it was im-
possible to even guess what the ultimate resulting govern-
ment would be.
Wild rumors were circulated with little
or no foundation that assassinations had just begun, that
there was danger of street rioting, that the Emperor him-
self was in danger of deposition.
The city of Tokyo,
however, has remained remarkably quiet and with hardly a
sign of disturbance or excitement.

On Tuesday, May l6th, it was definitely announced

During Tuesday and Wednesday conversations continued

plication in the discussions.
On Thursday, May 19th, the press reported that the
attitude of the military had stiffened and that they
seemed determined to force their radical program on what-
ever cabinet might be formed.
General Araki emerged as
the spokesman and perhaps as the outstanding leader of the
military faction.
Baron Hiranuma's name was again men-
tioned as possible head of a super-party cabinet.
Prince Saionji arrived in Tokyo and immediately be-

Sir:
1412
I have the honor to refer to my despatch/No.623,
of May 20, 1932, outlining the events in connection with
the "May 15th affair" in which Premier Inukai was assassinated,
and to present herebelow comment on these events.
The events of Sunday evening, May l5th, present a
situation which Western observers have difficulty in under-
standing because of the extraordinary concepts which actuated
the perpetrators. I have no definite knowledge of their
motives

=== Page 540 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
motives, but may attempt to present some of the con-
clusions to which I am led by study of the situation.
The Japanese people pride themselves on a peculiar
feeling of responsibility, the evidences of which to
Westerners, often assume a somewhat ludicrous, and in
some cases, tragic aspect. Japanese history is full
of stories of samurai who committed suicide in protest
against actions of their superiors, or who performed
some desperate deed, fatal to themselves, as a warning
to their superiors.
In recent years this somewhat feudal concept of duty
has been largely replaced by more modern ideas, but among
subordinate officials, particularly of the armed forces,
it remains strong. This leads to acts which are often
anti-social and,according to Western ideals, criminal.
Nevertheless Japanese understand the motive is unselfish
and to them highminded. The law may demand, and secure
punishment for the crime, but in the eyes of the people
the guilt is atoned for by the good faith with which the
deed was committed. Hence the tendency in Japan, con-
demned by foreign observers, to condone acts of violence
by misguided patriots against political leaders who are
considered to have betrayed the country.
Only in this way can the assaults of May 15th be
explained. The younger officers, whose activities the
Embassy has noted from time to time, feel with the entire
country that the political leaders have played the dountry
false. During the past few months, criticism of parlia-
mentary government as exemplified by the Diet and of the
oonduot

=== Page 541 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
conduct of the political parties and the Cabinet has been
vociferous. The lack of probity and patriotism of the
Diet representatives as well as their turbulent conduot
in the House, their lack of feeling of responsibility
for the welfare of the country, their venality and
corruption have disgusted and alarmed the entire nation.
One recent example which caused particular resentment among
military men and had, I believe, no little influence in
creating the present state of public opinion, was the
attempt to oust Count Uchida from the presidency of the
South Manchuria Railway purely for reasons of party
893.77
2857
politics(See Embassy's despatch No. 586, of April 22,
1938).
The events of Sunday evening, thus, may be inter-
preted as a peculiarly Japanese demonstration against
governmental mismanagement. The conclusion which seems
inevitable is that the entire affair was in the nature
of a gesture in the hope of arousing public opinion
to the critical state of affairs and of precipitating
a political crisis which would resuit in the downfall
of the Government. Inoidentally, both of these objects
have been attained.
The shooting of the Premier was merely a part of
the demonstration. He could not have been held personally
responsible for the evils of the Government, but a
sacrifice was necessary and a sacrifice of such pro-
minence that his death would serve as a warning to the
whole nation. However sophisticel  this explanation
may appear, I am convinced that it is true. Moreover,
it is certain that there are many intelligent Japanese
who, although they regret the cruel death of the Premier,
confess

=== Page 542 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
confess that they have full sympathy with the ideals
of the perpetrators and are inclined to agree with
them that a sacrifice was necessary to shock the
nation into conscionsness of the situation which
confronts it.
Bearing out this theory, it will be noted that
the conspirators attacked(1) the Prime Minister,(2)
the Selyukai Headquarters, (3) the Residence of Count
Makino,(4) the Metropolitan Police Station,(5) banking
institutions. In all of these attacks no one of any
political importance was injured except the Premier.
The "bombs" thrown at the Seiyukai, the Police Station
and the banks were small hand grenades incapable of
doing any material damage. They merely served to in-
dicate the institutions which the conspirators con-
sidered culpable. The Prime Minister represented the
Government as a whole; the Seiyukai represented the
political parties and the Diet, both guilty of many
abuses. Count Makino has been criticized for faulty
advice tendered to the Emperor; the Police have been
blamed for playing politics and neglecting their proper
duties; the banks have been attacked for exchange opera-
tions at the expense of the nation's finance. The con-
clusion seems inescapable that the attacks were merely
demonstrations. How otherwise than a gesture could be
interpreted the act of throwing a small hand grenade
against a deserted bank at a late hour on Sunday?
The theory is further borme out by the faot that
the conspirators surrendered voluntarily to the police
or gendarmes. This is quite in accordance with the
Japanese

=== Page 543 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Japanese tradition of responsibility for one's conduct.
They must have had full knowledge of the penalty that
that
awaited them, and must have resolved the cause was
worth the sacrifice of their lives.
There has been much speculation as to the number
of men involved in this conspiracy, and as to what baeking
they had. It seems incredible that a small band of
about twenty men, however resolute, would attempt such
desperate deeds unless assured of support, moral or
otherwise, from some powerful direction. However, no
information is available in regard to such support.
That military and naval officers should have been able
to get together and plan a concerted attack indicates
some degree of organization, particularly as the two
branches of the service have little in common in this
district, there being no naval station of importance
nearer to Tokyo than Yokosuka, fifty miles away. In
this connection the MIYAKO SHIMBUN stated editorially:
" We do not know whether the terrorists were backed by
others, but there can be no dispute that what they did
has been encouraged by the agitation of men of Fascist
views. Toward the end of the Wakatsuki Cabinet a certain
plot was engineered by a certain group(of young officers)
but it was nipped in the bud. Had the authorities made
public at that time a true version of the affair, we
believe the recent incident might have been prevented".
It is rumored, with credible evidence, that all of
these young men were under the influence, if not pupils,
of an extraordinary institution, the Aikyojiku(Patriotic
Sohool) of Mito, a town north of Tokyo. This town played
a

=== Page 544 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
a significant part in Japanese history, for it was here
that plans for the Meiji Restoration were hatched, and from
this town came the assassin of I1 Kamon no Kami, the en-
lightened statesman of the Shogunate and protagonist of
the movement to open Japan to foreign intercourse. More-
over, as further evidence that the town has retained its
reactionary character, it was here and in the surrounding
district that the infamous "blood brotherhood" was organized
which, during the past few months, has accounted for the lives
of Mr. Inouye and Baron Dan. In spite of this circumstantial
connection, I should hesitate to link thess slayers with the
conspirators of Sunday except for other happenings on that
same evening. At almost exactly the same hour as the assassi-
nation of Mr. Inukai, a man by the name of Nishida, formerly
a member of the "blood brotherhood", who had turned informer
and testified against the group, was himself assassinated
in the suburbs of Tokyo. Moreover, the press reports that
only quick action by the employees of the electric stations
in Tokyo prevented an attempt to throw the city into dark-
ness made at the identical hour by a group of men said to
be from Mito. Thus, all of the devious lines of evidence,
when followed, lead back to that historic town with its re-
putation for fanatical patriotism.
Externally at least the vernacular press is unanimous
in condemning the action of the young officers. Editorially ,
the newspapers condemn the appeal to force and deplore that
mistaken ideas have resulted in tragedy. The NICHI NICHI
stated on May l6th: " A patriotic motive does not condone
a murder or any other abuse. There is no good effect in
direct action, however exalted its motive may be. Once
the public shows a tendenoy to regard murder as justified
when

=== Page 545 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
when impelled by patriotism, all the forces opposed to
law and order will get out of hand". There is, however,
an undercurrent of sympathy for the conspirators and their
ideals. For example, the YOMIURI SHIMBUN stated on May
17th: " Japan has seen this year a chain of undesirable
incidents..... Those who took part were spurred by the
economic depression and actuated by the weakness of the
Government's foreign policy. No superficial control can
prevent recurrence of these undesirable incidents. The
authorities need to study well the real causes of unrest....
and unless they be remedied, undesirable events cannot be
1,2/.
prevented". I append hereto translations of some of the
press editorials commenting on the violence of Sunday
evening.
The actual connection between the young officers
who took part in this affair and the Army in general will
probably never be revealed. It is sufficient to say that
any such organization could not exist without the knowledge
of the superior officers. However, one may be certain that
whatever attitude the military displays publicly toward the
conspirators, the aims and convictions of the latter are
those of the military as well. The Army has always stood
as the bulwark of intense patriotism, self-abnegation for
the good of Emperor and country and has had small patience
with the cynical self-interest of party politicians. They
regard themselves as direct guardians of the person and
prerogatives of the Emperor and have repeatedly demon-
strated that in fulfilling their interpretation of this
duty they will stand no interference from politicians or
civil government. They have seen the country in a financial
depression

=== Page 546 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
depression so severe that economists prediot the virtual
extinction of the landed farmer. They have seen un-
employment growing while prices have fallen to a level
where production is unprofitable. At the same time they
have watched the large banking and mercantile institu-
tions make vast sums out of exchange manipulations, while
politicians with scarcely concealed contempt for national
welfare indulge in endless wrangling and mutual abuse.
I wish to emphasize that not only does public opinion
to a large degree support the Army, but that the public
viewpoint coincides with that of the military. In many
respects the Army seems far more representative of public
opinion than the Diet. There is much evidence that the
people are quite as disgusted with party politics as con-
ducted in recent years as is the Army. They feel that the
only direction in which they can turn for patriotic and
devoted service to the country is the military. The Army
recognizes the existence of this support and is prepared
to dictate terms to the politicians. It is difficult to
avoid drawing the conclusion from recent statements and
events that parliamentary government in Japan is being
weighed in the balance at this time. In any event, it
is quite plain that the Army is going to play a larger
part in politics. I quote herebelow from the influential
JIJI SHIMPO of May 1oth:
" At yesterday's conference of the three heads of
the Army, an opinion was reached as to the formation
of the succeeding cabinet. It was decided to permit
the formation of a party cabinet, viz, the extension
of the Seiyukai Ministry under the premiership of Dr.
Suzuki on the following conditions:
1. That the new Ministry carry out and effect
practical purification of party politics, and as
a proof testifying to its intention thereof.
exoluding

=== Page 547 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
excluding such politicians as are suspected or
despised by the nation.
2. That in the light of the bad experience with
the London Naval Treaty, the new Ministry shall
strictly respect and take care to avoid inter-
ference with the Emperor's supreme control of the
armed services, particularly because the question
of the supreme command will necessarily come forth
in connection with the future policy vis-a-vis
Manchuria.
3. That the new Ministry shall make a wholehearted
effort for the control of public thought.
4. That the new Ministry shall cooperate with the
Army in matters relating to social wellfare.
It was also arranged that the new War Minister(who-
ever may be chosen) shall accept the offer to participate
in a new cabinet only after the aforementioned four con-
ditions shall be agreed to by the organizer of the new
cabinet".
I have no reason to discount the genuineness of the extra-
ordinary statement. It is quite in accord with the spirit
animating the Army at present.
There has been much talk of Fascism in Japan, but the word
is misleading when applied to movements in this country. It
predicates the personal rule of an outstanding personality.
No such rule would be possible in Japan, where all rule is
contingent upon Army approval. The Army itself is far too
well organized and disciplined to permit any of its members
undertaking a personal dictatorship. If a dictatorship
results from the present situation it will be with the sup-
port and under the control of the Army, in the name of the
Emperor and dedicated to the welfare of the people. It
will, in view of the statements of its leaders, have a strong
tinge of state socialism, favoring the agrarian classes and
opposing the domination of capitalists in politics. It may
be expected to adopt a more resolute policy toward Manchuria.
▲s

=== Page 548 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
As regards parliamentarism, the Army has not followed
the young officers in declaring against representative govern-
ment and political parties per se . Army leaders would
hesitate to take a step which would bring them into con-
flict with the Constitution. The statement above quoted
indicates that the military are willing to allow the parties
to exist, but insist on sweeping changes in methods of adminis-
tration. In this the nation seems to concur. The powerful
Osaka MAINICHI stated editorially on May 18th : " Never before
have parliamentary politics been placed on the slab for a
more profound and critical scrutiny than at this time. Prince
Saionji is reported as desiring to see constitutional poli-
tics progress smoothly. This may be the best course, but
thus far in this country the form is complete but the actual
workings are rudimentary. Whether parliamentary politics
are to be abolished or whether further united efforts are
to be made to realize the real object of this system, is
the paramount question now confronting the nation".
391
As reported in my telegram No. 132 May 18, 6 P.M.
Prince Saionji has been called upon by the Emperor, as usual,
to give advice in appointing a new premier. The aged Genro
has not yet, five days later, delivered his opinion, and is
said to be giving close study to the situation. It is certain
that he is seriously concerned over a situation which has such
grave possibilities for parliamentarism. He has always been
a stout protagonist of constitutional party government. on
the other hand, today's newspapers report that the military
are even more determined to prevent the formation of a straight
party government.
The present situation illustrates the inherent weakness
os

=== Page 549 === (confidence 0.98)
-11-
of the Japanese Constitution under present conditions. In
previous despatches from time to time the Embassy has pointed
out the necessity for some authority in the background to
give the Emperor "advice". The Japenese Government at present
is composed, for practical purposes, of three distinct ad-
ministrative controls. The civil administration, headed by
the Prime Minister, and the General Staffs of the Army and Navy.
The Ministers of War and Navy, who are the heads of the ad-
ministrative branches of their respective departments, have no
direct control over the General Staff. The latter have direct
access to the Emperor, which means, actually, that they are,
except in fiscal matters, independent of the War and Navy
Ministers and of the Prime Minister.
Under the Emperor Meiji a group of men who had built
up the modern state exercised a coordinating influence. All
branches of the State services were represented in this group;
Prince Yamagata, Army; Prince Ito,civil administration; Prince
Matsukata, finance; and so forth. In times of political crisis,
this group met, debated the questions at issue and gave the
Emperor their collective advice. They were men of immense
prestige and great energy and as they acted as a unit their
views as to personnel and general policy were bound to prevail.
There are no men today who occupy their position. This
means that there is no coordinating organ in the Government and
the separate branches of the Administration go their more or
less independent ways. Prince Saionji, who has been relied
on to fill the position once occupied by the Elder Statesmen
or Genro of Meiji days, is an old man. He apparently has not
the energy, nor the prestige with the younger men, needed to
oow

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.97)
-12-
cow warring factions. Until some coordinating organ is
developed, or there is a constitutional reorganization,
Japanese politics must remain subject to uncertainties
in times of stress.
Respectfully yours,
Eduin I. haill
Edwin L. Neville,

Tokyo.
The Assassination of Premier Inukai
and the Unrest in Tokyo.
Editorial.
Tokyo ASAHI,May 17, 1932.
The assassination of Premier Inukai at his official
residence in Tokyo in broad daylight is an unprecedently
serious incident in the history of modern Japan. A mote-
worthy fact was that gangs of outlaws attacked the Metropo-
litan Police Office; official residence of Count Makino,
Lord Keeper of Privy Seal; headquarters of the Government
Party; the Seiyukai; Bank of Japan; Mitsubishi Bank and
electric power stations in the outskirts of Tokyo at almost
the same time. Though such an attack might have been a
mere demonstration, yet there is something sufficient to
justify the conclusion that it was carried out systematically
in an extensive scale. These outrageous actions were com-
mitted on Sunday afternoon , May 15, when all officers and
officials were nearly off their duties. Nevertheless, the
main entrance of the Metropolitan Police Office was broken
through by a gang of outlaws who fired at the room of Super-
intendent of the Central Police and then these outlaws sur-
rendered themselves to the authorities voluntarily before
either the police or gendarmerie could arrest them. This
fact alone gives the citizens of Tokyo a feeling of great un-
easiness.
The present incident seems to bear one significant point
which profoundly appeals to our sense of solicitude. In it
that the incident was an outcome of a well studied plot em-
bodying a sort of new theory which may amount to a political
revolution. While nothing definite is yet known as to motives

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
motives of the outlaws, it is quite apparent that the
incident was the outcome of indignation at evils attendant
on the existing party administration. It is generally
admitted t at the political parties as they exist at present
are not such as to inspire the people with any confidence
and that evils of a party government as it is operated by
such a party can no longer be tolerated. However, any such
direct actions as to assassinate a Prime Minister and attack
banks, et cetera, with revolvers and hand grenades will tend
to undermine the very foundations of the Constitutional
Administration which had been laid by the late Meiji Emperor
himself. We may recall here that the late Meiji Emperor, in
one of His Edicts, pronounced that State affairs shall be
conducted in accordance with public opinion. Whatever the
motives of the outlaws, the direct actions committed by them
are unpardonable.
We ardently hope that the appropriate authorities will
take prompt measures so as to give the citizens a feeling of
ease and tranquility. At the same time, we advise the autho-
rities to punish the outlaws rigidly and strictly under the
1aw.

Tokyo.
The Assassination of Premier
Inukai.
(Editorial)
Tokyo HOCHI, May 19, 1932.
The assassination of Premier Inukai on May 15 has aroused
a great sensation in our financial world. The Tokyo Stock
Exchange suspended business for two days, while various func-
tions arranged by the Japan Industrial Club and other public
bodies in Tokyo were postponed indefinitely. All this goes
to show that capitalists of the country were terror stricken
in the extreme by the occurrence of the shocking incident.
The suspension of transactions at the Stook Exchange seems to
be chiefly attributable to the fear thet quotations would fall
considerably, but it is not difficult to conjecture what ideas
the assassins of Premier Inukai have entertained toward the
Stock Exchange.
Whatever the reason, the assassination of Premier Inukai
at his official residence in broad daylight is unpardonable.
The throwing of bombs at the buildings of the Bank of Japan
and other principal monetary organs in Tokyo at the same hous
as the assassination of the Premier, is also unpardonable.
The Government should take appropriate measures so as to
prevent the recurrence of such unlawful action in the future.
Why the Central Bank and other banking institutions were
attacked as above stated by associates of the assassins is
worthy of consideration. Undoabtedly the above attack was
due

=== Page 554 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
due to an animosity against capitalists. In fact, the parti-
cipants in the present shocking incident are as antagonistio
toward capitalists as they are toward political parties.
Capitalists are apparently terror-stricken in the extremo
as already stated. What is the reason thereof? No one can
deny the fact that capitalists in cooperation with political
parties have hitherto been too selfish and too avaricious to
work for the sake of the State and the people. It is hardly
necessary to point out that the State wherein we live is com-
posed of the ruler, people, and land. We trust that capitalists
and political parties have no intention whatever to ignore the
Imperial House and other classes of people. If capitalists are
under the impression that capitalists and laborers constitute
the whole of the people, and if they should insist upon the
realization of financial and economic policies based on the
promotion of interests of capitalists and laborers only, it is
but natural that various classes of the people other than
capitalists and laborers would entertain an animosity against
capitalists.
Some capitalists contend that unless the prices of com-
modities show an upward tendency the financial world would not
revive, while others say that domestic industries will in no
way attain a development unless a high tariff wall is set up.
Capitalists belonging to the above category urge that if the
financial world should not revive and domestic industries not
attain a development the number of unemployed would increase.
This argument seems to be sane and reasonable. But the whole
of the people is not composed of capitalists and laborers only.
Capitalists must not forget that besides laborers there are
other classes of the people who do not derive any benefit from
high prices of commodities and a high tariff wall, and whose
living will be threatened more seriously than now by suwh a
polioy.

=== Page 555 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
The people of today say that the extreme left and the
extreme right blocs are alike dangerous. This apprehension
is quite correct. However, the people lack preparedness to
study the causes of such danger. The right bloc is not an
assembly of laborers or capitalists. While a section of
capitalists and politicians is indulging in luxurious living,
men and women belonging to the right bloc are working hard day
and night for a living. They belong to a section of the people
who will not benefit themselves from an advance in the prices of
comnodities and a high tariff wall. With the occurrence of the
present shocking incident, we hope that capitalists will awake
to the actual conditions of society and see that the Japanese
people are not merely composed of capitalists and proletaire.
In future, capitalists should not press the Government with a
proposal to formulate and carry out such financial and economic
policies as to benefit capitalists and laborers as they did
before. We eagerly hope that the next Ministry will fully
consider these matters in outlining its policies.

THE YENCHING GAZETTE May 18, 1932
In Eight Months
Exactly eight months have elapsed since Japan "took over"
Manchuria, the three eastern provinces of China that have come to
be regarded as an "international trading ground" for the nitions of
the world. Her action. following a series of incidents that were
emploved as legitimate excuses at the time she sent her forces
into the territory, was to consummate several objectives that she
has long had in mind. These includ- i the setting up of a changed
government that would b: in sympathy with her plans to exploit
the region, to remedy what she asserted to be a bad state of banditry
and to force the adherence on the part of China to her own
interpretations of rtain treaty and railway and cconomic
agreements.
Now at the end of cight montbs to what extent has Japan
succeeded in accomplishing these obi ctives?
The new kovernment has bren set up. The manner of its
coming into existence, its fadse character and the way of its opera-
tion ate too well known to warrant din cussion here. It is now
struggling against great oxtds for its e xistence, despite the fact that
it has the full military strength of the Japanese empire behind it
and that it is wholly Japanese managed. No nation has recognized
it, not even Japan, herself. No nation has had any relations with
it except Japan and Soviet Russia, the latter only because of the
necessity due to the joint-controlled Chinese Eastern Railway.
The bandit situation in the eight months has grown worse
constantly. Joined by insurgents and national troops at times
Japan has had to pour so'diers into Manchuria to cope with the
c( nditions that threatened her own nationals and pro perty, more
than they had ever been threatened before September last.
Following the Manchurian invasion there came the war in
Shanghai. The act of Japan in Manchuria, in stirring the ire of
the Chinese and the outbreaks of Chinese in Shanghai against the
faranese residents there, an well as strengthening the resolve to
continue the anti-Japanese boycotts, was largely responsible for
the disastrous conflict that took place in the southern treaty port—
disastrous to Japan as well as to China. For the Shanghei war,
Japan must admit, cost her great toll in lives and finances, but
more important than these she must reckon the world prestige that
was lost.
And now because of her Manchurian policy and the desperate
imeans she has employed to put these policies in effect Japan finds
herself pointed toward what one observer in the north has seid to
be inevitable war with Russia. To say the least the atmoephere
along the border is intense and eventual conflict is not difficult
to imagine.
Within her own borders a chaetic politloal and
state of aftals exista, reflected in maay waye
only last S

=== Page 569 === (confidence 0.99)
Weighed from the negative viewpoint the results have been
far from satisfactory. On the positive side we find one thing
accomplished which Japan may consider well worth while—she
has driven for the time being at lenst Marshal Chang Hsueh-liang.
whom she held as her arch-cnemy, from the territory and thereby
banished his rule.
Naturally after these eight months with the situation as
it remains one wonders as to what the future holds. Certainly
it is no easy, pleasant task that confronts the Japanese militarists
if they remain fixed in their determination to achieve their
original objectives. That they realize this is indicated by their
latest announcement for the benefit of the League of Nations that
because of insurgent activities the withdrawal of troops is some-
thing hich cannot be considered even at this time.
As to China her hope for the future is with the Nations
represented by the League. At the start of the crisis she called
to the League for help, and with the exception of the stand of her
Nineteenth Route Army at Shanghai, she has continued to leave
her part to the League. What the League has in store for her
when the time comes to hear and act on the Commission yet in
Manchuria will be a most interesting world event to watch and
study.

It is obeious that present events um Maonchorna are methong but the
prac tical cartving out of the fitst step, of his program.
Pan-Japanese dreams of woild conquest will probably vanish as
Pan Germanic dreams did.
But the Tanaka project includes a mete practical side, an Imperial
project by no means ephemeral. It visualizes as frontiers: the Bering
Straits. Lake Baikal. Tibet. Australia-New Zealand, and Chatham
Island. The Japanese do not conceal their conception of China as a
horse on which to gallop over this imperial territory. Japan hears so
much in the papers about the feuds and difficulties among Western
powers and of their hatred for Soviet Russia that men like Tanaka
consider these projects casy of fulfilment.
Manchuria is, to Japan, a stepping stone. To Japan, Shanghai is
a pawn to bluff with.
The sudden evacuation of the great international port surprised
everyone.
One of its repercussions was the wave of terrorism culminating in
the assassination of President Inukai.
The young cadets ef the Army and Navy have organized the "Van-
guard of the Nation".
It is an ther name and expression for the pro-War paity.
Its aim in the present crisis is to use force in the name of H. M.
the Emperor to rid the government and Court of elements which to it
seem undesirable and after this destructiveness to build, again by use of
force, along the lines laid out by Tanaka, toward a Greater Japan.
Today's wired news tells that the Military of Japan intend taking
control of the nation's destiny.... which they are sure is a "mavifest"
destiny. The men who conceive it their life work to carry out Tanaka's
project are apparently steering the evacuated Shanghai troops into Man-
churia where events are rushing to a crisis.
U. S. S. R. seems to consider these reinforcements as a menace to
herseif.
In an editiorial in Isvestia which we are reprinting elsewhere, the
Bolshevist leader Karl Radek boldly says what he thinks:
"Our nation has at its command resources in men and material
much greater than those of Japan. In case of danger, we can arm
millions of men quite adequate for the defence of our frontiers.
And, foreseeing an American intervention, Rade

=== Page 571 === (confidence 0.99)
"Russia is on the qui vive, will not be caught napping. If provoked,
she will be justified in making temporary alliances with any capitalist
powers wlhich at present do not attack her frontiers or essential
interests"
That is a lot of tin can muslc, circus big drum internal political
propaganda, will answer those who only see in present events a chance
to trouble Russia and who imagine that the Soviet are suffering from
new difficulties.
But that answer is echo of a far too simple conception of internation:
al politics and of the Manchurian question in which the whole world is
interested both economically and otherwise.
Pan-Japanism is a danger for everybody.
It is about time the world wakes up to this fact.
A. N.

REGARDING: recognition to Manchukuo regime.
Survey of principal problems confronting the
Japanese Government in determining whether or
