=== Page 591 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
This telegram must be
closely paraphrased be-
TOKYO
fore being communioated
to anyone.
Dated June 20, 1932
Red'd 9 a. m.'
Secretary of State,
Washington.
161, June 20, noon.
In amplification of my telegramal55, June 13, 8 p.' m.
and 158, June 17, 7 p. m., and with reference to Depart-
ment!s 129, June 16, 6 p. m., I submit the following survey
of the principal problems which are confusing and perplexin?
the Japanese Government in determining whether or not .
extend early recognition to the Manchukuo regime. From the
changing tone of his various published press interviews,
and from other sources, I gather that Count Uohida is now,
after his conferences in Tokyo, by no means so certain of
the wisdom of that step as he was reported to be when he
came.
Onc. Establishment of the united administration for
Japan!s intcrests in Manchuria.
At a meeting on June 18 attonded by high officials of
the Ministrics of War, Foreign Affairs, Overseas Affairs
and

=== Page 592 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
2- #161, from : Tokyo, Junc 20, noon.
and Financo, a gencral plan is roported to have been agreed
upon. Points which were reportedly not sottled are the name
of tho new body, the method of deriving revenues to support
it, and the supervising authority (whcther the Promier or the
Overseas Ministry).
Tho chief of the new administration would probably be
a military officer. (General Muto is the only man so far
vto has been prominently mentioned as possible appointee to
this position). He would have directly under his authority
the Kwantung army, the Kwantung government, the South
Manchurian Railway and the Japanese consular representc
in Manchuria.
Two. The degree of independence to be granted. .
The prinoipal conflicts here, as in the other problems
enumerated in this summary, are between the Japanese
connected with the Manchukuo regime, who reportedly favor
a large degree of independence and freedom from any outside
control, and the Japanese having interests in Manchukuo,
commercial or otherwise, which the latter fear will be
endangered by too great a dogree of independence, particular-
ly if the question of extraterritoriality is involved.
Friotion

=== Page 593 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
3- #161, from Tokyo, June 20, noon.
Friction betwoon Goneral Honjo in Manohuria and General
Araki is said to be partly duo to this question and to be
responsible for some of the delay in settling it
(There are reasons to suspect that the unexpected plan
of Count Uchida to return to Dairen is partly conneoted
with an offort to settle the foregoing question).
Threc. Difficulties foroseen if Manchukuo is rccognized
by Japan
(a)- Criticism from foreign powers.
Japanese officials seem to vacillate between saying
that foreign opinion is vitally connected with thc problem
and that it should not be taken into consideration at all.
(b)- Necessity for a commercial treaty with Manchukuo.
The large Japanese commercial interests would favor a
preferential tariff but the Foreign Office sees danger in
possible interference with the "open door" polioy.
The question as to whether or not the Nine Power Treaty
has been violated would be brought sharply to the fore.
Four. Difficulties foreseen if Manchukuo is not rccog-
nized by Japan.
(a)- The popular demand in Japan for recognition of
Manchukuo.
The desire

=== Page 594 === (confidence 0.99)
REP
4- #161, from Tokyo, June 20, noon.
The dusire for eventual rocognition of Manchukuo is
wide-spread as evidenced by the Diet resolution.
Since September tho army has sold the idea of the
Manchurian cnterprise to the people on the basis of its
being an idealistic and humanitarian venture looking to
the recognition of a new state at the earliest possible
date. Failure to recognize would be misunderstood, the
pecpie having bscn carefully guarded agains any suspicion
that the present regime in Changchun was in any sense a
puppet government.
(b)- Faith to be kept with the goverment offi als
at Changchun.
It is expected by some Japanese that recognition will
remove suspicion of the motives of the Japanese from the
minds of the local Chinese who were persuaded to cooperate
with the Manchukuo by promises of recognition.
(o)- The taking over of the customs by Manchukuo and
a preferential tariff for Japan desired by some interests
would be difficult if not impossible.
The customs office at Dairen, being based on a treaty
with Ohina, presents greater difficulties than those at othes
ports in Manchuria both on account of administration and

5- #161, from Tokyo, June 20, noon.
volume cf trade.
(a)- The finanoial burden of the maintenarue of an
army in Manohuria.
Many Japanese conneoted with the Changchun regime,
including Ohashi, the Manchukuo Vice Minister for Foreign
Affairs, are reportedly in favor of the return to Japan
of most of the Japanese troops following reoognition
V and the granting of real independenoe. This it is felt
would instill confidence in the Chinese members of the
regime and discourage the opponents. The cost of the
Japanese soldiers who were retained might be borne largely
by thc Manchukuo regime.
(e)- The railway and land problems would, the Japanese
feel, continue in their present unsatisfaotory condition.

1/3

June 5, l932, referring to the altered political situa-
tion in Japan as a result of the establishment of a new
Cabinet with Visoount Saito as Prime Minister.
The editor of the PEKING & TIENTSIN TIMES wonders
whether either the Japanese or Chinese governments are
sufficiently strong to effect a compromise settlement
of the Manchurian situation and to obtain ratification
and enforcement of any agreement that might be reached.
He is of the opinion that the altered politioal situa-
tion

=== Page 601 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
tion in Japan has greatly improved the prospects of
successful negotiation, and that the time is now
favorable for a conference being held. The editor
of LE JOURNAL DE PEKIN, on the other hand, in his
usual cynical manner, seems to be of the opinion
that the policy of Japan vis-a-vis China has not
altered since the advent of the Saito Ministry.

The Free City of Shanghai would thus automatically make a match
with Manchukuo.
For Manchukuo is the keystone to Sauto's speech.
Sarto as ex governoi of Korea knows lhis colonization onions when
he says that the world cannot and should not ignore the existence of
Mannikinchuria.
It is upon this new State, according to the new Premier, dependant
upon it- development and power, that the peace of the world will be built.
Thus it is quite understandable that Tokyo and all its pacifistic
admirals and generals are bound to preoccupy themselves with Chang.
chun and to fight so that Henry Pu Yi may sit on the chaise percee of
China which takes the place of the throne of Henry Hsuan Tung.
Mr. Saito bec omes emotional when he contemplates such a future
Pax Japonica and Says.
I am firmly opposed to all impatience or rashness in our attitude
toward Manchuria.
Watning to amateurs :
"It is absolutely n cessary to bide our time before reaching a
conclusive solution of our present problems. This time is needed by
the Japanese aumy to combat the soldier-bandits in northern Manchuria
so as to protect Japanese residents."
In other words, since a Mannikinchurian army is in practice not to
be depended on, the Japanese soldiers are there for quite a while yet.
As to the Soviet Russians, the Saito Cabinet is firmly resolved to
respect their rights on the Chinese Eastern Railway.
But naturally the Cabinet could not prevent the Free State
(remember the Free Yangtze City) from following a divergent policy.
The League of Nations, Mr. Saito goes on to say, watches clossly
the events in the Far East and Japan, he says, has given its Commiskms
of Inquiry all necessary facilities.
But naturally Japan could not prevent the Free State (remember the
Free City) from following a quite different policy.
Japan had absolutely nething
ton Kooor
Che Commission in Muk
You are referred to Manchukuo
mission
arotests

=== Page 607 === (confidence 0.99)
Let us keep in mind that Article 11 gives the League Council the
right to make recommendations to the parties of a dispute, in their
presence and with their collaboration.
Article 15, on the contrary, allows the council to make recommenda-
tions without the collaboration of the parties.
If these resoluiions are unanimously adopted, the League Council is
expected to apply the prescribed sanctions.
In case of non-unanimity the parties would settle the difficulty
between themselves, in other words by war.
Well, in the case of Sino-Japanese affairs, war has been and is
being waged but war has settled none of the great outstanding problems.
War is not likely to settle them for years.
The position taken by Mr. Saito in the name of his government
remains what it was before his advent to power.
Japan continues to wish a special settlement of the Shanghai
questions and Question.
Meanwhile China resolutely insists on setling the Shanghai problems
together, integrally, with Manchurian problems.
China ignores the existence of Manchukuo.
Japan brings it up into view on all occasions while dodging mention
of its potential activities along the Chinese Eastern.
The Saito Cabinet is a fighting, army and navy, cabinet.
If the Shogunate, the Military High Command and the militarist
clique chose an admiral as Premier, it is to be able to tell bigger fish
stories to the little Everyman...or as we say in French, c'est pour mieux
emmener les gens en bateau.
A. N.

Department of State
The Honorable
The Jecretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
During recent years it has become apparent to foreign
observers of conditions and tendencies in Japan that there
has been a cradual decline in discipline in the country,
this decline being especially noticable among the younger
Army and Mavy officers and among the students in the higher
schools and universities. In the eiji era and extending
into the reign of the mperor Taisho, the standard of dis-
cipline in Japan was very high.
The nation then was faced
with a tremendous task - that of adapting Western civiliza-
tion to Japan in order to preserve the national existence 
and

=== Page 614 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
and the spirit of sacrifice carried the nation willingly
into submission to an unusually rigorous discipline.
From absolute loyalty to the mperor down through the
various grades of obedience to authority, the nation
maintained a level of unquestioning discipline which ex-
cited the admiration of the vorld.
Since the Great War,
however, there has been a cradual deterioration in obedience
to authority, culminating recently in a series of incidents
which drew the attention of students of Japan to the
changed conditions.
In Cctober, l9cl, an attempted coup a état by a group
of younc Arny officers was discovered and frustrated.
The
scheme vas practically to abolish the civilian authority
(which at that time was not enthusiastic over the Army's
anchurian venture) and to usurp the powers of covernment.
thile the plot apparently did not contemplate the overthrow
of the dymasty, certuin factors of the scheme indicated an
intention to control the Throne, as was done during the
Shogunate.
Lighteen Army officers, ranging in rank from
lieutenants to majors, were involved.
There was no direct
evidence indicating that hich Army officials were connected
with the plot, and the presumption is that the younger
officers acted on their own initiative, without authority
from their superiors.
On ay 15, 1932, a croup of Young Army and Navy officers
or cadets assassinated Premier Inukai and demonstrated
against various persons and orcanizations by throwing hand
grenades at public buildings and banks, while a group of
young farmers attempted to cut off the electric supply by
In this case also there
damaging the transformer stations.
was no direct evidence that high Army officials were im-
plicated

=== Page 615 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
plicated in the attempt at terrorization.
Unconfir ed storics of other acts of insubordination
and direct action on the part of the younger Army officers
are also current in Japan. ilitary operations in Lianchuria
and control of the now state of anchukuo are rumored to
be under the direction of a group of three young Army of-
ficers and not under the direction of General Honjo or of
Tokyo headquarters, as is cenerally believed.
Army units
are reported to have moved fron Horea into Manchuria under
the orders of their young cormanding officers, without
authorization from headquarters.
These incidents cannot be directly ascribed to a de-
terioration of discipline in the Aray and Navy;
the basic
causes lay much deeper and are due to the general discon-
tent wione the military und naval forces with the civil
authority and with the hich Arimy and Tavy commands, which
they feel are too cautious and too subservient to the civil
authority.
The fact that the incidents could occur, hov-
ever, and that the youn er o ficers could so far forget
their duties to the civil authorities and to their own
high officials as to take direct action in accordance with
their ovm views, shows plainly the extent to which discipline
in the Army, and to a smaller extent in the Iavy, has de-
teriorated.
This evidence of the decline of discipline
attracted much attention in the extraordinary session of
the Diet held in the early part of June, 1932, and numer-
ous interpellations were addressed to the inisters of
War and Navy on the subject. Because of the severe
criticism of the discipline in the Army, Lieutenant-
General Sadao Araki, the inister of War, felt constrained
to apologize for the actions of his subordinates - a
Addressing
practically unheard-of proceeding in Japan.
the

=== Page 616 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the House of Peers, he stated that 
"Among other things, I thought the Army ought to
act as the mainstay of the nation, and I pledged my
word with my colleagues that we will concentrate our
efforts to live up to the duty of the Imperial Army,
however dissatisfied we may be with the various phases
of social conditions both in this country and abroad.
"Despite all our efforts to this end, the deplor-
able incident occurred unexpectedly (the assassination
of Premier Inukai by young Arny officers), to the great
consternation of the public. In this connection, we
sincerely regret that eleven military cadets were found
to be involved in this incident.
Having been in the
position to supervise these cadets, we feel ashamed of
ourselves and tender our sincere apology for the in-
cident.
"Veedless to say, assassination is not permissible
for any reason.
. It can even be said, the fact that
men beloncing to the Imperial Army perpetrated such
an act undermines the prestige of the Imperial Army."
Discipline in the higher schools and universities has
been on the decline for some years. Strikes of students,
often on frivolous crounds, against their instructors and
school officials have been conmon. The day when students
treated their instructors with deferential courtesy and
exag erated respect, as the early writers on Japan relate,
seens to have passed. During the past year, moreover,
there have been startling disclosures of corruption among
the officials of some of the schools. The higher schools
of Japan are inadequate to acconmodate the large number of
students vho desire admission. Consequently the practice
has grown up of granting admission to the schools not be-
cause of scholarship but on payment to the schools of
considerable sums as contributions to alunni or other
funds. Likewise, contributions are often required in
order to obtain a diploma, or, in the case of a graduate,
in order to obtain a higher degree. These practices are
apparently fairly common in the private schools and also
obtain to some extent in the governmental institutions of
learning.
The various incidents and tendencies mentioned above
indicate a growing slackness of discipline and a breakdown
of

=== Page 617 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
of high ethical standards in the Japanese nation. The
causes of this deterioration are varied and complicated.
The older ceneration in Japan attributes the decline
of discipline (just as the older generation in every
country does) to deterioration of morals - to a sort of
rovolt of youth.
There is no doubt that the old standards
of morals and conduct, affected by modern conditions of
life, are undercoing a cradual change.
Radical thought,
despito all efforts of the police, flourishes in the higher
institutions of lcarning.
There is nore selfishness and
creed, and less high patriotic sentinent, among the younger
Ceneration than anonc the Japanese of the Meiji era.
The
older ceneration endeavors to renedy this deterioration of
thought by iproving moral teaching in the schools but
mates little offort to reach the basic causes.
The decline of discipline is sometimes laid to the
docay of thc old feudal institutions and virtues.
The
patriarchial and apprentice systems, with their stern dis-
cipline and ideals of mutual loyalty and self-abnegation,
are cradually breaking up under the attacks of the modern
econonic systen.
The cood of the individual rather than
the velfare of the family or the clan is being sought by
the youncer ceneration.
With the decay of family discipline
Coes the decay of all discipline.
The reaction acainst the tremendous efforts of the
nation during the .eiji era is also ascribed as a cause of
the ceneral deterioration of discipline.
In the Leiji era
it was necessary for the nation to exert itself to the utmost
in order to attain to a place of power in the family of
nations, and every Japanese subject was expected to sacri-
fice himself if necessary to enable the nation to reach
its goal.
The Japanese now feel that Japan has arrived,
that

=== Page 618 === (confidence 0.97)
-6-
that it has reached a hich place in the co munity of
nations, and that creat efforts are no longer necessary.
There is a ceneral feeling of self-satisfaction and self-
esteem, vith a corresponding relaxation of effort and
discipline.
The casier living since the Creat War, when
Japan retched a hei ht of affluence never dreamed of by
the Japanese of the lei ji era, has tended to inerease the
feolin of self-sutisfaction and accomplishment and has
rendered less necessary the rigorous discipline and strong
efforts of carlier duys.
Perhaps the principal cause of the decline of dis-
cipline in modern Ja an, hovever, is the corruption in
hi  pluces, which has naturally tended to kill much of
the innate Ju anose ros ect for authority.
Since the
rout ar the civil authority has for a large part of the
time been in the hands of party politicians, who, to an
even creater entont than is true in other countries, place
their omm welfore and that of their parties before the
velfare of the nation.
They are subservient to the
dictatoa of the great financial interests, who manipulate
legislation for their om benefit. A striking example of
this was the supposedly eliberate smashing of the iinseito
Cabinet in Decenber last, in order that the Seiyukai party
might come into power and replace the cold embargo, thus
depressing the yen and making millions for their financial
friends who had sold yen and bought dollars. Such exhibitions
of corruption can only end in an entire loss of respect for
the civil authority, and with loss of respect naturally
Coes a decline of discipline.
High Army and Navy officials,
moreover, sometimes join with the party politicians in their
intrigues, thus becoming tarred with the same brush and
causing

=== Page 619 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
causing a detcrioration of respect and discipline in
their services.
The decline of discipline is expressing itself in
various forms of direct action and in so-called "fascist"
movements - novements which contain some socialistic ele-
ments and which differ ontirely from the Italian fascist
movenent in theat they cxpress disorder and indiscipline
rath r than order and discipline.
t present a croup of
voun orficers, vithout international cxperience or
knovledge of nationel economy ana finance, is more or
less in control in Japan and is conductinc the campaign
to control  anchuria. The older, wiser nen who formerly
controllod affairs in Jupan and who advocated a policy of
yationt, cradual development, both in the country and in
its foreign relations, huve been relegated to the back-
Cround.
They can only watch and hope that the young,
head-strone und undiseiplinod men in control today will
not lead the nation into a major catastrophe. They do
not anticipate a revolution vithin the country, because,
as one ola statosman pressed it, "The Japanese people are
sort of foolish, and very patient."
They do fear, however,
irreparable damace to the nation's finances and to its
intornational prestice.
The present situation in Japan, with its social un-
rest, disturbed political conditions and break-down of
discipline, perhaps can best be compared with the situation
at the time of the Restoration, when somewhat similar con-
ditions prevailed.
The breakdorm of the feudal system
released thousands of undisciplined, swaggering samurai on
the country, no adequate political system had yet been es-
tablished, and the transfer of power from the Shogun to
the Emperor and the opening of the country to foreign
intercourse, with the resultant introduction of new ideas,
had

=== Page 620 === (confidence 0.96)
-8-
had created creat social unrest.
At this time of crisis,
a roup of youne loaders, who later became the Genro, arose
and imposed upon the country the stern discipline and ideals
of patriotic sacrifice vhich not only carried the nation
through the crisis but made possible the great development
of the eiji era.
Japan today is again faced with extremely serious
problens - more serious than any which have arisen since
those of the period of the estoration. Joth internally
and in its forei n relations the nation is confronted
with problens which may have far-reaching effects upon the
future progress of the country. The nation again needs
strone leaders, such as those who arose after the Restoration,
who vill impose upon the people higher standards of discipline
und of service to the nution. To such leaders are in sight
at present - certainly not mong the young men who are lead-
ing the nation into bankruptcy and into international isola-
tion. In the moantime the unrest und discontent with present
conditions in Japan are steadily deepening.

=== Page 629 === (confidence 0.94)
AN OPEN LETTER TO THE MEMBERS OF THE LEAGUE OF ATIONS
COMISSION OF INQUIRY
pecial Attention is drawn to our Seven Requests
as published in this Letter.
Statement by the Shiunso, Tokyo.
T
 pmember of he League  Satons om
devclop
1111
pdoatie
ard ourud ta 
carrsing on vour or of ovestigation in China.
Accordingly, we thought it fitting and proper to
present to you the real and frank views of the
Japanese people-views that are at variance with the
objective attitude shown by our diplomatic officials-
hoping that you will profit by this unreserved expres-
sion of our views and sentiments.
Gentlemen: Lei us state the stand of this coun-
try in a nutshell: regardless of the nature of your
report on the matters on which you have investigated,
Japan never will retreat a single inch from our
acquired position so far as our rights and interests
in Manchuria and Mongolia are concerned. Should
you erroncously judge the real views of the Japanese
people because of the foolish attitude of our Govern-
ment officials, you would be misleading the Western
nations and thereby endangering the peace of the Far
East.
CANNOT WITHDRAW
Gentlemen: Our words may fall on your ears
harsh and uncourteous. But let it bc understood that
we are simply speaking the truth. Please bear in
mind my explanation as to why the people of Japan
show such a firm and unshakeable stand regarding
their posithpm in Manchuria and Mongolia. Uatl
little ove

=== Page 630 === (confidence 0.99)
in these lone islands in Asiatic waters; and it was
the United States and other Western countries that
coaxed our country to abandon her policy of seclusion
and enter into relations with outside countries. This
change in her national policy was followed by the
adoption of modern industrial systems and the con-
sequent increase in her population. She was thus
placed in a position in which her overseas economic
expansion was inevitable. As you are well aware,
however, all the doors of opportunity have been
closed against us. The United States in the east ex-
cludes our immigrants; Australia in the south also
bars the entry of our people; Africa in the west re-
fuses to recognize the right of our nationals to
engage in commerce in its territory; and even French
Indo-China is prepared to reject our nationals. Thus.
the only territory that can absorb the surplus popu-
lation of Japan is Manchuria and Mongolia in which
she has secured special rights and interests through
treaties by virtue of her victories in the wars with
Russia and China. It is not only in terms of our
national defense that Manchuria and Mongolia are
held as our "life line" We have no doubt that you
will accept as natural and just the conviction of our
people that "Japan, under no cireunstances, will
retreat from her acquired position so far as Man
churia and Mongolia are concerned."
WILL RECOGNIZE MANCHUKUO
Gentlemen: There is another point to which we
wish to call your attention. Regardless of the present
vacillating attitude of our Government, it is a fore-
gone conclusion that Japan will extend recognition
'to the new State of Manchuria in the near future.
We hope you will pass a right judgment regarding
this matter and will not waste time and efforts over it.
Some argue that Japan's recognition of the new
State of Manchuria would violate Article 1 of the
Nine-Rower Treaty: "All the Powers, excepting
China, shall respect her independence and territorial
and administrative integrity." On this point, we need
not refer to the editorial views of the London Times
that the Washington Treaty merely expressed an
ideal. Moreover, Manchuria even at that time was
under an independent and autonomous political
power, so that it is highly absurd to criticize Japan's
act of recognizing the new State of Manchuria by
referring to a provision of the Nine-Power Treaty.
Almost simultaneously with the Washington
Treaty, the late Marshal Chang Tso-lin, who was the
master of the Three Eastern Provinces, publicly
clared the autonomy of his provinces. It is kno
throughout the world that he refused to obey
orders of the Central Govarnmer

=== Page 631 === (confidence 0.99)
in the name of the Mukden Government. Moreover,
Article 2 of the same treaty stresses the advisability
of "giving to the Chinese people every opportunity
for establishing a firm and stable government." Thus,
Japan would be acting in absolute comformity with
this provision, when she assists the State of Man-
churia which has come into being at the voluntary
decision of the 30,000,000 people of the region who
had suffered under the despotism of the Chinese
militarists for many years. Those who doubt the
truth of the above argument should only remember
the fact that when the United States protested to
Japan regarding the Chinchow situation last January,
Britain and other countries rejected the American
wish to follow her example.
Ai no time during the 4,000 years of Chinese
history have Manchuria and Mongolia ever been com-
pletely controled by South China. Even under the
powerful sway of the First Empetor of the Chin
dynastry, China was unable to extend her control be
yond the Great Wall.
We believe that you have yourselves seen
in your tour through Manchuria that no safety can
be assured of Japanese lives and property there unless
this country extend recognition to that country.
After having seen that banditry stalks in broad day-
light in the region which Japan regards as her "life
line", you are now in a position to testify from your
own experience to the danger which those delegates
of the members of the League of Nations who have
never been in the Far East may undergo in discussing
Manchurian affairs. You yourselves must be of the
opinion that the existing circumstances force Japan
to recognize the Manchurian State without delay.
In this connection, let us see how promptly the
United States recognized the Republic of Panama. It
was on November 3, 1903, that the Republic of
Panama declared its independence and, three days
later, namely, November 6, it was given recognition
by the United States. Five days later, namely, on
November 11, the United States gave Panama de jure
recognition. T:e promptitude and audacity shown
by the United States form a sharp contrast with
Japan's slow-thinking and stubborn honesty.
Gentlemen: You have already visited Shanghai
and Peiping and observed the state of affairs in these
points. Now, we shall take this opportunity of ex-
pressing our views concerning China in general,
CHINA'S OLD GAME
In our opinion, China's greatest national calamity
lies in its traditional diplomatic axioms

=== Page 632 === (confidence 0.99)
devil against the other." Both are crafty diplomatic
policies and are based upon the spirit of dependence
on outside help. In other words, because of this
traditional reliance on outside help in her diplomatic
affairs, China has, since olden times, regarded coun-
tries at a far distance as being just and her neighbors
as unjust. She would flatter the former and prove
unfaithful to the latter to the estreme in both cases.
To defend its welfare without self endeavors this
has been China's dipfomatic essence. To call other
countries good or bad according to the nature of
their respective distances to China is absurd enough,
on the basis of her policy of expediency. And
it is a pity that the Chinese people should still
consider this to be the best poliey of preserving their
national independence, and fail to realize the fact
that it is the principal cause of internal as well as
external disturbances and of the consequent miseries
of the 100,000,000 inhabitants of China. The adage
that tidelity is the foundation of human life is
eternally true as regards individuals as well as States.
Should States be able to prosper when they have no
fidelity, one may rightly believe that all the religions
of the past have been false and that the civilisation of
the world is the work of the Satan.
The disastrous consequences of China's unfaith
ful and crafty diplomatic policy are most conspieu
ously seen in her own history. Sung made an
alliance with Yuan against its neighbor, Chin, but
was conquered by Yuan in consequence. Ching re-
covered Lioatung Peninsula from Japan through the
intercession of three European Powers, but shortly
afterwards lost the same peninsula to Russia and thus
the beginning of her territorial losses were made.
China soon lost Wei-hai-wei, Kuanchow Bay, and
Chiaochow Bay. Such invariably would be the result
of any diplomatic policy devoid of fidelity and de-
pendent on outside power.
CAN'T TRUST THE CHINESE
It is only too natural that lack of fidelity also
characterizes Chinese citizens. When their State
lacks this virtue, no one can expect its citizens to
possess it. One cannot trust what the Chinese authori-
ties say, for their words often belie their actions.
Nor can one judge China's to-morrow from what she
does today. Its citizens can never be trusted because
of their changeableness and lack of fidelity. They
would not hesitate to adopt all objectionable tactics in
order to achieve their sordid ends. The results of all
this are seen in China's constant political disturbances
and in the unspeakable rampancy of her militarists:
All the evils in China come from the lack of fdelity
in the State and its citizens. And we truly pity our
western neighbor who adheresto

=== Page 633 === (confidence 0.99)
diplomatic policies of enmity and craftiness——what
one may call "opium diplomacy"—instead of doing
away with it. We wonder whether or not this view
of ours is shared by you who have already traveled
in China, having visited such places as Shanghai and
Peiping.
Gentlemen: In the light of what we have set
forth above, we greatly doubt whether or not the
United States, in whom China puts her trust as her
principal supporter. is really a good friend of China.
Everyone is well aware of the fact that American
support has had the effect of encouraging Chinese
atrocifies. Nor can we believe anything beneficient
will result from the wrong kind of backing shown by
the United States to China. Should any benefit come
from such an expression of American friendship, one
may truly say that the farmers of the world can
"gather grapes from the thorns and figs from thel
thistles." Surely, no such miracle will lappen in this
world of ours. We are firmly convinced that China
can never be saved unless she realizes that the good-
will shown by the I'nited States is leading her into
deeper waters and unless she reconstructs her diplo-
matic policy from the very foundation, basing it on
the eternal principle of fidelity and on the spirit of
self-reliance. We wonder whether this opinion of
ours is not shared also by you, gentlemen.
MIS-PLACED SENTIMENT
Gentlemen: You should consider whether or
not the attitude of China and the United States to-
wards Japan regarding the Manchurian problem is not
prejudicial to world peace which these two countries
ostensibly clamor for. China, which asks America's
assistance in all difficult cases, has been showing a
united front—officials and citizens—in carrying on an
anti-Japanese boycott. The United States, too, bas
been advocating an anti-Japanese boycott after the
Manchurian Incident. It is certainly highly absurd
that these disturbers of world peace should criticize
Japan's military operations in Manchuria, especially
in view of the fact that our military operations have
been necessitated by self-defense. Can anything be
more inconsistent than to advocate peace, on the one
hand, and to agitate an economic boycott against a
nation, on the other? You are called upon to con-
sider this problem to the full extent.
Gentlemen: We believe that you agree with us
regarding the matters we have above discussed.
Supposing that you agree with us, the following
points must be the results of your inspection in Man-
churia:

=== Page 634 === (confidence 0.99)
1. That Japan never will retreat from her
acquired position in Manchuria under
all circumstances.
2.
That Japan should justly recognize
Manchuria in the near future.
3.
That the principal source of China's
troubles and miseries is traced to her
traditional diplomatic policy of decep-
tion and distrust.
That China would never be able to
4.
attain her temporal salvation unless
she replaces her traditional national
policy by a new policy of fidelity and
self-reliance.
10 d10.
5.
That the injudicious friendship shown
by the United States towards China
would cause the latter further troubles
and calamities.
6.
That both China's anti-Japanese boy-
cott and the American proposal for an
anti-Japanese economic disruption are
unpardonable crimes against the peace
of. the world.
7. That the past attitude of the League
of Nations has only obstructed a just
solution of Far Eastern problems.
We have no doubt that you will use your great
influence in rectifying the past errors of the League
of Nations. If you should succeed in forming right
views of the Far Eastern situation; rectify the mis-
taken stand of the League of Nations; assist China in
adopting a fundamental policy of fidelity; and even-
tually lead the nations of the world to recover mutual
trust and faith among themselves-then, your r
tour of inspection in the Far East would be an
of immense significance in the history of mank

Dear Mr. Secretary:
SECRETARY'S OFFICE
843.01
894.00/
Since my letter to you of July l6, there have been few
important political developments. The various formal and
informal representations made to the Foreign Office by my
British, French and Italian colleagues concerning interference
3
with the Chinese Maritime Customs and concerning the impendin
recognition by Japan of "Manchukuo" have been regularly re-
2
ported to you by cable.
So many different issues and conflicting interests and
influences are involved in the question of extending full re-
cognition to "Manchukuo" that it is impossible to predict with
any certainty when that step will be taken. My French coll-
eague, who is perhaps as sound in his views and as well in-
formed as any of the foreign representatives here, believes
that recognition will be postponed at least until after the
League of Nations shall have taken formal action on the Lyttoh
Report, and Herriot has informed him that this acti will
R
The Honorable
1933
Henry L. Stimson,
Secretary of State,
Nashington.

=== Page 654 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
not take place until the end of November at earliest.
fact that the French Government has, after long uneertainty,
granted the Ambassador leave of absence with permissien to
visit France indicates that they expect no important develop-
ments during the nert two or three months.
My own feeling is that while the Japanese Government at
present expects to extend full recognition to "Manchukuo" in
due course, it is decidedly apprehensive as to the findings
and tone of the eventual Lytton Report and the effect which
it may have on world opinion, and I am inclined to believe
that the various expressions of'opinion which have been allowed
to reach the press, through Shiratori and others, that full
recognition of "Manchukuo" may be delayed indefinitely, in-
dicate a policy of "watchful waiting" until such time as the
attitude of the League of Nations becomes known. Shiratori,
when he speaks for himself, is not inclined to use such gentle
terms as he adopted in his press conferences with the foreign
correspondents on July 16 and 18, reported in my telegram
l90. In that particular case it looked very much as if he
had been given his cue by his superiors. There are also,
of course, various unsettled issues of an administrative
nature which tend to delay a definite decision as to the date
of recognition. Therefore, while Count Uchida himself has
consistently adhered to his statement to the Lytton Commission

=== Page 655 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
that the matter of recognition is settled and is not open
to discussion, it may well be that the other less official
utterances which have found their way into the press give
a more accurate impression of the way the wind is blowing.
All of this is obscure and problematical. We must not lose
sight of the fact that certain factors, such as the fanatical
military element, are always present and may at any moment
force the hand of the more moderate members of the Government
by force or otherwise.
In this general connection, a prominent peer recently
said to us that Viscount Saito is steadily getting control of
the various elements in the Government and will probably last
longer than was at first expected, and he added: "Strange
as it may seem, there are some sensible men in the Army, but
they are not the ones who do the public talking".
The long telegram received by the Embassy on July 26
from the "Foreign Minister of Manchukuo" concerning the Chinese
Customs was, so far as I can ascertain, sent identically to
the diplomatic missions of all of the Powers having interests
in the disposal of the customs revenues.
I felt it desirable
to cable it to you in full, since a resumé often obscures the
tone of such a communication, and I likewise cabled it to Mr.
Johnson in Peking in case he should not have received it and

=== Page 656 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
should wish to send you his comments. As I am now informed,
the telegram was not sent directly to the missions in Peking.
My French and Belgian colleagues consider the message to be
"fair and reasonable". The British Ambassador is absent in
the mountains.
Our only despatch in this pouch which could be of parti-
cular interest to you is a report on the irrepressible Mr.
Shiratori, the "Spokesman of the Foreign Ofrice", whom we may
characterize as the enfant terrible of Tokyo.
Respectfully yours,

Mexico's stirictly confidential des-
patch No. 1797 of August 22, 1932, encloses

of a conversation between the Ambassador
and the Japanese Minister. The Japanese

in Japan is completely dominant; that com-
munism is growing rapidly; that the

doubtful benefit to Japan; and in general

=== Page 659 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to despatch No. 1797
of August 22, 1932,
from the Embassy at Mexico City.
(COPY)
CONFIDENTIAL.
MEMORANDUI OF CONVERSATION
THE AIBASSADOR
Mexico, August 19, 1932,
With the Japanese Minister, Mir. Yoshiatsu Hori.
At dinner last evening (at Ir. illiam Fraser's)
I had quite a long talk with the Japanese Minister
regarding conditions in Japan.
The substance of the conversation was as follows:
The Iinister expressed himself as being very ap-
prehensive about conditions in Japan, both political
and econonic.
As to the political conditions, he indicated that
Japan was in a condition of uncertainty and change;
that the military party was completely dominant and
tolerated no interference or criticism from either of
the other parties; that the communist party was
growing rapidly, and was recruiting its members not
from the lower classes, but from the young college
graduates belonging to the responsible classes of
Japanese society; that whereas in the older days the
civilian statesmen had been able to hold, by their
wisdom, the military party in check, there seemed now
to be no such statesmen in Japan, and the situation
was very much disturbed. He expressed himself as
being very apprehensive of the irmediate developments.
On the economic side, the Minister stated that
Japan was suffering from the depression primarily in
two ways: First, from the fact that the farmers were
receiving a very low price for their rice crop; and
second, from the fact that the exportation of silk
had been cut down very much owing to the depression
in the United States. He indicated that these factors
were causing unrest in Japan. He pointed out that
they were now making artificial silk in Japan and
exporting that abroad.
He spoke of the military operations (in Manchuria)
as being very expensive, and of doubtful benefit to
Japan. He said that there was much talk of "mass" im-
migration taking place, and if it did occur, he did
not see what benefit it would be to Japan, since the
Japanese would merely raise more rice in Manchuria,
and there was little or insufficient market for the
surplus

=== Page 660 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
surplus of rice which they were already raising in
Japan.
Regarding the Manchurian operations, his attitude
appeared to be one of disapproval, as also his attitude
towards the present foreign policy of Japan as dominated
by the militaristic party.
He spoke of the relations between Russia and Japan
and stated it was impossible for Japan to break off
diplomatic relations with Russia, because of the
Japanese fishery industries which netted Japan about
thirty million yen a year. He stated that the Rus-
sians were constantly making trouble for the Japanese
fishermen in various ways, but principally through the
Russian Governnent's support (in money) of Russian
fishermen who bid in competition with Japanese fisher-
men for the right to fish off the Siberian coast. He
stated that under the Portsmouth Treaty provisions,
Japanese fishermen had to bid for the rights to fish
on the Siberian coast, and that the cost of securing
these rights was becoming increasingly great. He
stated that the fish caught on the Siberian coast -
principally salmon - were not consumed in Japan, but
were exported to the United States and Europe; that
the Russians were establishing competitive canning
plants; that in London, Japanese canned salmon com-
peted with Russian canned salmon; but that there were
sone reports indicating that perhaps the points at
difference between the Japanese and Russian govern-
ments concerning fisheries had been ironed out, and
that a new arrangement would be made.
I asked the Minister how the Japanese were get-
ting alon in Formosa, and he stated that govern-
mentally Formosa was now practically self supporting.
(Initialed) J. . C.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 25, 1932.
Digut.
An article entitled "The Far East" in THE ROUND TABLE
(a British magazine) for June, 1932, is summarized below:
894.00/431
Since September 18, 1931, the Far East has been the
centre of the international stage and it would have been
well if such had been the case sooner, as it is on the western
shores of the Pacifio that there must be faced the greatest
threat to the system which has been built up to avert another
world war.
The basi factors in the situation concern China primarilo
The political and disarmament agreements of the Washington Con-
ference, of which the Nine Power Treaty was one of the most
important, were strictly interdependent. The Nine Power Treaty
forms the basis of British policy not only in China but through-
out the ar East, as British relations with Japan must be in-
fluenced at almost every point by the British policy towards
China. The question asked and left open by the Washington
Conference is how the contact between the ancient culture of
China which has persisted for thousands of years and Western
civilization can be turned to good account.
N
During the period from 1842 to 1922 British policy in
hina
P
was
one for the furtherance of British trade and no atfempt
S
was made to extend British political power or to promote mutual
N
understanding, although in 1898 the British Prime Minister
(Lord Salisbury) realizing that political relations between
China

=== Page 662 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
China and the West could not continue as they were, but seeing
in China only another Turkey, tried to obtain a fair share for
Great Britain and attempted negotiations with Russia to that
end. This was followed in l899 by Secretary Root's enunciation
of the "open door" policy, which, in spite of difficulties and
complications has been the broad basis of the policy of the
Powers since that time. There have been no new alienations of
Chinese territory although in 1905 Japan succeeded to Russia's
rights in Manchuria. However, Japan's object was not territor-
ial aggrandizement but to keep Russia at a safe distance in
order to assure to Japan free access to the resources of China
including Manchuria. At the time Marquis Ito remarked, "It was
unprofitable for any country to endeavour to go beyond those
limits which appear to have been set by nature to its powers;
to do so was to open a source of weakness", and for that reason
Japan "neither wanted Manohuria, nor was she strong enough to
maintain large garrisons indefinitely on the remote borders of
that province." Marquis Ito said also that England and Japan
were fitted to work together in China ag the former had been
the first to open up China and the latter understond the Chi-
nese better than any other foreigners did.
Much has changed since that time but there still remain
the indestructibility of China, the economio needs of Japan
and the fact that few foreigners understand the mind and soul
of China. Neither do they understand the mind and soul of Japan,
whose history and organization are next described briefly. The
sum and substance of Japan's foreign policy is the poverty of
the country in natural resouroes and the rapidly growing popu-
lation.

=== Page 663 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3 -
In order to keep pace with the growing population Japan
has but two alternatives - emigration and industrialization,
the former being excluded either for political or climatie
reasons. Because of the lack of natural resources Japan
has a greater interest than any other nation in international
justice, that is, not only the enforcement of existing rights,
but also the development of an orderly means for remedying
the maldistribution of wealth. Whether the issue is called
"justice" "necessity" or "self-preservation", the logio of
facts remains inexorable and during the past twelve years
statesmen at Geneva have barred discussion of these vital
international problems, which are working themselves out to-
day in Shanghai and Manchuria - tomorrow perhaps elsewhere.
Japan's economic problem ban find no relief by political
action in China and Manchuria. Industrialized Japan's ex-
ports must be inoreased. China possesses a powerful weapon
in the boycott. The 30,000,000 purchasers in Manchuria
could not take the place of China's 400 millions, to say
nothing of the luxury purchasers in the United States. Thus
it may be noted that China, although unorganized and inter-
nally disturbed, is more powerful than the highly militarized
Japan; that Japan is vitally interested in the establishing
of good relations with China on a durable basis which must
be a basis insuring self-respect for both countries; and that
aside from the immediate problom of Sino-Japanese relations
the Far Eastern situation has disclosed general issues of
international economic policy which the world can no longer
afford to shirk.
The

=== Page 664 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
The Sino-Japanese conflict puts to the severest test to
which it has been subjected the colleotive system made up
of the Covenant of the League of Nations and the Kellogg
Pact, the object of which is to establish the rule of law in
the world. Japan, by violating agreements, on and since
September 18 has jeopardized the peace sottiement in the Far
East and also the naval agreement of 1922 which was interde-
pendent with the Far Eastern political arrangements. Although
it is important that war has not actually been declared, the
fact remains that, morally, a state of war exists and peace
remains to be restored. The action of the League since Sep-
tember 18 indicates the emergence of a new technique for the
working of the collective system which may make possible the
breaking of the deadlock of the past twelve years caused by
the conflict for priority between security on the one hand
and disarmament and treaty-revision on the other. The doo-
trine of collective non-recognition, coupled with/voluntary
economic sanctions enforced by public opinion may provide a
weapon against an aggressor no less formidable than military
measures. The League Commission of Inquiry has a historio
opportunity and a task which involves in fact a revision of
existing treaties.
Great Britain has a unique part to play in shaping
events in China which presents the greatest political prob-
lem of our age, a problem in essence not political but
educational.

September 16, 1932.
I set down my reactions to Minister Johnson's telegram
of September 7, 1932, as of possible interest to you. The
8
6
Minister suggested that (1) if the opportunity should arise
.00/43
at Geneva, the United States should propose an international
discussion of the Far Eastern situation, at which (2) recog-
nition of Japan's special concern in the Far East might help
allay Japan's growing hostility toward the United States and
2
other Western powers. The Minister thinks that owing to our
9
position in the Far East we should take the lead in (3)
preventing Japan's destruction by the forces that are carrying
her at present. The Minister finds evidence (4) of a tendency
among Japan's present leaders to turn away from the West and
894.0c
seek a solution of Japan's problems in Asia. The Minister
observes (5) that after Japan has recognized "Manchoukuo",
she will be committed to defending invasion by arms.
(1) Suggested Far Eastern conference. The suggestion
is a good one, but the time for it is not opportune. The
League should have the opportunity to carry out plans  or
19
dealing with the question before other measures arinstituted.
The main objection to a new conference at this time, however,
is its inopportuneness. It must be realized that Japan has
P
S
achieved the aim of one if not two generations and will not
/KN
at this time be moralized or talked out of her purpose, and
that there is no peaceful solution of the Manchuria question
other

=== Page 666 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
other than to accept the situation that Japan has oreated.
A Japanese bureaucracy and complete Japanese control of
economic activity have been established in Manchuria, and
Japan has a new rich domain to exploit to her heart's content.
The present leaders of Japan bank the future of the n&tion on
their achievement. These rulers of Japan frankly assert that
they will not yield any of their gains in Manchuria except to
superior force, and there is every reason to believe them.
Any suggestion concerning a further conference at this time
therefore will not only do no good, no nation being disposed
to use force, but will be a needless and undignified gesture
of conciliation.
The time will come, however, for a conference as suggested
by the Minister, and if it is not the Japanese themselves who
propose it, they will at least be in a receptive mood for it.
This will be when the retention of Manchuria and perpetuation
of the "Mancrpukuo" fiction definitely prove inexpedient and
unprofitable. Factors which will lead Japanese generally to
recognize the inexpediency of their present policy will be
(1) Sustained Chinese armed and economic resistance;
(2) Non-recognition of "Manchoukuo" by the leading
powers, the United States particularly, and
an uncompromising legal attitude with regard
to their substantive rights in Manchuria under
treaties with China.
It was determined Russian "Partisans", the Nikolaievsk
massacre, and the high cost of Siberian occupation that made
the Siberian expedition unpopular in Japan. Likewise, long
drawn out Chinese guerilla warfare (which at present indicates
an

=== Page 667 === (confidence 0.97)
-3 -
an improvement in technique), continued extraordinary expend-
itures for "The Manchurian Affair", and unceasing economic
and political difficulties with China will wear out the
patience and cool the patriotism of the Japanese people.
Already signs of indifference to the Manchuria campaign
are reported in Japan. A university professor in Formosa
noted and commented on it, and General Muto on leaving Tokyo
for Manchuria exhorted the Japanese people to continue their
support of the Japanese Army in Manchuria. Mr. Matsuoka,
who recently returned from a tour of Manchuria and who
will be Japan's special delegate at Geneva in October,
called upon the people of Japan to show more interest in
Manchuria. The Japanese press indicates that the great mass
of the people are more concerned with their present individual
difficulties than with foreign policy. The Finance Minister
has repeatedly intimated that the nation is barely solvent,
and there is deep unrest among the farming and fishing
population of Japan that practically nothing was done for
their relief at the last Diet. The endurance of the Japanese
people may be superior to that of other races, but even
7
the Japanese have a limit, and that limit cannot be so far
off.
Non-recognition of "Manchoukuo", with the moral censure
of Japan which it carries, and unyieldingdiplomatio attitude
toward new arrangements in Manchuria by the principal powers,
may

=== Page 668 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
may not injure Japan concretely, but they will stiffen and
prolong Chinese resistance and incidentally help to unite
(and perhaps regenerate) China. As for the United States,
we might consider closing our Harbin and Mukden consular
and trade commiss ioner's offices, as we did at Tsingtao
during Japanese occupation. It is believed that at that
time Consul Adams of Tsingtao was officially assigned to
the Tsinan Consulate but remained in Tsingtao to care for
government
the property, performing no official services.
(2) Japan's growing hostility to the United States and
Europe. The Japanese press does not indicate any real
hostility toward the West. Occasional irritation with
Mr. Stimson is expressed, but the feeling of editors is
rather that the attitude of Western people is due to
ignorance of the Manchurian question. Venom is totally
lacking, and there is none of the challenging hostility, for
instance, that characterized the Japanese press attitude
toward the anti-Japanese agitation in California in l907-ll.
In fact, the tone of the press is sometimes apologetic, and
generally explanatory in referring to Manchurian developments.
In any event, it is significant that while the army is
speeding up munitions output for some eventuality, the navy,
which is the nation's weapon for use against the west, is
not getting any particular reinforcement.
(3) Japan's Possible Self-Destruction. Assuming that
wO

=== Page 669 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
we feel genuinely concerned about.this possibilfty, it does not
seem that the Uni ted States can do anything to prevent
events taking their course unless Japan is receptive to
our good offices.
(4) "Back to Asia" Movement. It is difficult to take
this movement seriously. Some Japanese reactionaries may
play with the idea out of ignorance or because of resent-
ment with the West, but no solution of Japan's problems is
to be found in Asia. With our resources and lerge popula-
tion, it is possible to be self-sustaining and prosperous,
but Japan's standard of living and cultural advancement
depend on her trade and close relations with the civilized
world. The "Pan-Asiatic" sentiment may have its followers,
but the mass of the educated people are undoubtedly more
interested in Olympic Games and the science and progress of
the West. The "Back to Asia" movement represents an
inferiority complex, a psychological leaning in the direction
of the least resistance which is not in keeping with the
Japanese character.
(5) Defense of "Manchoukuo". Japan is committed to
the defense of "Manchoukuo" for the time being and to
recognition of "Manchoukuo" as an independent state. These
developments certainly complicate the situation, but they
will last only so long as they are profitable or expedient
or hold promise of advantage of some sort. Other nations
have

=== Page 670 === (confidence 0.90)
have made mistakes of this nature and backed out grace-
fully: the British in their support of the Greeks in Asia
Minor, the Allied support of Denikin, Wrangel and other
anti-Bolshevik régimes, the Japanese support of the Semenoff
Government in Vladivostok, et cetera.

situation which those forces have created. It is
better that we deal with it now by the methods of
peace than later by the methods of war which I believe
otherwise to be inevitable.

Dr. Shotwell regarded as very important the proposal of
Poland for moral disarmament, which was turned over to a
little known committee of the League - the Committee on In-
tellectual Cooperation. In making clear the meaning of moral
disarmament, Dr. Shotwell emphasized that there must be an ef-
fort to stop the work of war fomenters, that such individuals
should be considered as committing a crime through violating
the peace, and that there should be a detective censorship of
the press, movies, et cetera, in order to prevent propaganda
which would lead to international misunderstanding, bad feeling
and eventually war.
Dr. Shotwell said that a draft treaty
of moral disarmament was drawn up which recommended that eduoa-
in the schoolo
tional authorities should provide for the study of the struc-
ture of international peaoe and the peaceful settlements of
study
disputes in the schools and that this thought be injected into
the theatres, movies and other instruments which reach the
public.
Dr. Shotwell stressed that the world must be prepared
mentally for arbitration and that it must mow the difference
between conciliation and arbitration before disarmament can
safely be undertaken. There is a need for international civics
and the League should not be judged as the headquarters of
police. The test of success and failure is whether there exists
a just method of adjudication of disputes. We should concen-
trate on reform making treaties instruments of justice.
EGC

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 30, 1933.
American-Japanese Relations: Possible
Means of Improving.
894.00/431-5
The American press of January 29, 1933, carries an
Associated Press article from Tokyo under date line
January 28 with a heading as follows:
"Entire Tokyo Navy To Maneuver Near American Islands"
"Annual Operations Will Be Held Two Months Earlier
Than Usual."
"War Games Arranged In Mandated Section"
9
"Japanese Will Be Between Philippines and U. S. in
Typhoon Season."
It seems reasonable to assume that the Japanese Govern-
ment's decision with regard to the time and place of holding
the Japanese naval maneuvers has been influenced to a degree
by the fact that the United States Soouting Foroe, which has
5
heretofore been stationed in the Atlantic Ocean, is now with
894.00
the Pacific Fleet engaged in maneuvers off Hawaii.
Relations between the United States and Japan are
undoubtedly more tense than is desirable and it seemt thet
concentration of the United States Fleet in the Paciio
one of the faotors in the situation most irritating
the
S
Japanese.
/KN
The query arises whether it would be practicable to
capitalize the present situation in such a way as to remove
what is a groundless feeling on the part of the Japanese
that

=== Page 683 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
that the present concentration of the American Fleet in
the Pacific has any hostile intent towards Japan and at
the same time not compromise this Government's legitimate
right to station its Fleet where it ahooses.
Would it be practicable and beneficial for the
American Government to make a good will gesture toward
Japan in the nature of extending an invitation to the
Japanese Fleet, or the flagships thereof, to pay a courtesy
call to Manila (or possibly to Honolulu)? In extending any
such invitation, it would seem desirable to keep in mind
the following factors:
(a) The situation would have to be arranged
so that the Japanese would not fear that they were
running into a trap. This might be done by stating
that only the flagships of the American Fleet would
be present in Manila (or possibly in Honolulu) to
receive the Japanese Fleet.
advauce
(b) A careful,sounding out by informal means
of the Japanese reaction to any such invitation
would seem desirable. This might be done through
the Japanese Naval Attaché in Washington.
(c) Before anything is done toward approaching
the Japanese it would seem desirable to obtain
Ambassador Grew's comments. on and reaction to any
such move.
(a) The fact that the Japanese Fleet is to be
in waters closer to American possessions than is
usual might afford a reasonable basis for issuing
such an invitation.
(e) Such invitation might be extended by the
President to the Emperor of Japan, or through
regular diplomatic channels, or by the senior
American naval officer to the senior Japanese naval
officer.
(f) It would seem that any such good will offer
would be much more effeotive if issued by the new
Administration. This is of course no criticism of
the present Administration but it is believed in
conformity with the present psychological set-up of
the

=== Page 684 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
the relations between the United States and
Japan.
(g) There needs to be considered the Chinese
reaction to any such Japanese visit of courtesy to
Manila. It is believed that the advantages to
American-Japanese relations from the successful
carrying out of such a move would outweigh any
disadvantage to the United States from Chinese
resentment at such action. In fact, such action
might serve to dispel any false Chinese idea that
the United States contemplates using armed force
to support China in its controversy with Japan.
The action cutlined above might of course result in the
precipitation of unfortunate incidents due to contact of
American and Japanese naval personnel and the whole projeot
is one which would require delicate and careful handling.
Such a plan of action would, however, appear to merit
consideration.
The Navy Department should of course be consulted about
any action to be taken.
ME/RK
m.m.H.
